Appendix C
Formalization of a label-free coordination game and problem

In this section, we formalize label-free coordination (LFC) games and the LFC problem, and we provide auxiliary results required to prove our main theorems.

In Appendix
C.1
, we recall the definition of Dec-POMDPs and introduce some additional notation. In Appendix
C.2
, we recall the definition of isomorphisms and automorphisms, provide more comprehensive examples, and prove first lemmas about these concepts. Afterwards, in Appendix
C.3
, we discuss the pushforward and prove, among other things, that a pushforward policy has the same expected return as the original policy. In Appendix
C.4
, we show that automorphisms define group actions on joint actions, policies, etc. and introduce the concept of an orbit. In Appendix
C.5
, we then introduce Dec-POMDP labels to construct the set of relabeled Dec-POMDPs used to define an LFC game. In Appendix
C.6
, we recall the definition of LFC games and of the LFC problem, and we provide different expressions for the payoff in LFC games. We also prove that LFC games for isomorphic Dec-POMDPs are equivalent, up to a permutation of the principals in the game. In Appendix
C.7
, we prove that any strategy profile that is optimal among those that respects symmetries between principals is a Nash equilibrium in the game, making use of group actions and orbits. This theorem is needed later to prove that all principals using OP with tie-breaking is a Nash equilibrium. Lastly, in Appendix
C.8
, we briefly discuss a condition under which the objective in the LFC problem is equivalent to the formulation without relabeled Dec-POMDPs used in our experiments as outlined in Section
6
.

C.1
Recapitulation of Dec-POMDPs

Before we turn to isomorphisms and automorphisms, we briefly recapitulate Dec-POMDPs and introduce some additional notation that we will use throughout the following. That is, we also introduce a history of length
t
,
\tau_{t}\in\mathcal{H}_{t}
, the set of deterministic policies
\Pi^{0}
and we define the measure space
(\Omega,\mathcal{P}(\Omega),\mathbb{P}_{\pi})
corresponding to a Dec-POMDP in which agents follow the joint policy
\pi
. We also define the notation
x_{-i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},\dotsc x_{N})
and the projection operator
\mathrm{proj}_{i}(x)=x_{i}
. Apart from this, the definitions in this section are a more elaborate version of those in Section
3
of the main text.

To begin, recall the definition of a Dec-POMDP.

Definition 9
.

A (finite-horizon) Dec-POMDP is a tuple

D=\Big(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{A}_{i},P,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{O}=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{O}_{i},O,b_{0},T\Big)

where

•

\mathcal{N}=\{1,\dots,N\}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
is a finite set of agents.

•

\mathcal{S}
is a finite set of states.

•

\mathcal{A}_{i}
is a finite set of actions for player
i\in\mathcal{N}
.

•

P\colon\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{S})

is the transition probability kernel (where
\Delta(\mathcal{S})
denotes the
set of probability mass functions over
\mathcal{S}
).

•

\mathcal{R}\colon\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
the joint reward function.

•

\mathcal{O}_{i}
is a finite set of observations for player
i\in\mathcal{N}
.

•

O\colon\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{O})
is
the observation probability kernel.

•

b_{0}\in\Delta(\mathcal{S})
is a distribution over the initial
state.

•

T\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is the horizon of the problem.

When considering different Dec-POMDPs
D,E
at the same time, we write
\mathcal{A}^{D},\mathcal{A}^{E}
, etc., to indicate to which Dec-POMDP the set belongs. Similarly, we do this for transition, observation, and reward functions. We omit the index
D
if it is clear which Dec-POMDP is meant.

Given a Dec-POMDP
D
, define the set of
local action-observation histories
of length
t\in\{0,\dotsc,T\}
for player

i\in\mathcal{N}
as

\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i,t}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\left(\mathcal{A}_{i}\times\mathcal{O}_{i}\right)^{t},

(where
\left(\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{O}\right)^{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{\emptyset\}
)
and the set of local action-observation histories for player
i
as

\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\bigcup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left(\mathcal{A}_{i}\times\mathcal{O}_{i}\right)^{t}.

Moreover, we define the set of histories of length
t
as

\mathcal{H}_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})\times\left(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{O}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})\right)^{t}.

and
\mathcal{H}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{H}_{T}
as the set of histories. Histories
\tau\in\mathcal{H}
are also called episodes, when one is talking about a particular sample of the stochastic process induced by agents following a policy in the Dec-POMDP, as defined below.

At step
0\leq t\leq T
, agent
i\in\mathcal{N}
chooses a distribution over actions, conditional on a past action-observation history
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i,t}
.
This choice is described by a
local (stochastic) policy
for player
i
, which is a mapping
\pi_{i}\colon\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})
. Since actions can be chosen stochastically, a policy and past observations do not imply which past actions have been taken, so policies are also able to condition on past actions (i.e., agents remember their past actions). We write
\pi_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})
for the probability of action
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
given the action-observation history
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i,t}
. A
(joint stochastic) policy
is a tuple
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{N})

with a local policy for each player.
We denote
\Pi^{D}
as the set of joint stochastic policies for a Dec-POMDP
D
, and

\Pi^{D}_{i}
as the set of local policies for player
i
.
A
local deterministic policy
for player
i\in\mathcal{N}
is defined as a policy
\pi_{i}

such that
\pi_{i}(\cdot\mid\tau_{i,t})
is concentrated on exactly one action (i.e., there exists
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
such that
\pi_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})=1
) for
each
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i,t}
.
A joint deterministic policy is defined analogously to joint stochastic policies, and
(\Pi^{0})^{D}
is the set of joint deterministic policies for
D
.
4
4

4

Normally, a deterministic policy is defined as having values in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, but we can identify each element
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}

with a probability mass function in
\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})
with support
\{a_{i}\}
, so these definitions are interchangeable. Since a deterministic policy outputs only one action for each action-observation history, it there is only one possible action for each observation history
(o_{i,1},\dots,o_{i,t})
, and we could hence write a deterministic policy as a map from observation histories to actions. We omit this here for simplicity.

A Dec-POMDP
D
together with a joint policy
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
specify the stochastic process according to which an episode evolves, yielding a distribution over histories. Formally, we define a discrete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{P}(\Omega),\mathbb{P}_{\pi})
with random variables for states, actions, observations, and rewards at all time steps.

The distributions of these random variables are defined inductively in the following way. First,
S_{0}\sim b_{0}
, that is, the first state is an independent random variable with values in
\mathcal{S}
and image distribution
b_{0}
. Similarly, the first action of agent
i
is distributed according to
A_{i,0}\sim\pi_{i}(\cdot\mid\emptyset)
(note that
\emptyset\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i}
by definition), and the first reward is
R_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{R}(S_{0},A_{0})
, where we define
A_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(A_{i,0})_{i\in\mathcal{N}}
.

Now assume there are random variables for all states, actions, and observations until step
0\leq t\leq T
(in the case
t=0
, there are no observations defined yet). We can summarize them into a random variable for the history of length
t
as

H_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(S_{0},A_{0},(S_{t^{\prime}},(O_{i,t^{\prime}},A_{i,t^{\prime}})_{i\in\mathcal{N}},R_{t^{\prime}})_{1\leq t^{\prime}\leq t})

(and we let
H\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=H_{T}
).
At step
t+1
, a new state
S_{t+1}\sim P(\cdot\mid S_{t},A_{t})
and a new joint observation
O_{t+1}\sim O(\cdot\mid S_{t+1},A_{t})
are sampled. Note that, in a slight abuse of notation, we use
O
for both observation probabilities and observation random variable. We can define a random variable for the action-observation history of agent
i
at time
t+1
as
\overline{A\!O}_{i,t+1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(A_{i,0},O_{i,1},A_{i,1},\dotsc,A_{i,t},O_{i,t+1})
. Conditioning on this action-observation history, agent
i
samples an action
A_{i,t+1}\sim\pi_{i}(\cdot\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t+1})
. This yields a new joint action
A_{t+1}
. Finally, the new reward is

R_{t+1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{R}(S_{t+1},A_{t+1})
, which concludes the definition.

We will sometimes make use of a simplified notation for a tuple excluding a particular player. Let
(x_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{N}}
be a vector indexed by agents, with elements
x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}_{i}
where
\mathcal{X}_{i},i\in\mathcal{N}
are some sets. Then we define
x_{-j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{j-1},x_{j+1},\dotsc,x_{N})
for
j\in\mathcal{N}
. Moreover, for any
x^{\prime}_{i}\in X_{i}
, we then write
(x^{\prime}_{i},x_{-i})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{i-1},x_{i}^{\prime},x_{i+1},\dotsc,x_{N})
. We will also sometimes use the projection operator
\mathrm{proj}_{i}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=x_{i}
to clearly refer to a particular element of
x
when
x
is a more complicated expression.

Now, given a problem
D
and a joint policy
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
, the measure
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}
defined above specifies a distribution over rewards
R_{0},\dotsc,R_{T}
. We can use this fact to define the expected return of the policy. To that end, let
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\pi}}
denote the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}
.

Definition 10

(Self-play objective)
.

Let
D
be a Dec-POMDP. Define the
self-play (SP) objective

J^{D}\colon\Pi^{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for
D
via

J^{D}(\pi)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T}R_{t}\right]

for
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
. Here,
J^{D}(\pi)
is called the expected return of a joint policy
\pi
.

C.2
Dec-POMDP isomorphisms and automorphisms

Here, we recall the definitions of isomorphisms and automorphisms from Sections
4.1
and
4.3
and give more comprehensive examples than in the main text. Then we prove some elementary results.

Let
D,E
be two Dec-POMDPs. Consider a tuple of bijective maps

f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(f_{N},f_{S},(f_{A_{i}})_{i\in\mathcal{N}},(f_{O_{i}})_{i\in\mathcal{N}}),

where

\displaystyle f_{N}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}^{E}

(18)

\displaystyle f_{S}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{E}

(19)

\displaystyle\forall i\in\mathcal{N}\colon\quad

\displaystyle f_{A_{i}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}^{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{E}_{f_{N}(i)}

(20)

\displaystyle\forall i\in\mathcal{N}\colon\quad

\displaystyle f_{O_{i}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{i}^{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{f_{N}(i)}^{E}.

(21)

Given a joint action
a\in\mathcal{A}^{D}
and such tuple
f
, we define

f_{A}(a)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\left(f_{A_{f^{-1}_{N}(j)}}\left(a_{f^{-1}_{N}(j)}\right)\right)_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{E}}\in\mathcal{A}^{E},

(22)

and analogously for
o\in\mathcal{O}^{D}

f_{O}(o)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\left(f_{O_{f^{-1}_{N}(j)}}\left(o_{f^{-1}_{N}(j)}\right)\right)_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{E}}\in\mathcal{O}^{E}.

(23)

That is, in the joint action
f_{A}(a)\in\mathcal{A}^{E}
, the agent
j=f_{N}(i)\in\mathcal{N}^{E}
(where
i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}
) plays the action
f_{A_{i}}(a_{i})\in\mathcal{A}_{j}^{E}
.
Note that it is really
f_{A}(a)\in\mathcal{A}^{E}
, as for any
a\in\mathcal{A}^{D}
and
i\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=f_{N}^{-1}(j)\in\mathcal{N}^{D}
, by definition it is

f_{A_{i}}(a_{i})\in\mathcal{A}^{E}_{f_{N}(i)}=\mathcal{A}^{E}_{f_{N}(f_{N}^{-1}(j))}=\mathcal{A}^{E}_{j}.

The analogous holds for observations.

Definition 11

(Dec-POMDP isomorphism)
.

Let
D,E
be Dec-POMDPs such that both have the same horizon
T^{D}=T^{E}
, and let

f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(f_{N},f_{S},(f_{A_{i}})_{i\in\mathcal{N}},(f_{O_{i}})_{i\in\mathcal{N}})

be a tuple of bijective maps as defined in equations (
18
)–(
21
). Then
f
is an isomorphism from
D
to
E
if
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}^{D}
,
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{D}
and
o\in\mathcal{O}^{D}
, it is

\displaystyle P^{D}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)=

\displaystyle P^{E}(f_{S}(s^{\prime})\mid f_{S}(s),f_{A}(a))

(24)

\displaystyle O^{D}(o\mid s,a)=

\displaystyle O^{E}(f_{O}(o)\mid f_{S}(s),f_{A}(a))

(25)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{D}(s,a)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{E}(f_{S}(s),f_{A}(a))

(26)

\displaystyle b^{D}_{0}(s)=

\displaystyle b_{0}^{E}(f_{S}(s)).

(27)

If such an isomorphism exists,
D
and
E
are called isomorphic. We denote
\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
for the set of isomorphisms from
D
to
E
.

Remark 12
.

In a Dec-POMDP, distributions over histories and policies can be ranked by their associated expected return. If a reward function is multiplied with a positive constant
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and shifted by a constant
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, then the new reward function
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\alpha\mathcal{R}+\beta
still induces the same ranking. For this reason, one could consider such transformations as part of an isomorphism between two problems. For instance,
Harsanyi & Selten 1988
make it part of their definition of an isomorphism in the framework of standard-form games.
For simplicity, we ignore this complication here and consider only isomorphic problems with reward functions that have the same range.

As in the main text, we write
fa
instead of
f_{A}(a)
and
fa_{i}
instead of
f_{A_{i}}a_{i}
, and we do the same for observations, states, etc. We also write
f\tau_{i,t}
for actions of isomorphisms on action-observation histories, which is defined as the element-wise application of
f
, and letting
fr\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=r
for rewards, we define
f\tau
for entire histories
\tau\in\mathcal{H}
.

Now we prove a first basic result about actions of isomorphisms. Note that an isomorphism
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
is a bijective map

f\colon\mathcal{N}^{D}\times\mathcal{S}^{D}\times\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}\mathcal{A}^{D}_{i}\times\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}\mathcal{O}^{D}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}^{E}\times\mathcal{S}^{E}\times\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{E}}\mathcal{A}^{E}_{fi}\times\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{E}}\mathcal{O}^{E}_{fi},

(28)

which can be inverted and composed with other maps. The
following result shows that actions of isomorphisms on joint actions and joint observations are compatible with function composition and with taking inverses of functions. It follows that also the element-wise application to histories and action-observation histories is compatible in this way. In particular, this justifies omitting brackets when applying isomorphisms. We will use this lemma liberally in the following.

Lemma 13
.

Let
D,E,F
be Dec-POMDPs,
a\in\mathcal{A}^{D},o\in\mathcal{O}^{D}
, let
e\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,D)
be the identity, and let
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
,
\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Iso}(E,F)
. Then it is

(i)

ea=a
and
eo=o
.

(ii)

\tilde{f}(fa)=(\tilde{f}\circ f)a
and
\tilde{f}(fo)=(\tilde{f}\circ f)o
.

In particular, actions of isomorphisms
f
on joint actions and joint observations can be inverted using
f^{-1}
.

Proof.

First, it is
ea=(ea_{e^{-1}i})_{i\in\mathcal{N}}=e
,
and analogously for
o
. Second,

\tilde{f}(fa)=\tilde{f}(fa_{f^{-1}i})_{i\in\mathcal{N}}=(\tilde{f}(fa_{f^{-1}\tilde{f}^{-1}i}))_{i\in\mathcal{N}}=(\tilde{f}fa_{(\tilde{f}f)^{-1}i})_{i\in\mathcal{N}}=(\tilde{f}\circ f)a

as function composition is associative and the inverse of
\tilde{f}\circ f
is

f^{-1}\circ\tilde{f}^{-1}
.

Next, it follows from the previous that
f^{-1}(fa)=(f^{-1}\circ f)a=ea=a
. The analogous holds for
o
, which concludes the proof.
∎

The following corollary states that the action of
f
on histories
\tau_{t}\in\mathcal{H}_{t}
is bijective. An analogous corollary also hold for action-observation histories.

Corollary 14
.

Let
D,E
be isomorphic Dec-POMDPs with isomorphism
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
and let
t\in\{0,\dotsc,T\}
. Then
f_{H}\colon\mathcal{H}_{t}^{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{t}^{E},\tau_{t}\mapsto f\tau_{t}
is a bijective map.

Proof.

Let
\tau_{t}=(s_{0},a_{0},s_{1},o_{1},a_{1},r_{1},\dotsc,s_{t},o_{t},a_{t},r_{t})\in\mathcal{H}^{D}_{t}
.
Define
f_{H}
as above and
f_{H}^{-1}\colon\tau\mapsto f^{-1}\tau
. Then

f^{-1}(f(\tau_{t}))=f^{-1}((fs_{0},fa_{0},fs_{1},fo_{1},fa_{1},fr_{1},\dotsc,fs_{t},fo_{t},fa_{t},fr_{t}))\\
=(f^{-1}fs_{0},f^{-1}fa_{0},f^{-1}fs_{1},f^{-1}fo_{1},f^{-1}fa_{1},f^{-1}fr_{1},\dotsc,f^{-1}fr_{t}))\\
\overset{\text{Lemma~\ref{lemma-action-function-composition-compatible}}}{=}(s_{0},a_{0},s_{1},o_{1},a_{1},r_{1},\dotsc,s_{t},o_{t},a_{t},r_{t})=\tau_{t}.

(29)

∎

Recall the definition of an
automorphism
, which can be thought of as describing a symmetry of the Dec-POMDP.

Definition 15

(Dec-POMDP automorphism)
.

An isomorphism
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,D)
from
D
to itself
is called an automorphism. We define
\mathrm{Aut}(D)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\text{$\mathrm{Iso}(D,D)$}
as the set
of all automorphisms of
D
.

Now we give an example of an isomorphism and automorphism, using the lever coordination game.

Example 16

(Lever coordination game)
.

Consider the lever coordination game introduced in Section
1
. This can be formalized as a Dec-POMDP
D
with only one state, one observation for each agent, and in which
T=0
. The agents are
\mathcal{N}=\{1,2\}
and actions are
\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathcal{A}_{2}=\{1,\dotsc,10\}
. One possible reward function is

\mathcal{R}(a_{1},a_{2},s)=\delta_{a_{1},a_{2}}\left(1.0\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{1,\dotsc,9\}}(a_{1})+0.9\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{10\}}(a_{1})\right),

where

\delta_{a_{1},a_{2}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }a_{1}=a_{2}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

is the Kronecker delta.

As an isomorphic problem
E
, consider a problem with the same sets of actions for both players, but where the reward function is defined as

\mathcal{R}^{E}(a_{1},a_{2},s)=\delta_{a_{1},a_{2}}\left(1.0\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{2,\dotsc,10\}}(a_{1})+0.9\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{1\}}(a_{1})\right)

for
a_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
and the trivial state
s
.
Note that this is formally a different Dec-POMDP. Nevertheless, we can define an isomorphism
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
in the following way. For
i=1,2
, define
f_{A_{i}}\colon\mathcal{A}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{i}
such that
f_{A_{i}}(10)=1
and
f_{A_{i}}(1)=10
, and let
f_{A_{1}}=f_{A_{2}}
be arbitrary otherwise. Let the remaining components of
f
be the identity map. Then for any joint action
a
and the trivial state
s
, it is

\mathcal{R}^{E}(fs,fa)=\delta_{fa_{1},fa_{2}}\left(1.0\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{2,\dotsc,10\}}(fa_{1})+0.9\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{1\}}(fa_{1})\right)\\
=\delta_{a_{1},a_{2}}\left(1.0\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{1,\dotsc,9\}}(a_{1})+0.9\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{10\}}(a_{1})\right)=\mathcal{R}^{D}(s,a)

(30)

and hence
D
and
E
are isomorphic (as observation and transition probabilities as well as the initial state distribution are trivial here).

Note that we could have used any two (potentially different) permutations
\hat{f}_{1},\hat{f}_{2}
of the two sets
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}
and defined a new reward function
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(s,a)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{R}^{D}(s,\hat{f}_{1}^{-1}a_{1},\hat{f}_{2}^{-1}a_{2})
. This reward function would then define a new Dec-POMDP
D^{\prime}
, and the isomorphism from
D
to
D^{\prime}
would be exactly
f
defined by
f_{A_{1}}=\hat{f}_{1},f_{A_{2}}=\hat{f}_{2}
and the identity in the other components, as
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(fs,fa)=\mathcal{R}^{D}(s,f_{A_{1}}\hat{f}_{1}^{-1}a_{1},f_{A_{2}}\hat{f}_{2}^{-1}a_{2})=\mathcal{R}^{D}(s,\hat{f}_{1}\hat{f}_{1}^{-1}a_{1},\hat{f}_{2}\hat{f}_{2}^{-1}a_{2})=\mathcal{R}^{D}(s,a)
. We will use this idea in Section
C.5
to define relabeled Dec-POMDPs.

Next, consider the automorphisms of the lever coordination game. Note that the agents in this game are symmetric. For instance, we can define
g
via
g_{N}(1)=2
and
g_{N}(2)=1
, and such that
g_{A_{1}}=g_{A_{2}}=\hat{g}
, where
\hat{g}
is any permutation of
\{1,\dotsc,10\}
such that
\hat{g}(10)=10
. Then one can easily check that
\mathcal{R}^{D}(gs,ga)=\mathcal{R}^{D}(s,a)
for any joint action
a
and the state
s
.

Next, we introduce the automorphisms of the two-stage lever game (Example
3
), which we will need later to prove that OP is suboptimal in the corresponding LFC problem.

Example 17
.

Recall that in the two-stage lever game, there are two agents,
\mathcal{N}=\{1,2\}
, and the problem has two rounds, so
T=1
. Each round, each agent has to pull one of two levers,
\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathcal{A}_{2}=\{1,2\}
. If both agents choose the same lever, they get a reward of
1
. Otherwise, the reward is
-1
. There is again only one state, but there are two observations,
\mathcal{O}_{1}=\mathcal{O}_{2}=\{1,2\}
. In the second stage (
t=1
), each player observes the previous action of the other player, so
O_{i,1}=A_{-i,0}
for
i=1,2
. The reward and observation probabilities are given in Table
4
.

).

Using the table for reward function and observation probabilities, we can easily visualize isomorphisms and automorphisms. Consider any isomorphism
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
where
E
is some other Dec-POMDP. Considering, for instance, the reward function, we know that
\mathcal{R}^{D}(s,a)=\mathcal{R}^{E}(fs,fa)
. This means that if we want to check the value of the reward function of
E
when agents choose action
fa
, we can look it up in the table corresponding to
\mathcal{R}^{D}
in the row for
a
. So we can visualize an isomorphism by applying
f_{A}
to the index (i.e., first) column of this table, but leaving the other cells unchanged. This creates a new table with the reward function for
E
, or, equivalently, this new table corresponds to the reward function
\mathcal{R}^{D}
precomposed with
f^{-1}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{R}^{D}(f^{-1}_{S}\cdot,f^{-1}_{A}\cdot)=\mathcal{R}^{E}
.
Analogously, we can apply
f_{O}
to the header row and
f_{A}
to the index column of the table with observation probabilities, yielding
O^{D}(f^{-1}_{O}\cdot\mid f^{-1}_{S}\cdot,f^{-1}_{A}\cdot)=O^{E}
.
An automorphism is then simply an isomorphism such that applying it to index column and header of the tables does not change the table, other than permuting rows and columns.

Now let
g
be an automorphism.
g_{N}
can either be the identity or it can switch both agents. In either case, it must be
g_{A_{1}}=g_{A_{2}}
, which can also either be the identity or the map that switches the actions. The observation permutation then has to be equal to that of the actions,
g_{O_{1}}=g_{O_{2}}=g_{A_{1}}
. There is trivially only one option for the state permutation.

. Note that apart from a permutation of rows and columns, the tables are identical to the ones in Table
4
, showing that
g
is an automorphism.

We visualize the application of the automorphism that switches agents as well as actions and thus also observations in Table
5
. For instance, using the definition of actions of automorphisms on joint actions in Equations (
22
) and (
23
), we have
f_{A}(1,1)=(f_{A_{f_{N}1}}1,f_{A_{f_{N}2}}1)=(f_{A_{2}}1,f_{A_{1}}1)=(2,2)
, and
f_{A}(1,2)=(f_{A_{2}}2,f_{A_{1}}1)=(1,2)
.

Before we turn to applying isomorphisms and automorphisms to policies, we briefly provide two basic results about isomorphisms and the relationship between isomorphisms and automorphisms.

First, we prove that function composition and inversion preserve isomorphisms.

Lemma 18
.

Let
D,E,F
be Dec-POMDPs and let
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
and
\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Iso}(E,F)
. Then
f^{-1}\in\mathrm{Iso}(E,D)
and
\tilde{f}\circ f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,F)
.

Proof.

Let
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{D}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}^{D}
. First, using the definition of an isomorphism and associativity of function composition, it is

P^{D}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)=P^{E}(fs^{\prime}\mid fs,fa)=P^{F}(\tilde{f}fs^{\prime}\mid\tilde{f}fs,\tilde{f}fa)=P^{F}((\tilde{f}\circ f)s^{\prime}\mid(\tilde{f}\circ f)s,(\tilde{f}\circ f)a).

An analogous calculation applies to observation probabilities, reward functions and initial state distribution. This shows
that
\tilde{f}\circ f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,F)
.

Next, let
s^{\prime},s\in\mathcal{S}^{E}
. Using Lemma
13
and the definition of an isomorphism, it is

P^{E}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)=P^{E}(ff^{-1}s^{\prime}\mid ff^{-1}s,ff^{-1}a)=P^{D}(f^{-1}s^{\prime}\mid f^{-1}s,f^{-1}a).

Again, an analogous calculation applies to observation probabilities and the other relevant functions. This shows
that
f^{-1}\in\mathrm{Iso}(E,D)
.
∎

The next lemma shows that one can decompose isomorphisms into any isomorphism composed with an automorphism. Essentially, an isomorphism is a map from one Dec-POMDP to another, composed with a symmetry of that Dec-POMDP. This will be important later when we apply these concepts to define the LFC problem and show how OP relates to it.

Lemma 19
.

Let
D,E
be Dec-POMDPs and
f,\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
. Then there exists exactly one
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(E)
such that
g\circ f=\tilde{f}
. Analogously, there exists exactly one
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
such that
f\circ g=\tilde{f}
. In particular, it is

\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)=f\circ\mathrm{Aut}(E)=\mathrm{Aut}(D)\circ f,

where
f\circ\mathrm{Aut}(E)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{f\circ g\mid g\in\mathrm{Aut}(E)\}
and
\mathrm{Aut}(D)\circ f
is defined analogously.

Proof.

Existence: By Lemma
18
,
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\tilde{f}\circ f^{-1}
is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{Iso}(E,E)=\mathrm{Aut}(E)
, and it is
g\circ f=(\tilde{f}\circ f^{-1})\circ f=\tilde{f}
.

Uniqueness: Assume
g\circ f=\tilde{g}\circ f=\tilde{f}
for automorphisms
g,\tilde{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(E)
. Then it follows that

g=g\circ(f\circ f^{-1})=(g\circ f)\circ f^{-1}=(\tilde{g}\circ f)\circ f^{-1}=\tilde{g}.

The proof for the second part of the lemma is exactly analogous, but using
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=f^{-1}\tilde{f}
.

Turning to the “in particular” statement, the above shows that

\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)\subseteq f\circ\mathrm{Aut}(E)

and

\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(D)\circ f.

The two inclusions in the other direction follow directly from Lemma
18
.
∎

C.3
Pushforward policies

Recall the definition of pushforward policies.

Definition 20

(Pushforward policy)
.

Let
D,E
be isomorphic Dec-POMDPs, let
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
, and let
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
.
Then we define the pushforward
f^{*}\pi\in\Pi^{E}
of
\pi
by
f
via

(f^{*}\pi)_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\pi_{f^{-1}i}(f^{-1}a_{i}\mid f^{-1}\tau_{i,t})

for all
i\in\mathcal{N}^{E},a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}^{E},t\in\{0,\dotsc,T\},
and
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i,t}^{E}
. That is, in the joint policy
f^{*}\pi
, agent
j\in\mathcal{N}^{E}
gets assigned the local policy
\pi_{i}
of agent
i\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=f^{-1}j\in\mathcal{N}^{D}
, precomposed with
f^{-1}
.

One can easily see that
f^{*}\pi
is a policy for the Dec-POMDP
E
.
Hence, when
f
is an automorphism,
f^{*}\pi
is a policy for the same Dec-POMDP as
\pi
.

Like actions of isomorphisms on joint actions and observations, the pushforward is compatible with function composition, and it can be inverted using the inverse map
f^{-1}
.

Lemma 21
.

Let
D,E
and
F
be Dec-POMDPs. Let
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
,
e\in\mathrm{Aut}(D,D)
be the identity and
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
,
\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Iso}(E,F)
. Then it is

(i)

e^{*}\pi=\pi
.

(ii)

\tilde{f}^{*}(f^{*}\pi)=(\tilde{f}\circ f)^{*}\pi
.

In particular, the pushforward can be inverted using the inverse map
f^{-1}
.

Proof.

First, let
i\in\mathcal{N}^{D},a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}^{D},t\in\{0,\dotsc,T\}
, and
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i,t}^{D}
. Then

(e^{*}\pi)_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})=\pi_{ei}(ea_{i}\mid e\tau_{i,t})=\pi_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t}),

which shows that
e^{*}\pi=\pi
.

Second, let
i\in\mathcal{N}^{F},a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}^{F}_{i}
and
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}^{F}_{i,t}
.
Using Lemma
13
, it is

(\tilde{f}^{*}(f^{*}\pi))_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})=(f^{*}\pi)_{\tilde{f}^{-1}i}(\tilde{f}^{-1}a_{i}\mid\tilde{f}^{-1}\tau_{i,t})=\pi_{f^{-1}(\tilde{f}^{-1}i)}(f^{-1}(\tilde{f}^{-1}a_{i})\mid f^{-1}(\tilde{f}^{-1}\tau_{i,t}))\\
\overset{\text{Lemma~\ref{lemma-action-function-composition-compatible}}}{=}\pi_{(f\circ\tilde{f})^{-1}i}((\tilde{f}\circ f)^{-1}a_{i}\mid(\tilde{f}\circ f)^{-1}\tau_{i,t})=((\tilde{f}\circ f)^{*}\pi)_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t}),

(31)

which proves that
\tilde{f}^{*}(f^{*}\pi)=(\tilde{f}\circ f)^{*}\pi
.

Regarding the invertibility of the pushforward, note that by (ii), it is

(f^{-1})^{*}(f^{*}\pi)\overset{\text{(ii)}}{=}(f^{-1}\circ f)^{*}\pi=\pi.

This concludes the proof.
∎

One may wonder how pushforward policies and isomorphisms are related. In particular, how is the distribution over histories induced by a pushforward policy related to the distribution induced by the original policy? This question is answered by the following theorem, which we will use throughout this paper. The theorem demonstrates how the isomorphism from
D
to
E
preserves the structure of the problem. It is similar to
Kang & Kim 2012
’s Theorem 3, which applies to automorphisms in POSGs and makes a statement about the expected returns of all agents under the pushforward policy. We will provide a result about expected returns as a corollary.

Theorem 22
.

Let
D,E
be isomorphic Dec-POMDPs, let
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
, and let
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
. Then for any
\tau\in\mathcal{H}^{D}
, it is

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(H^{D}=\tau)=\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(H^{E}=f\tau).

In particular, for any
i\in\mathcal{N},t\in\{0,\dotsc,T\}
and
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i}
, it is

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})=\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{fi,t}=f\tau_{i,t}).

Proof.

Proof by induction over
t\in\{0,\dotsc,T\}
.

To start the induction, let
s\in\mathcal{S}^{D},a\in\mathcal{A},r\in\mathbb{R}
and
\tau_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(s,a,r)\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{D}
. Then it is

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(H_{0}^{D}=\tau_{0})

\displaystyle=b_{0}^{D}(s)\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}\pi_{i}(a_{i}\mid\emptyset)\delta_{\mathcal{R}^{D}(s,a),r}

(32)

\displaystyle=b_{0}^{E}(fs)\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}\pi_{f^{-1}fi}(f^{-1}fa_{i}\mid\emptyset)\delta_{\mathcal{R}^{E}(fs,fa),r}

(33)

\displaystyle=b_{0}^{E}(fs)\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}(f^{*}\pi)_{fi}(fa_{i}\mid\emptyset)\delta_{\mathcal{R}^{E}(fs,fa),r}

(34)

\displaystyle=b_{0}^{E}(fs)\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{E}}(f^{*}\pi)_{i}(fa_{f^{-1}i}\mid\emptyset)\delta_{\mathcal{R}^{E}(fs,fa),r}

(35)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(H_{0}^{E}=f\tau).

(36)

Here, in (
33
), we use the definition of an isomorphism, in (
34
) we use the definition of the pushforward policy, and in (
36
) we use the definition of
fa
from Equation (
22
).

Next, let
t>0
and assume that it is
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\tau^{D}_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})=\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(\tau^{E}=f\tau_{t-1})
for any
\tau_{t-1}\in\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
. Let

\tau_{t}=(\dotsc,s_{t-1},o_{t-1},a_{t-1},r_{t-1},s_{t},o_{t},a_{t},r_{t})\in\mathcal{H}_{t}^{D}

arbitrary, define
a_{i,t}
for
i\in\mathcal{N}
such that
(a_{i,t})_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}=a_{t}
, and define
\tau_{i,t}
as the action-observation history for player
i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}
corresponding to
\tau_{t}
.

If
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(H^{D}_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})=0
, then also
\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(H_{t-1}^{E}=f\tau_{t-1})=0
, and thus also

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(H^{D}_{t}=\tau_{t})=0=\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(H_{t}^{E}=f\tau_{t}).

Thus, assume now that
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(H^{D}_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})>0
.
Then it is

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(S_{t}^{D}=s_{t},O^{D}_{t}=o_{t},A^{D}_{t}=a_{T},R^{D}_{t}=r_{t}\mid H^{D}_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})

(37)

\displaystyle=P^{D}(s_{t}\mid s_{t-1},a_{t-1})O^{D}(o_{t}\mid s_{t},a_{t-1})\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}\pi_{i}(a_{i,t}\mid\tau_{t})\delta_{\mathcal{R}^{D}(s_{t},a_{t}),r}

(38)

\displaystyle=P^{E}(fs_{t}\mid fs_{t-1},fa_{t-1})O^{E}(fo_{t}\mid fs_{t},fa_{t-1})

(39)

\displaystyle\quad\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}\pi_{f^{-1}fi}(f^{-1}fa_{i,t}\mid f^{-1}f\tau_{t})\delta_{\mathcal{R}^{E}(fs_{t},fa_{t}),r}

(40)

\displaystyle=P^{E}(fs_{t}\mid fs_{t-1},fa_{t-1})O^{E}(fo_{t}\mid fs_{t},fa_{t-1})

(41)

\displaystyle\quad\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}(f^{*}\pi)_{fi}(fa_{i,t}\mid f\tau_{t})\delta_{\mathcal{R}^{E}(fs_{t},fa_{t}),r}

(42)

\displaystyle=P^{E}(fs_{t}\mid fs_{t-1},fa_{t-1})O^{E}(fo_{t}\mid fs_{t},fa_{t-1})

(43)

\displaystyle\quad\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{E}}(f^{*}\pi)_{i}(fa_{f^{-1}i,t}\mid f\tau_{f^{-1}i,t})\delta_{\mathcal{R}^{E}(fs_{t},fa_{t}),r}

(44)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(S_{t}^{E}=fs_{t},O^{E}_{t}=fo_{t},A^{E}_{t}=fa_{t},R^{E}_{t}=r_{t}\mid H^{E}_{t-1}=f\tau_{t-1}).

(45)

Again, we have used the definitions of isomorphism, pushforward policy, and Equations (
22
) and (
23
) in lines (
39
), (
41
), and (
45
), respectively.
Using the inductive hypothesis, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(H^{D}_{t}=\tau_{t})

(46)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(S_{t}^{D}=s_{t},O^{D}_{t}=o_{t},A^{D}_{t}=a_{T},R^{D}_{t}=r_{t}\mid H^{D}_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(H^{D}_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})

(47)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(S_{t}^{E}=fs_{t},O^{E}_{t}=fo_{t},A^{E}_{t}=fa_{t},R^{E}_{t}=r_{t}\mid H^{E}_{t-1}=f\tau_{t-1})\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(H^{D}_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})

(48)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{I.H.}}{=}\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(S_{t}^{E}=fs_{t},O^{E}_{t}=fo_{t},A^{E}_{t}=fa_{t},R^{E}_{t}=r_{t}\mid H^{E}_{t-1}=f\tau_{t-1})\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(H^{E}_{t-1}=f\tau_{t-1})

(49)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(H^{E}_{t}=f\tau_{t}).

(50)

This concludes the induction and thus proves that
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(H^{D}=\tau)=\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(H^{E}=f\tau)
for any history
\tau\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\tau_{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{T}^{D}
.

Turning to the “in particular” part of the proposition, let
i\in\mathcal{N},t\in\{0,\dotsc,T\}
, and
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i,t}
. Let
\tau_{t}\in\mathcal{H}_{t}^{D}
such that
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(H^{D}_{t}=\tau_{t})>0
and thus also
\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(H^{E}_{t}=f\tau_{t})>0
.

First, assume that actions and observations of agent
i
in this history equal those in
\tau_{i,t}
. Then it is
\{H^{D}_{t}=\tau_{t}\}\subseteq\{\overline{A\!O}^{D}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\}
and hence
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\mid H_{t}=\tau_{t})=1
. Moreover, by Equations (
22
) and (
23
), it follows for any
t^{\prime}\leq t,a_{t^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{A}^{D}
and
o_{t^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{O}^{D}
that

\mathrm{proj}_{fi}(fa_{t^{\prime}})=fa_{i,t^{\prime}}
and analogously
\mathrm{proj}_{fi}(fo_{t^{\prime}})=fo_{i,t^{\prime}}
. Hence, it follows that actions of observations of agent
fi
in
f\tau_{t}
are equal to those in
f\tau_{i,t}
, and thus it is
\{H^{E}_{t}=f\tau_{t}\}\subseteq\{\overline{A\!O}^{E}_{fi,t}=f\tau_{i,t}\}
, which implies
\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(\overline{A\!O}^{E}_{fi,t}=f\tau_{i,t}\mid H_{t}=f\tau_{t})=1
.

If, on the other hand, a history
\tau_{t}
disagrees with
\tau_{i,t}
in any way, then trivially
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\mid H_{t}=\tau_{t})=0
and thus by same argument as before also
\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{fi,t}=f\tau_{i,t}\mid H_{t}=f\tau_{t})=0
.

It follows that for any
\tau_{t}\in\mathcal{H}^{D}_{t}
such that
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(H^{D}_{t}=\tau)>0
, it is

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\mid H_{t}=\tau_{t})=\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(\overline{A\!O}^{E}_{fi,t}=f\tau_{i,t}\mid H_{t}=f\tau_{t}),

(51)

and hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau_{t}\in\mathcal{H}^{D}_{t}}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\mid H_{t}=\tau_{t})\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(H^{D}_{t}=\tau_{t})

(52)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:21})}{=}\sum_{\tau_{t}\in\mathcal{H}^{D}_{t}}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\mid H_{t}=\tau_{t})\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(H^{D}_{t}=f\tau_{t})

(53)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:22})}{=}\sum_{\tau_{t}\in\mathcal{H}^{D}_{t}}\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{fi,t}=f\tau_{i,t}\mid H_{t}=f\tau_{t})\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(H^{D}_{t}=f\tau_{t})

(54)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau_{t}\in f^{-1}\left(\mathcal{H}^{D}_{t}\right)}\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{fi,t}=f\tau_{i,t}\mid H_{t}=\tau_{t})\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(H^{D}_{t}=\tau_{t})

(55)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{fi,t}=f\tau_{i,t}).

(56)

In line (
56
), we have used that, by Corollary
14
,
f
is a bijective map when applied to histories, and thus
f^{-1}\left(\mathcal{H}^{D}_{t}\right)=\mathcal{H}^{E}_{t}
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

It is an immediate corollary that the expected return of a policy is not changed by the pushforward.

Corollary 23
.

Let
D,E
be Dec-POMDPs, let
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
, and let
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
. Then

J^{D}(\pi)=J^{E}(f^{*}\pi).

Proof.

By
Corollary
14
and Theorem
22
, it is

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(f\left(H^{D}\right)=\tau^{E})\overset{\text{Corollary~\ref{corollary-action-isomorphism-is-bijective-histories}}}{=}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(H^{D}=f^{-1}\tau^{E})\\
\overset{\text{Theorem~\ref{lem-pull-back-isomorphism-compatibility}}}{=}\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(H^{E}=f(f^{-1}\tau^{E}))=\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(H^{E}=\tau^{E})

(57)

for any history
\tau^{E}\in\mathcal{H}^{E}
.
This shows that the random variable
f\left(H^{D}\right)
has the same image distribution under
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}
as the variable
H^{E}
under
\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}
. In particular, this means that for any
t=0,\dotsc,T
, the variables
f(R_{t}^{D})=R^{D}_{t}
and
R^{E}_{t}
have the same distribution in the respective probability spaces (*).
Using the definition of the expected return, it follows that

J^{D}(\pi)=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T}R^{D}_{t}\right]\overset{\text{(*)}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{f^{*}\pi}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T}R^{E}_{t}\right]=J^{E}(f^{*}\pi).

(58)

∎

C.4
Relation to group theory

The study of symmetries is a focus of group theory, and the concepts introduced above hence correspond to group-theoretic notions. For instance, as we show below,
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
is a group, and its elements do act on the elements of a Dec-POMDP in the sense of group actions. We discuss this here as we will need these results later, in the discussion of symmetric profiles of learning algorithms in Appendix
C.7
, as well as in the discussion of random tie-breaking functions in Appendix
F.3
. For a reference on the group-theoretic concepts discussed here, see
Rotman 2012
.

We begin by showing that
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
is a group.

Proposition 24
.

Let
D
be a Dec-POMDP. Then

(\mathrm{Aut}(D),\circ)
is a group, where
\circ
is the function composition.

Proof.

First, we show that the binary operation

\circ\colon\mathrm{Aut}(D)\times\mathrm{Aut}(D)\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(D),(g,\tilde{g})\mapsto g\circ\tilde{g}

is well-defined.
By Equation
28
, an automorphism
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
is a bijective self-map, so we can compose any two automorphisms
g,\tilde{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
. Moreover, by Lemma
18
, for any
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D),\tilde{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
, we also have
\tilde{g}\circ g\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,D)=\mathrm{Aut}(D)
. This shows that
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
is closed under function composition.

Second, note that
\circ
is an associative operation as function composition is associative. Moreover, for the identity map
e
, it is
e\circ g=g
for any
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
, so
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
has a neutral element. Lastly, by Lemma
18
, it is also
g^{-1}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
, and since
g^{-1}\circ g=e
, this implies that
g
has an inverse in
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Next, we turn to group actions, which formalize the idea that elements of groups can be applied to sets. In the case of symmetry groups, this connects the abstract group elements with their role as transformation of an underlying set. For instance, consider the set
X
of the vertices of an equilateral triangle in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the cyclic group
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{3\mathbb{Z}}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{3\mathbb{Z}}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{3\mathbb{Z}}$}}{\raisebox{1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{3\mathbb{Z}}$}}}
. Each element of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{3\mathbb{Z}}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{3\mathbb{Z}}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{3\mathbb{Z}}$}}{\raisebox{1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{3\mathbb{Z}}$}}}
can be regarded as a rotation of the vertices of the triangle, mapping one vertex to another.

Definition 25

(Group action)
.

Let
(G,\cdot)
be a group with identity
e
and let
X
be any set.
A group action is defined as a map
\alpha\colon G\times X\rightarrow X
such that

(i)

Identity:
\alpha(e,x)=x
for any
x\in X

(ii)

Compatibility:
\alpha(f,\alpha(g,x))=\alpha(f\cdot g,x)
for any
f,g\in G
,
x\in X
.

It is common to write
gx\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\alpha(g,x)
for
g\in G
,
x\in X
, if it is clear which group action is referred to.

We have already proven these two properties for isomorphisms and both their actions on joint actions and observations, as well as the pushforward of policies, in Lemma
13
and Lemma
21
, respectively. Hence, it follows that also
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
acts on these sets in the sense of group actions.

Corollary 26
.

Let
D
be a Dec-POMDP.
The actions of
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
on
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{O}
, defined as
\alpha_{A}\colon(g,a)\mapsto g_{A}(a)
respectively
\alpha_{O}\colon(g,o)\mapsto g_{O}(o)
as in Equations
22
and
23
are group actions. Similarly, the pushforward of policies by automorphisms
(g,\pi)\mapsto g^{*}\pi
as defined in Definition
20
is an action of
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
on
\Pi^{D}
.

Proof.

This follows directly from Lemma
13
and Lemma
21
.
∎

Of course, automorphisms also act on states and agents, and one can also easily see that they act on histories and action-observation histories.

Some further results immediately follow from this, such as the fact that
\mathcal{N}
decomposes into equivalence classes of
orbits
under
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
. The orbit of agent
i
is defined as the set of all agents
j
that can be obtained from
i
by applying automorphisms.

Definition 27

(Orbit)
.

Let
D
be a Dec-POMDP and assume that
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
acts on the set
X
. Then for
x\in X
, the set

\mathrm{Aut}(D)x\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{gx\mid g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)\}

is called the orbit of
x
under
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
.

For instance, for
i\in\mathcal{N}
, the set

\mathrm{Aut}(D)i\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{gi\mid g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)\}

is the orbit of agent
i
under
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
. It is a standard result from group theory that orbits form a partition
\{\mathrm{Aut}(D)i\mid i\in\mathcal{N}\}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{N})
of the set. This follows from the fact that since group actions have an identity and are invertible, belonging to the same orbit is an equivalence relation. For example, in the case of the triangle in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, all the vertices in
V
can be reached from any other vertex by rotations, so they all belong to the same orbit. In general, though, the orbits may form any other partition of the set.

C.5
Dec-POMDP labelings and relabeled Dec-POMDPs

In the following, assume that a Dec-POMDP
D
is given. Here, we want to define a set of isomorphic Dec-POMDPs
{\mathcal{D}}
as described in Section
4.2
, in which the sets of states, actions, etc. are of the form
\{1,2,\dotsc,k-1,k\}\subseteq\mathbb{N},k\in\mathbb{N}
. This set can then be used to define the LFC game for
D
in a way that does not depend on labels.

We begin by defining a
labeling
of
D
. A labeling
f
is a special Dec-POMDP isomorphism from
D
to another, relabeled Dec-POMDP, that can be constructed using
f
.

Definition 28

(Dec-POMDP labeling)
.

A Dec-POMDP labeling is a tuple of bijective maps

f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(f_{N},f_{S},(f_{A_{i}})_{i\in\mathcal{N}},(f_{O_{i}})_{i\in\mathcal{N}}),

where

\displaystyle f_{N}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\{1,\dotsc,|\mathcal{N}|\}

(59)

\displaystyle f_{S}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\{1,\dotsc,|\mathcal{S}|\}

(60)

\displaystyle\forall i\in\mathcal{N}\colon\quad

\displaystyle f_{A_{i}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}\rightarrow\{1,\dotsc,|\mathcal{A}_{i}|\}

(61)

\displaystyle\forall i\in\mathcal{N}\colon\quad

\displaystyle f_{O_{i}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{i}\rightarrow\{1,\dotsc,|\mathcal{O}_{i}|\}.

(62)

We denote
\mathrm{Sym}(D)
for the set of labelings of
D
.

Note that if
X
is some set, then
\mathrm{Sym}(X)
usually denotes the
symmetric group
of
X
. The symmetric group is the set of permutations of
X
, together with the operation of function composition. We use the same notation, as
\mathrm{Sym}(D)
can be understood of as containing all the permutations of the different sets that
D
consists of, with the caveat that we first map those sets to subsets of the first
k
natural numbers. This is done for simplification, especially regarding the treatment of the individual action and observation sets of different agents.

Next, we introduce the pushforward Dec-POMDP
f^{*}D
for a labeling
f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
. This is a Dec-POMDP that is isomorphic to
D
, with isomorphism
f
. In the following, we let
f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
act on joint actions, observations, etc., in the same way as before for isomorphisms. For instance, for
a\in\mathcal{A}
, it is
fa\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(f_{A_{f_{N}^{-1}(i)}}(a_{f_{N}^{-1}(i)}))_{i\in\{1,\dotsc,|\mathcal{N}|\}}
. The compatibility of these actions with function composition and function inversion trivially still hold.

Definition 29

(Relabeled Dec-POMDP)
.

Let
f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\{1,\dotsc,N\}=\mathcal{N}
. The pushforward of
D
by
f
, called a relabeled Dec-POMDP, is the Dec-POMDP

f^{*}D\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\hat{\mathcal{N}},\hat{\mathcal{S}},\hat{\mathcal{A}},\hat{P},\hat{\mathcal{R}},\hat{\mathcal{O}},\hat{O},\hat{b}_{0},\hat{T}),

where

•

\hat{\mathcal{N}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{1,\dotsc,N\}=\mathcal{N}
.

•

\hat{\mathcal{S}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{1,\dotsc,|\mathcal{S}|\}
.

•

\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{1,\dotsc,|\mathcal{A}_{f^{-1}i}|\}
for
i\in\hat{\mathcal{N}}
.

•

\hat{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=P(f^{-1}s^{\prime}\mid f^{-1}s,f^{-1}a)
for
s^{\prime},s\in\hat{\mathcal{S}},a\in\hat{\mathcal{A}}
.

•

\hat{\mathcal{R}}(s,a)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{R}(f^{-1}s,f^{-1}a)
for
s\in\hat{\mathcal{S}},a\in\hat{\mathcal{A}}
.

•

\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{1,\dotsc,|\mathcal{O}_{f^{-1}i}|\}
for
i\in\hat{\mathcal{N}}
.

•

\hat{O}(o\mid s,a)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=O(f^{-1}o\mid f^{-1}s,f^{-1}a)
for
o\in\hat{\mathcal{O}},s\in\hat{\mathcal{S}},a\in\hat{\mathcal{A}}
.

•

\hat{b}_{0}(s)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=b_{0}(f^{-1}s)
for
s\in\hat{\mathcal{S}}
.

•

\hat{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=T
.

First, we have to check that this is well-defined, e.g., that
f^{-1}a\in\mathcal{A}
for any
a\in\hat{\mathcal{A}}
. For states, it is clear from the definition of
\mathrm{Sym}(D)
that
f^{-1}(\hat{\mathcal{S}})=f^{-1}(\{1,\dotsc,|\mathcal{S}|\})=\mathcal{S}
. Moreover, the same applies to agents, i.e.,
f^{-1}i\in\mathcal{N}
for any
i\in\{1,\dotsc,|\mathcal{N}|\}
. This leaves joint actions and observations.

Proposition 30
.

For
\hat{\mathcal{A}},\hat{\mathcal{O}}
as defined above, it is
f^{-1}(\hat{\mathcal{A}})=\mathcal{A}

and
f^{-1}(\hat{\mathcal{O}})=\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

Let
\hat{a}\in\hat{\mathcal{A}}
. Then for any
i\in\hat{\mathcal{N}}
, we can define
j\in\mathcal{N}
and
a_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
such that
fj=i
and
fa_{j}=\hat{a}_{i}
.
Then

f^{-1}\hat{a}=(f^{-1}\hat{a}_{fj}))_{j\in\mathcal{N}}=(f^{-1}fa_{j}))_{j\in\mathcal{N}}=(a_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}}=a\in\mathcal{A}.

The same argument works for
\hat{o}\in\hat{\mathcal{O}}
.
∎

Importantly, it can be
f^{*}D\neq D
for a labeling
f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
.
Nevertheless, it is easy to see from the definitions that
f^{*}D
is actually isomorphic to
D
, with isomorphism
f
. This also implies that it is
f^{*}D=D
if and only if
f
is an automorphism.

Lemma 31
.

For any
f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
, it is
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,f^{*}D)
.

Proof.

This follows directly from the definition of an isomorphism, together with Lemma
13
. For instance, considering transition probabilities, it is

P(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)=P(f^{-1}fs^{\prime}\mid f^{-1}fs,f^{-1}fa)=\hat{P}(fs^{\prime}\mid fs\mid fa)

for any
s^{\prime},s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}
. Similar calculations apply to all the other relevant functions.
∎

It follows as a corollary that
f^{*}\pi
is a policy for the Dec-POMDP
f^{*}D
, where
\pi\in\Pi^{D},f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
. Note also that the results in Lemma
21
still apply to the pushforward by labelings.

Corollary 32
.

Let
f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
and
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
. Then
f^{*}\pi\in\Pi^{f^{*}D}
.

Proof.

Follows from the definition of
f^{*}\pi
and Lemma
31
.
∎

Lastly, we provide some further useful results about labelings. First, the set
\mathrm{Sym}(D)
already contains all the isomorphisms in
\mathrm{Iso}(D,f^{*}D)
.
We will need this result later to relate results about isomorphisms and automorphisms to the relabeled Dec-POMDPs used in an LFC game.

Lemma 33
.

Let
f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
. Then

\mathrm{Iso}(D,f^{*}D)=\{\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)\mid\tilde{f}^{*}D=f^{*}D\}

Proof.

“
\supseteq
”: for any
\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
such that
\tilde{f}^{*}D=f^{*}D
, it is also
\mathrm{Iso}(D,\tilde{f}^{*}D)=\mathrm{Iso}(D,f^{*}D)
, and thus it follows from Lemma
31
that
\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,\tilde{f}^{*}D)=\mathrm{Iso}(D,f^{*}D)
.

“
\subseteq
”: Let
\hat{f}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,f^{*}D)
. Note that the set of agents, states, and the individual action and observation sets in
f^{*}D
are all of the form
\{1,\dotsc,k\}
where
k\in\mathbb{N}
depends on the respective set. Now consider, for instance, the map
\hat{f}_{A_{i}}
for
i\in\mathcal{N}
. Then by the definition of an isomorphism,
\hat{f}_{A_{i}}
is a bijective map, and its domain and codomain are
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\{1,\dotsc,k\}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, since
\hat{f}_{A_{i}}
is bijective, it must be
k=|\mathcal{A}_{i}|
.
Mutatis mutandis
, the same applies to all of the other maps that are part of the tuple
\hat{f}
. Hence,
\hat{f}
satisfies the definition of a Dec-POMDP labeling, so
\hat{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
.

Next, it follows that
\hat{f}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,\hat{f}^{*}D)
by Lemma
31
, and thus
e=\hat{f}\circ\hat{f}^{-1}\in\mathrm{Iso}(f^{*}D,\hat{f}^{*}D)
by the assumption and Lemma
18
. Hence, using the definition of an isomorphism, it follows that also
f^{*}D=\hat{f}^{*}D
. This shows that

\hat{f}\in\{\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)\mid\tilde{f}^{*}D=f^{*}D\},

which concludes the proof.
∎

Second, we show that labelings and pushforward are compatible with composition with isomorphisms.

Lemma 34
.

Let
D,E
be isomorphic Dec-POMDPs with
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
. Then

\mathrm{Sym}(D)=\mathrm{Sym}(E)\circ f.

Moreover, it is
\tilde{f}^{*}E=(\tilde{f}\circ f)^{*}D
for any
\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(E)
.

Proof.

First, let
\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
and define
\hat{f}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\tilde{f}\circ f^{-1}
. Note that
\hat{f}
has as components bijective maps with a domain and codomain that satisfies the definition of a labeling of
E
. Hence,
\hat{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(E)
. Next, let
\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(E)
. Then similarly,
\tilde{f}\circ f
fulfills the requirements for a labeling in
\mathrm{Sym}(D)
.

To prove the second statement, let again
\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(E)
. Note that since
D
and
E
are isomorphic, they must have the same set of players and sets of states with the same cardinalities. Now let
i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}
. Using the definition of an isomorphism and of a labeling, it is then

\mathcal{A}^{\tilde{f}^{*}E}_{i}=\{1,\dotsc,|\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{f}^{-1}i}^{E}|\}=\{1,\dotsc,|\mathcal{A}_{f^{-1}(\tilde{f}^{-1}i)}^{D}|\}=\{1,\dotsc,|\mathcal{A}_{(\tilde{f}\circ f)^{-1}i}^{D}|\}=\mathcal{A}^{(\tilde{f}\circ f)^{*}D}_{i}.

A similar argument applies to the sets
\mathcal{O}_{i}
for
i\in\mathcal{N}
. Finally, let
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\tilde{f}^{*}E},a\in\mathcal{A}^{\tilde{f}^{*}E}
. Using again the definition of an isomorphism and a labeling, it follows that

P^{\tilde{f}^{*}E}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)=P^{E}(\tilde{f}^{-1}s^{\prime}\mid\tilde{f}^{-1}s,\tilde{f}^{-1}a)\\
=P^{D}(f^{-1}\tilde{f}^{-1}s^{\prime}\mid f^{-1}\tilde{f}^{-1}s,f^{-1}\tilde{f}^{-1}a)=P^{(\tilde{f}\circ f)^{*}D}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a).

(63)

Again, an analogous argument applies to the observation probability kernel and reward function, as well as the initial state distribution.
This concludes the proof.
∎

C.6
The label-free coordination game and problem

Here, we recall the definitions of an LFC game and of the LFC problem. To begin, we define a measure space of policies and recall the definition of a learning algorithm. For any Dec-POMDP
D
, let
\Delta(\Pi^{D})
be the set of measures on the space
(\Pi^{D},\mathcal{F}^{D})
where
\mathcal{F}^{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\otimes_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{F}^{D}_{i}
is a product
\sigma
-Algebra and
\mathcal{F}_{i}^{D}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\Pi_{i}^{D})
are
\sigma
-Algebras that make the random variables and sets discussed in this paper measurable. For instance, for
i\in\mathcal{N}
, this could be the Borel
\sigma
-Algebra with respect to the standard topology on
\Pi_{i}^{D}
that comes from regarding
\Pi_{i}^{D}
as a subset of
[0,1]^{\mathcal{A}^{D}_{i}\times\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}^{D}_{i}}
. Although we do not investigate this here, all the relevant functions and sets should be measurable in that sense.

Definition 35

(Learning algorithm)
.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be a finite set of Dec-POMDPs. A learning algorithm for
{\mathcal{D}}
is a map

\sigma\colon{\mathcal{D}}\rightarrow\bigcup_{D\in\mathcal{D}}\Delta(\Pi^{D})

such that
\sigma(D)\in\Delta(\Pi^{D})
for all
D\in\mathcal{D}
. We write
\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
for the set of learning algorithms for
\mathcal{D}
.

Note that this definition is general enough so as to include planning algorithms that construct a policy directly from the environment dynamics, instead of incrementally updating a policy from experience. Nevertheless, here, we imagine that
\sigma(D)
is a policy that was trained by an RL algorithm, using a simulator of
D
. Note also that a learning algorithm can learn different joint policies in different training runs, which we formalize as outputting a measure over joint policies.

Similarly to the case of policies, for a distribution
\nu\in\Delta(\Pi^{D})
and an isomorphism
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
, we can define a pushforward distribution
f^{*}\nu\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\nu\circ(f^{*})^{-1}\in\Delta(\Pi^{E})
, which is the image measure of
\nu
under
f^{*}
. It is apparent that for two isomorphisms
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E),\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Iso}(E,F)
, it is
\tilde{f}^{*}(f^{*}\nu)=(\tilde{f}\circ f)^{*}\nu
.

In the following, for some distributions
\nu^{(i)}\in\Delta(\Pi^{D})
for
i\in\mathcal{N}
and bounded measurable function
\eta\colon\Pi^{D}\times\dotsb\times\Pi^{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we will use the notational shorthands

\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim\nu^{(i)},\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\eta(\pi^{(1)},\dotsc,\pi^{(N)})\right]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(1)}\sim\nu^{(1)}}\left[\dots\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(N)}\sim\nu^{(N)}}\left[\eta(\pi^{(1)},\dotsc,\pi^{(N)})\right]\right]\dots\right]

and

\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim\nu^{(i)}}\left[\eta(\pi^{(1)},\dotsc,\pi^{(N)})\right]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\Pi^{D}}\eta(\pi^{(1)},\dotsc,\pi^{(N)})\mathrm{d}\nu^{(i)}(\pi^{(i)}).

Note that by Fubini’s theorem
(see
Williams 1991
, ch. 8)
, it is

\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim\nu^{(i)},\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\eta(\pi^{(1)},\dotsc,\pi^{(N)})\right]=\int_{\Pi^{D}\times\dotsb\times\Pi^{D}}\eta(\pi^{(1)},\dots,\pi^{(N)})\mathrm{d}\otimes_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\nu^{(i)}.

Now we define the LFC game for a Dec-POMDP.

Definition 36

(Label-free coordination game)
.

Let
D
be a Dec-POMDP and define
{\mathcal{D}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{f^{*}D\mid f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)\}
. The
label-free coordination (LFC) game
for
D
is defined as a tuple
\Gamma^{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\mathcal{N}^{D},(\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}})_{i\in\mathcal{N}},(U^{D})_{i\in\mathcal{N}})
where

•

\mathcal{N}^{D}
is the set of players, called principals.

•

\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
is the set of strategies for all principals
i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}
.

•

the common payoff for the strategy profile
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}\in\Sigma^{D}
is

U^{D}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{f_{j}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Iso}(D_{j},D)),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\Big[\\
\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(k)}\sim f_{k}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{k}(D_{k}),\,k\in\mathcal{N}}\Big[J^{D}((\pi^{(l)}_{l})_{l\in\mathcal{N}})\Big]\Big]\Big],

(64)

where
\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{D}})
is a uniform distribution over
{\mathcal{D}}
and
\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Iso}(D_{j},D))
a uniform distribution over
\mathrm{Iso}(D_{j},D)
.

Remark 37
.

Note that set of strategies
\Sigma^{D}
is continuous. The game
\Gamma^{D}
could hence be considered a continuous game, which is a generalization of the concept of a normal-form game to continuous strategy spaces
(
Glicksberg 1952
, see)
. In continuous games, it is usually assumed that the set of strategies is compact and that the payoffs are continuous functions, which we believe does apply in our case.

Moreover, we believe that there is some other normal-form game with finite strategy space such that the
mixed strategies
in that game correspond to the set of strategies
\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
in
\Gamma^{D}

(
Osborne & Rubinstein 1994
;
Gibbons 1992
, for a reference on these concepts from game theory, see)
. In particular, one can see that the set of strategies
\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
is already convex.

We do not need any further characterization of an LFC game in the following, so we do not investigate issues such as compactness or convexity of the set of strategies. The formalism at hand was chosen primarily to work well with an intuitive formulation of the LFC problem and to suit our discussion of the OP algorithm.

Next, to recall the definition of the LFC problem, let any set
{\mathcal{C}}
of Dec-POMDPs be given, and denote
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\bigcup_{D\in{\mathcal{C}}}{\mathcal{D}}^{D}
where
{\mathcal{D}}^{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{f^{*}D\mid f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)\}
is the set of all relabeled problems of
D
. The LFC problem for
{\mathcal{C}}
is then defined as the problem of finding one learning algorithm
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
to be used by principals in a randomly drawn game
\Gamma^{D}
for
D\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{C}})
.

Definition 38

(Label-free coordination problem)
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be any set of Dec-POMDPs.
Define the objective
U^{\mathcal{C}}\colon\Sigma^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
via

U^{\mathcal{C}}(\sigma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{E}_{E\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{C}})}\left[U^{E}(\sigma,\dotsc,\sigma)\right]

(65)

for

\sigma\in\Sigma^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
. Then we define the
Label-free coordination (LFC) problem
for
{\mathcal{C}}
as the optimization problem

\max_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}}U^{\mathcal{C}}(\sigma)

(66)

and we call
U^{\mathcal{C}}(\sigma)
the value of
\sigma
in the LFC problem for
{\mathcal{C}}
.
If
{\mathcal{C}}=\{D\}
, we write
U^{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=U^{\{D\}}
in a slight abuse of notation and refer to this as the LFC problem for
E
.

Remark 39
.

The aim of the LFC problem is to find a general learning algorithm to recommended to principals in any LFC game. For this reason, we defined the problem here for a distribution over LFC games. However, a learning algorithm is optimal in the problem for a set of Dec-POMDPs if and only if it is optimal in the problem for each Dec-POMDP in that set. That is because, as one can easily see, the sets
{\mathcal{D}}^{D},{\mathcal{D}}^{E}
do never overlap for two non-isomorphic Dec-POMDPs
D,E
, and we will show in Corollary
43
that the LFC problems for two isomorphic Dec-POMDPs are identical. So to evaluate a learning algorithm in the LFC problem for a set of Dec-POMDPs, we can decompose the set into equivalence classes of isomorphic Dec-POMDPs and evaluate the learning algorithm separately for each of these classes. In the following, we will thus simplify our analysis and restrict ourselves entirely to problems defined for single Dec-POMDPs. Note that the objective in the LFC problem is then simply

U^{D}(\sigma)=U^{\{D\}}(\sigma)=\mathbb{E}_{E\sim\mathcal{U}(\{D\})}\left[U^{E}(\sigma,\dotsc,\sigma)\right]=U^{D}(\sigma,\dotsc,\sigma).

(67)

If we then prove, e.g., that a learning algorithm is optimal in any such problem, it follows that it is also optimal for the problem defined for any set of Dec-POMDPs.

Now we will provide two different expressions of the payoff in an LFC game. To that end, let
D
be a Dec-POMDP and define
{\mathcal{D}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{f^{*}D\mid f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)\}
. First, we provide an expression in terms of labelings.

Lemma 40
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
.
Then

U^{\mathcal{D}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}D),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}((\pi^{(j)}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(68)

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
33
that it is
\mathrm{Sym}(D)=\bigcup_{E\in{\mathcal{D}}}\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
, where one can easily see that the union is disjoint (i). Moreover, it follows from Lemma
19
that
|\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)|=|\mathrm{Iso}(D,F)|
for any Dec-POMDPs
E,F\in{\mathcal{D}}
, so there exists
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that
M=|\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)|
for any
E\in{\mathcal{D}}
, and from (i) it follows that
|\mathrm{Sym}(E)|=|{\mathcal{D}}||M|
(ii).

Next, by Lemma
18
, for any
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,D_{j})
, it is
f^{-1}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D_{j},D)
for any
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D_{j},D)
. In addition, by the same Lemma, for
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D_{j},D)
, it is
f=(f^{-1})^{-1}
and
f^{-1}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,D_{j})
and thus
f\in\{\tilde{f}^{-1}\mid\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,D_{j})\}
. Hence, it is

\{\tilde{f}\mid\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D_{j},D)\}=\{\tilde{f}^{-1}\mid\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,D_{j})\}.

(69)

Using the above, it follows that

\displaystyle U^{{\mathcal{D}}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N})

(70)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}_{j}\sim\mathrm{Iso}(D_{j},D),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(k)}\sim\mathbf{f}_{k}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{k}(D_{k}),\,k\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}((\pi^{(l)}_{l})_{l\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]\right]

(71)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:507})}{=}\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}_{j}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,D_{j}),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(k)}\sim(\mathbf{f}_{k}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{k}(D_{k}),\,k\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}((\pi^{(l)}_{l})_{l\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]\right]

(72)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(i),\,(ii)}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(E)),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim(\mathbf{f}_{j}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}(\mathbf{f}_{j}^{*}D),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}((\pi^{(k)}_{k})_{k\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(73)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(E)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}D),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}((\pi^{(j)}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right].

(74)

This concludes the proof.
∎

Second, we can prove a useful decomposition of the payoff in an LFC game into isomorphisms and automorphisms. We can already see here the connection to the OP objective (we will recall the OP objective in Appendix
D.1
). Recall the projection operator,
\mathrm{proj}_{i}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=x_{i}
for
x=(x_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{N}}
.

Lemma 41
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
.
For any
E,F\in{\mathcal{D}}
, choose
f_{E,F}\in\mathrm{Iso}(E,F)
arbitrarily. Then

\displaystyle U^{\mathcal{D}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N})

(75)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim f_{D_{j},D}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}(D_{j}),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}\left[J^{D}\left(\left(\mathrm{proj}_{k}(\mathbf{g}_{k}^{*}\pi^{(k)})\right)_{k\in\mathcal{N}}\right)\right]\right]\right].

(76)

Proof.

Let
i\in\mathcal{N},D_{i}\in{\mathcal{D}}
. We know from Lemma
19
that for any
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D_{i},D)
there is a unique
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
such that
f=g\circ f_{D_{i},D}
. Also, for the pushforward measure, it is
(g\circ f_{D_{i},D})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(D_{i})=g^{*}f_{D_{i},D}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(D_{i})
for any
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
. Using this, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}_{j}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Iso}(D_{j},D)),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(k)}\sim\mathbf{f}_{k}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{k}(D_{k}),\,k\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}((\pi^{(l)}_{l})_{l\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]\right]

(77)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(k)}\sim\mathbf{g}_{k}^{*}f_{D_{k},D}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{k}(D_{k}),\,k\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}((\pi^{(j)}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]\right]

(78)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(k)}\sim f_{D_{k},D}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{k}(D_{k}),\,k\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[J^{D}\left(\left(\mathrm{proj}_{j}(\mathbf{g}_{j}^{*}\pi^{(j)})\right)_{j\in\mathcal{N}}\right)\right]\right]\right],

(79)

where we have used a change of variables for pushforward measures in the last line.
∎

Finally, we can show that the payoff in an LFC game is equal for isomorphic Dec-POMDPs, up to a possible permutation of principals. Intuitively, this means that the game does not depend on labels for the problem.

Theorem 42
.

Let
D,E
be isomorphic and
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
arbitrary. Define

{\mathcal{D}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{f^{*}D\mid f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)\}
and
{\mathcal{C}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{f^{*}E\mid f\in\mathrm{Sym}(E)\}
.
Then
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{C}}
,
and for any profile of algorithms
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N})\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
, it is

U^{D}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N})=U^{E}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f^{-1}1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f^{-1}N}).

Proof.

First, note that by Lemma
34
, it is

{\mathcal{C}}=\{\tilde{f}^{*}E\mid\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(E)\}\overset{\text{Lemma~\ref{d-sym-closed-under-labeling}}}{=}\{(\tilde{f}\circ f)^{*}D\mid\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(E)\}\\
=\{\hat{f}^{*}D\mid\hat{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(E)\circ f\}\overset{\text{Lemma~\ref{d-sym-closed-under-labeling}}}{=}\{\hat{f}^{*}D\mid\hat{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)\}={\mathcal{D}}.

(80)

Now let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
arbitrary.
Then, using the expression of
U^{D}
from Lemma
40
, it is

\displaystyle U^{D}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N})

(81)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}D),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}((\pi^{(j)}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(82)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}D),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{E}(f^{*}(\pi^{(j)}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(83)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}D),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{E}((\pi^{(f^{-1}j)}_{f^{-1}j}(f^{-1}\cdot\mid f^{-1}\cdot))_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(84)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}D),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{E}((\mathrm{proj}_{j}(f^{*}\pi^{(f^{-1}j)}))_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(85)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim f^{*}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}D),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{E}((\mathrm{proj}_{j}(\pi^{(f^{-1}j)}))_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(86)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim((\mathbf{f}_{i}\circ f^{-1})^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}D),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{E}((\mathrm{proj}_{j}(\pi^{(f^{-1}j)}))_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(87)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim((\mathbf{f}_{i}\circ f^{-1})^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}((\mathbf{f}_{i}\circ f^{-1})^{*}E),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{E}((\mathrm{proj}_{j}(\pi^{(f^{-1}j)}))_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(88)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(E)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}E),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{E}((\mathrm{proj}_{j}(\pi^{(f^{-1}j)}))_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(89)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(E)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim(\mathbf{f}_{f^{-1}i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f^{-1}i}(\mathbf{f}_{f^{-1}i}^{*}E),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{E}((\mathrm{proj}_{j}(\pi^{(j)}))_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(90)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(E)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f^{-1}i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}E),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{E}((\mathrm{proj}_{i}(\pi^{(i)}))_{i\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(91)

\displaystyle=U^{E}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f^{-1}1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{f^{-1}N}).

(92)

Here, in (
83
), we use Theorem
22
; in (
84
) and (
85
), we use the definition of the pushforward policy; in (
86
), we apply a change of variables for pushforward measures, applied to each of the measures
(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}D)
separately; in (
87
), we use the associativity of the pushforward measure as well as the fact that
(\mathbf{f}_{i}\circ f^{-1})^{-1}=f\circ\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1}
; in (
88
) and in (
89
), we use the second respectively first part of Lemma
34
; in (
90
), we again use a change of variables for pushforward measures, this time applied to the joint measure
\otimes_{i\in\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}D)
; and in (
91
) we use the symmetry of the set
\mathrm{Sym}(E)^{\mathcal{N}}
with respect to player permutations, concluding the proof.
∎

First, this result implies that the LFC problems for two isomorphic problems are identical.

Corollary 43
.

Let
D,E
be two isomorphic Dec-POMDPs and let
{\mathcal{D}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{f^{*}D\mid f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)\}
and
{\mathcal{C}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{f^{*}E\mid f\in\mathrm{Sym}(E)\}
. Then
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{C}}
and
U^{D}(\sigma)=U^{E}(\sigma)
for any
\sigma\in\Sigma^{{\mathcal{D}}}=\Sigma^{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
42
, we have
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{C}}
.
Now let
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}=\Sigma^{\mathcal{C}}
.
Then again by Theorem
42
, it is

U^{D}(\sigma)=U^{D}(\sigma,\dotsc,\sigma)\overset{\text{Theorem~\ref{zero-shot-coordination-game-invariant-to-isomorphism}}}{=}U^{E}(\sigma,\dotsc,\sigma)=U^{E}(\sigma).

∎

Second, the theorem shows that symmetries between the agents in a Dec-POMDP are also symmetries between principals in
\Gamma^{D}
.

Corollary 44
.

Let
D
be a Dec-POMDP. Then it is

U^{D}(\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{N})=U^{D}(\sigma_{g^{-1}1},\dotsc,\sigma_{g^{-1}N}).

for any
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
42
, using that
\mathrm{Aut}(D)=\mathrm{Iso}(D,D)
.
∎

C.7
Optimal symmetric strategy profiles

Above, we have shown that the payoff in an LFC game is invariant with respect to symmetries of the agents in
D
. Similarly to the case of Dec-POMDPs and their symmetries, we can also apply the concept of symmetry to profiles of learning algorithms. We can then ask whether a profile of learning algorithms is invariant to symmetries of the principals, in which case we say that the profile is symmetric. In the following, we will show that optimal profiles among the ones that are symmetric are Nash equilibria of an LFC game. Since we will show in Appendix
F
that a profile in which all principals choose OP with tie-breaking is an optimal symmetric profile, it will result as a corollary that all principals using OP with tie-breaking is a Nash equilibrium of the game.

To begin, we define symmetric principals and profiles of learning algorithms. In the following, let again
D
be a Dec-POMDP and
{\mathcal{D}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{f^{*}D\mid f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)\}
.

Definition 45

(Symmetric principals and strategy profiles)
.

We say that two principals
i,j\in\mathcal{N}
are symmetric if there exists an automorphism
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
such that
i=gj
. A profile of learning algorithms
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}\in\Sigma^{D}
is called symmetric if it is
\sigma_{i}=\sigma_{g^{-1}i}
for any automorphism
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
and principal
i\in\mathcal{N}
.

An optimal symmetric profile is then defined as a symmetric profile
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
such that for all other symmetric profiles
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
, it is
U^{D}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\geq U^{D}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
. Note that if there are non-symmetric principals in
\mathcal{N}
, then for a single learning algorithm
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
, the property that
\sigma,\dotsc,\sigma
is an optimal symmetric profile in the LFC
game
for
D
is stronger than the property that the algorithm
\sigma
is optimal in the LFC
problem
for
D
. The latter only requires that
U^{D}(\sigma,\dotsc,\sigma)\geq U^{D}(\sigma^{\prime},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime})
for all
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
, while
\sigma,\dotsc,\sigma
being an optimal symmetric profile means that
U^{D}(\sigma,\dotsc,\sigma)\geq U^{D}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N})
for all symmetric profiles
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
, where a profile could potentially include different learning algorithms for non-symmetric principals.

For a simple characterization of symmetric profiles, consider the orbit
\mathrm{Aut}(D)i
of a principal
i\in\mathcal{N}
, as defined in Appendix
C.4
. Clearly, saying that a profile is symmetric can equivalently be expressed as saying that all principals from the same orbit are assigned the same learning algorithm.

Lemma 46
.

A profile
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}\in\Sigma^{D}
is symmetric if and only if it is
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}
for any two symmetric principals
i,j\in\mathcal{N}
.

Proof.

This can be easily seen from the definition of the orbit and the properties of actions of automorphisms on agents and principals.
∎

Lastly, we define a Nash equilibrium of an LFC game.

Definition 47

(Nash equilibrium of an LFC game)
.

A profile of learning algorithms
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
is a Nash equilibrium of the LFC game for
D
if, for any principal
i\in\mathcal{N}
and learning algorithm
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
, it is

U^{D}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\geq U^{D}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{-i}).

Now we show that any optimal symmetric profile is a Nash equilibrium. An analogous result for normal-form games was proven in
Emmons et al. 2021
. Our proof closely follows that proof, adapted to our setting.

Theorem 48
.

Any optimal symmetric strategy profile in an LFC game is a Nash equilibrium.

Proof.

In the following, fix a Dec-POMDP
D
and let
{\mathcal{D}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{f^{*}D\mid f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)\}
. Let
U\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=U^{D}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}
be an optimal symmetric strategy profile. Towards a contradiction, assume that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is not a Nash equilibrium, i.e., that there is
i\in\mathcal{N}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
such that
U(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{-i})>U(\boldsymbol{\sigma})
. We show that then there is another symmetric strategy profile
\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
that achieves a higher payoff than
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
,
U(\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})>U(\boldsymbol{\sigma})
, contradicting the assumption that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
was optimal among the symmetric profiles.

To that end, for arbitrary
p\in(0,1]
define the profile
\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=p\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i}+(1-p)\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}
for any
j\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)i
and
