\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}
for
j\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\mathrm{Aut}(D)i
. Note that, since we jointly change all learning algorithms in one orbit
\mathrm{Aut}(D)i
, the remaining profile
\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is symmetric by Lemma
46
. Next, let
K\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=|\mathrm{Aut}(D)i|
, choose Dec-POMDPs
D_{1},\dotsc,D_{N}\in{\mathcal{D}}
, and measurable sets
\mathcal{Z}_{1},\dotsc,\mathcal{Z}_{N}\subseteq\Pi^{D}
. Then it is

\displaystyle(\otimes_{j\in\mathcal{N}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(D_{j}))(\prod_{l\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{Z}_{l})

(93)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\mathrm{Aut}(D)i}\boldsymbol{\sigma}(D_{j})(\mathcal{Z}_{j})\prod_{l\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)i}\left(p\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i}(D_{l})(\mathcal{Z}_{l})+(1-p)\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(D_{l})(\mathcal{Z}_{l})\right)

(94)

\displaystyle=(1-p)^{K}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}(D_{j})(\mathcal{Z}_{j})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{j\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)i}p(1-p)^{K-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i}(D_{j})(\mathcal{Z}_{j})\prod_{l\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\{j\}}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{l}(D_{l})(\mathcal{Z}_{l})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{k=2,\dotsc,K}p^{k}(1-p)^{K-k}\mu^{j,k,D_{j}}\left(\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{Z}_{j}\right)

(95)

\displaystyle=(1-p)^{K}\otimes_{j\in\mathcal{N}}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}(D_{j})\left(\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{Z}_{j}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{j\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)i}p(1-p)^{K-1}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i}(D_{j})\otimes_{l\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\{j\}}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{l}(D_{l})\right)\left(\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{Z}_{j}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{k=2,\dotsc,K}p^{k}(1-p)^{K-k}\mu^{j,k,D_{j}}\left(\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{Z}_{j}\right)

(96)

where
\mu^{k,D_{1},\dotsc,D_{N}}
is some measure (not necessarily a probability measure) on the space
\Pi^{D}\times\dotsb\times\Pi^{D}
that depends on
k
and
D_{1},\dotsc,D_{N}
, but not on
p
.
This tells us that we can also decompose the integral with respect to the measure
\otimes_{j\in\mathcal{N}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(D_{j})
as in (
96
). It follows that

\displaystyle U(\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{1},\dotsc,\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{N})

(97)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim(\mathbf{f}_{j}^{-1})^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{j}(\mathbf{f}_{j}^{*}D),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}((\pi^{(l)}_{l})_{l\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(98)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{j}(\mathbf{f}_{j}^{*}D),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}\left(\left(\mathrm{proj}_{l}((\mathbf{f}_{l}^{-1})^{*}\pi^{(l)})\right)_{l\in\mathcal{N}}\right)\right]\right]

(99)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:84})}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\Big[(1-p)^{K}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}(\mathbf{f}_{j}^{*}D),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}\left(\left(\mathrm{proj}_{l}((\mathbf{f}_{l}^{-1})^{*}\pi^{(l)})\right)_{l\in\mathcal{N}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)i}p(1-p)^{K-1}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(m)}\sim\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{m}(\mathbf{f}_{m}^{*}D),\,m\in\mathcal{N}\setminus j}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{j}^{*}D)}\left[J^{D}\left(\left(\mathrm{proj}_{l}((\mathbf{f}_{l}^{-1})^{*}\pi^{(l)})\right)_{l\in\mathcal{N}}\right)\right]\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{k=2,\dotsc,K}p^{k}(1-p)^{K-k}\int J^{D}\left(\left(\mathrm{proj}_{l}((\mathbf{f}_{l}^{-1})^{*}\pi^{(l)})\right)_{l\in\mathcal{N}}\right)\mathrm{d}\mu^{k,\mathbf{f}_{1}^{*}D,\dotsc,\mathbf{f}_{N}^{*}D}\left(\pi^{(1)},\dotsc,\pi^{(N)}\right)\Big]

(100)

\displaystyle=(1-p)^{K}U(\boldsymbol{\sigma})+\sum_{j\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)i}p(1-p)^{K-1}U(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j-1},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j+1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N})

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{k=2,\dotsc,K}p^{k}(1-p)^{K-k}C_{k}

(101)

\displaystyle=B(m=0,p)U(\boldsymbol{\sigma})+B(m=1,p)U(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{-i})+\sum_{k=2,\dotsc,K}p^{k}(1-p)^{K-k}C_{k}

(102)

for some constants
C_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
for
k=2,\dotsc,K
, and where we write
B(k=0,p)
to denote the probability that a binomial distribution with
K
trials and success chance
p
has
0
successful trials (and
B(k=1,p)
analogously). Here, in (
99
), we use a change of variables for pushforward measures, separately for each measure
\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{j}(\mathbf{f}_{j}^{*}D)
; in (
101
), we use the linearity of the expectation; and in (
102
) we use that
U
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
are both invariant to symmetries between principals, and thus for
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
with
g^{-1}j=i
, it is

U(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{-i})=U(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{g^{-1}1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{g^{-1}(j-1)},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{g^{-1}(j+1)},\dots,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{g^{-1}N})\\
=U(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j-1},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j+1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}).

(103)

Now note that if we can show that

(1-B(m=0,p))U(\boldsymbol{\sigma})<B(m=1,p)U(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{-i})+\sum_{k=2,\dotsc,K}p^{k}(1-p)^{K-k}C_{k},

then it would also follow that

U(\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:85aa})--(\ref{eq:85b})}}{=}B(m=0,p)U(\boldsymbol{\sigma})+B(m=1,p)U(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{-i})+\sum_{k=2,\dotsc,K}p^{k}(1-p)^{K-k}C_{k}\\
>B(m=0,p)U(\boldsymbol{\sigma})+(1-B(m=0,p))U(\boldsymbol{\sigma})=U(\boldsymbol{\sigma}).

(104)

Thus, this would show that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is a symmetric profile with higher payoff than
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, proving the required contradiction.

In the following, we show the equivalent condition

U(\boldsymbol{\sigma})<\frac{B(1,p)}{B(m>0,p)}U(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{-i})+\frac{\sum_{k=2,\dotsc,K}p^{k}(1-p)^{K-k}C_{k}}{B(m>0,p)},

where
B(m>0,p)=\sum_{k=1}^{K}B(m=k,p)=1-B(m=0,p)
.
To that end, note that since
U(\boldsymbol{\sigma})<U(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{-i})
by assumption, we can choose some small
\epsilon>0
such that still

U(\boldsymbol{\sigma})<U(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{-i})-\epsilon.

Moreover, note that
B(m>0,p)

is a polynomial in
p
, and the degree of its nonzero term with lowest degree is
1
. Similarly, for
\sum_{k=2,\dotsc,K}p^{K-k}(1-p)^{2}C_{k}
, that lowest degree is
2
.
Hence, it is

\frac{\sum_{k=2,\dotsc,K}p^{k}(1-p)^{K-k}C_{k}}{B(m>0,p)}=\frac{\sum_{k=2,\dotsc,K}p^{k-1}(1-p)^{K-k}C_{k}}{C+Q(p)}

for some constant
C\neq 0
and polynomial
Q
in
p
, and it follows that

\lim_{p\rightarrow 0}\frac{\sum_{k=2,\dotsc,K}p^{k}(1-p)^{K-k}C_{k}}{B(m>0,p)}=\lim_{p\rightarrow 0}\frac{\sum_{k=2,\dotsc,K}p^{k-1}(1-p)^{K-k}C_{k}}{C+Q(p)}=0.

With the same argument, it is also
\lim_{p\rightarrow 0}\frac{B(m>1,p)}{B(m>0,p)}=0
and thus

\lim_{p\rightarrow 0}\frac{B(m=1,p)}{B(m>0,p)}=1-\lim_{p\rightarrow 0}\frac{B(m>1,p)}{B(m>0,p)}=1.

Hence, we can find some
p>0
that is small enough such that both

\frac{\sum_{k=2,\dotsc,K}p^{k}(1-p)^{K-k}C_{k}}{B(m>0,p)}<\frac{\epsilon}{2}

and

1+\frac{\epsilon}{2|U(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{-i})|}>\frac{B(m=1,p)}{B(m>0,p)}>1-\frac{\epsilon}{2|U(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{-i})|}.

It follows that

U(\boldsymbol{\sigma})<U(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{-i})-\epsilon=\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{2U(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{-i})}\right)U(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{-i})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\\
<\frac{B(m=1,p)}{B(m>0,p)}U(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{-i})+\frac{\sum_{k=2,\dotsc,K}p^{k}(1-p)^{K-k}C_{k}}{B(m>0,p)},

(105)

which is what we wanted to show. This concludes the proof.
∎

C.8
Evaluating equivariant learning algorithms

In the following, let
D
be a Dec-POMDP and define
{\mathcal{D}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{f^{*}D\mid f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)\}
.
In the special case in which a learning algorithm is in a sense independent from the used labels, we can find a simpler form of the algorithm’s value in the LFC problem for
D
. To do so, we define the notion of an equivariant learning algorithm.

Definition 49

(Equivariant learning algorithms)
.

Let
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
. Then
\sigma
is called
equivariant
if for any two labelings
f,\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
, it is

(f^{-1})^{*}\sigma(f^{*}D)=(\tilde{f}^{-1})^{*}\sigma(\tilde{f}^{*}D).

Remark 50
.

We believe that a learning algorithm implemented via neural networks and one-hot encodings, as used in our experiments, should be equivariant. To see this, note that by a symmetry argument, the distribution over functions corresponding to a randomly initialized neural network is invariant with respect to coordinate permutations. Assume that actions, observations, and agents of a given problem are implemented as one-hot vectors, i.e., elements of a canonical basis
\{e_{1},\dotsc,e_{k}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
where
k\in\mathbb{N}
is the cardinality of the respective set. Then the distribution over randomly initialized neural network policies will also not depend on particular assignments of actions, etc., to one-hot vectors. We conjecture that, if the used optimizer is equivariant with respect to coordinate permutations (i.e., if the parameter dimensions are permuted, then the prescribed updates to the parameters are equally permuted), then the resulting learning algorithm is equivariant. We leave a rigorous exploration of this issue to future work.

An equivariant algorithm can be evaluated in the LFC problem for
D
by evaluating its cross-play value in any Dec-POMDP

E\in{\mathcal{D}}
. The resulting policies can be permuted by random automorphisms or they can be evaluated as they are.

Proposition 51
.

Let
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
be equivariant. Then for any
f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
and
E=f^{*}D\in{\mathcal{D}}
, it is

\displaystyle U^{D}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim\sigma(E),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(E)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[J^{E}\left(\left(\mathrm{proj}_{j}(\mathbf{g}_{j}^{*}\pi^{(j)})\right)_{j\in\mathcal{N}}\right)\right]\right]

(106)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim\sigma(E),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{E}\left(\left(\pi^{(j)}_{j}\right)_{j\in\mathcal{N}}\right)\right].

(107)

Proof.

First, let
f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
and
E\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=f^{*}D
. Using the expression of the payoff in the LFC game for
D
from Lemma
40
, it is

\displaystyle U^{D}(\sigma)=U^{D}(\sigma,\dotsc,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\sigma(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}D),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}((\pi^{(j)}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(108)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim(f^{-1})^{*}\sigma(E),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}((\pi^{(j)}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]

(109)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim\sigma(E),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}((f^{-1})^{*}(\pi^{(j)}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]

(110)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim\sigma(E),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{E}((\pi^{(j)}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right],

(111)

where we use equivariance in (
109
), a change of variables for pushforward measures in (
110
), and Theorem
22
in (
111
).

Second, by Lemma
33
, it is
\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)\subseteq\mathrm{Sym}(D)
. Thus, using
Lemma
19
, it is
\mathrm{Aut}(E)\circ f=\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)\subseteq\mathrm{Sym}(D)
.
Hence, it follows that

\displaystyle U^{D}(\sigma)=U^{D}(\sigma,\dotsc,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim(f^{-1})^{*}\sigma(E),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}((\pi^{(j)}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]

(112)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(E)^{\mathcal{N}}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim(f^{-1}\circ\mathbf{g}_{i})^{*}\sigma(E),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}((\pi^{(j)}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(113)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(E)^{\mathcal{N}}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}\sigma(E),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{E}((\pi^{(j)}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(114)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(E)^{\mathcal{N}}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim\sigma(E),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{E}((\mathrm{proj}_{j}(\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}\pi^{(j)}))_{j\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right],

(115)

where we again use equivariance in (
113
), a change of variables and Theorem
22
in (
114
), and another change of variables in (
115
).
This concludes the proof.
∎

If a learning algorithm is equivariant, we can use either of the expressions above to evaluate it in the LFC problem. One may choose the first expression, i.e., apply random automorphisms to policies, since this transformation maps different policies that are equivalent under OP to a unique automorphism-invariant policy and thus reduces the variance of the cross-play values across different samples
\pi\sim\sigma(D)
. We will introduce these notions of equivalence and automorphism-invariant policies in Appendix
D
.

Note that an equivariant learning algorithm is not necessarily one that performs well in the LFC problem. For instance, a SP algorithm may be equivariant. However, if an algorithm is equivariant and it does well in cross-play, then the preceding shows that it will also do well in the LFC problem.

Appendix D
Characterization of other-play and of label-free coordination games

In this section, we define for a given policy
\pi
a policy
\Psi(\pi)
that corresponds to agents choosing local policies that are randomly permuted by automorphisms, and that is itself invariant to pushforward by automorphisms. We then use this self-map on policies
\Psi
, called the
symmetrizer
, to characterize both the OP objective and the payoff in an LFC game. This characterization helps us to analyze the OP-optimal policies in the two-stage lever game in Appendix
E
, and it allows us to prove a stronger result, showing that any OP algorithm that is not concentrated on only one equivalence class in the two-stage lever game is suboptimal in the corresponding LFC problem. We also use this notion of equivalence classes of policies to define OP with tie-breaking in Appendix
F
, which allows us to then show that random tie-breaking functions exist.

In the following, in Appendix
D.1
, we recall the definition of our generalization of OP, and we define policies that are invariant to automorphism. Afterwards, in Appendix
D.2
, we introduce the concept of a policy corresponding to a distribution over policies. In Appendix
D.3
, we introduce the other-play distribution, in which each agent’s local policy is chosen as pushforward by a random automorphism, and we define the symmetrizer. Using these concepts, in Appendix
D.4
, we give a new expression for both the OP objective and the payoff in an LFC game. The OP objective can be understood of as transforming a policy into one that is invariant to automorphisms, and evaluating that policy in SP. Finally, in Appendix
D.5
, we show that our generalized OP objective can in general not be understood of as the SP objective in a modified Dec-POMDP.

D.1
Generalization of other-play

In the following, fix a Dec-POMDP
D
. Recall that for a profile of automorphisms
\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}
and a joint policy
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
, we define the joint policy
\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\hat{\pi}
, where the local policy
\hat{\pi}_{i}
of agent
i\in\mathcal{N}
is given by the local policy of agent
i
in the pushforward policy
\mathbf{g}^{*}_{i}\pi
. That is, for
i\in\mathcal{N}
, we define

\hat{\pi}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathrm{proj}_{i}(\mathbf{g}^{*}_{i}\pi)=\pi_{\mathbf{g}_{i}^{-1}i}(\mathbf{g}_{i}^{-1}\cdot\mid\mathbf{g}_{i}^{-1}\cdot).

Using this, we define the OP objective as the expected return of a policy that is randomly permuted by such profiles of automorphisms.

Definition 52

(Other-play objective)
.

Define
J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}\colon\Pi^{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
via

J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[J^{D}(\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi)\right]

(116)

for
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
,
where
\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})
is a uniform distribution over
\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}
. We say that
J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}
is the
other-play (OP) objective
of
D
, and
J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)
is the
OP value
of
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
.

Remark 53
.

It is clear that this objective always admits a maximum. For instance, we can consider
\Pi^{D}
as a subset of
\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}[0,1]^{\mathcal{A}_{i}\times\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i}}
with its standard topology. As
\Pi^{D}
is a Cartesian product of simplices, it is a compact subset of this space. Moreover, one can check that the objective
J^{D}(\pi)
is continuous in the policy
\pi
, and that for
\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}
, the map
\pi\mapsto\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi
is continuous as well. Thus, also
J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}(\pi)
is continuous in
\pi
, as it is a finite linear combination of continuous functions. By the extreme value theorem, it follows that the function always attains a maximum.

Next, recall our formal definition of an OP learning algorithm as any algorithm that achieves an optimal OP value in expectation.

Definition 54

(Other-play learning algorithm)
.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be a finite set of Dec-POMDPs. A learning algorithm
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
is called an
OP learning algorithm
if for any
D\in{\mathcal{D}}
, it is

\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim\sigma^{\mathrm{OP}}(D)}[J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)]=\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi).

Now fix again a Dec-POMDP
D
and consider the notion of invariance to automorphism.

Definition 55

(Invariance to automorphism)
.

A policy
\pi\in\Pi
is called
invariant to automorphism
if
f^{*}\pi=\pi
for any
f\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
.

Clearly, if a policy is invariant to automorphism, then it has the same OP value and expected return.

Proposition 56
.

Let
\pi\in\Pi
be invariant to automorphism. Then

J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)=J(\pi).

Proof.

It is

J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[J(\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[J((\mathrm{proj}_{i}(\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}\pi))_{i\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\\
\overset{\text{(*)}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[J((\mathrm{proj}_{i}(\pi))_{i\in\mathcal{N}})\right]=J(\pi),

(117)

where we have used invariance to automorphism in (*).
∎

In the following, we will show that if a policy
\pi
is not already invariant to automorphism, then one can understand the OP objective as first transforming the policy into a policy
\Psi(\pi)
that is invariant to automorphism by applying the symmetrizer
\Psi
, and then evaluating the expected return of that policy. In that way, the OP objective ensures that policies cannot make use of arbitrary symmetry-breaking.

D.2
Policies corresponding to distributions over policies

In this section, let some Dec-POMDP
D
be fixed. As a first step towards defining the symmetrizer
\Psi
, we will define policies corresponding to distributions over policies for general distributions. Afterwards, we will turn to the particular policy
\Psi(\pi)
that corresponds to the
OP distribution
of
\pi
.

Recall that we introduced the set of distributions over policies as
\Delta(\Pi)
, the set of measures on the space
(\Pi,\mathcal{F})
. Let
\nu\in\Delta(\Pi)
. For a given distribution
\nu\in\Delta(\Pi)
and agent
i\in\mathcal{N}
, the marginal distribution
\nu_{i}
is defined as
\nu_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\nu(\mathrm{proj}_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}))
for any measurable set of local policies
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\subseteq\Pi_{i}
.
We say that
\nu
has
independent local policies
, if
\nu=\otimes_{i}\nu_{i}
, i.e.,
\nu
decomposes into independent marginal distributions over local policies for each agent. We denote such distributions by
\mu
.

Now let
\mu\in\Delta(\Pi)
be a distribution with independent local policies. We want to construct a policy
\pi^{\mu}
that represents each agent sampling a local policy
\pi_{i}\sim\mu_{i}
in the beginning of an episode, and then choosing actions according to that policy until the end of the episode. This policy should be equivalent to
\mu
in the sense that it should yield the same expected return as
\mu
, where we define the expected return of
\mu
as

J(\mu)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim\mu}\left[J(\pi)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\mu_{i},\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J(\pi)\right].

(118)

The statement that such a policy exists is analogous and more general than a result by
Kuhn 1953
, which says that in an extensive-form game, given some conditions on the game, for every mixed strategy there is an equivalent behavior strategy. Kuhn’s theorem is relevant to Dec-POMDPs since there is a correspondence between Dec-POMDPs and extensive-form games
(
Oliehoek et al. 2006
)
. For Dec-POMDPs, the analogous result states that for every distribution over deterministic policies, there is an equivalent stochastic policy.

We cannot directly apply Kuhn’s theorem here, as we require a result for distributions over stochastic policies instead of deterministic policies. This is because such a result fits better with our remaining setup—for instance, the domain of the OP objective is the set of stochastic policies, and learning algorithms are defined as distributions over stochastic policies. Nevertheless, our proof is based on similar ideas as, for instance, the proof of Kuhn’s theorem in
Maschler et al. 2013
, after translating between the different formal frameworks of extensive-form games and Dec-POMDPs.

To define a policy
\pi^{\mu}
that is equivalent to
\mu
, we begin by defining a new measure space
(\Pi\times\Omega,\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{P}(\Omega))
, which is the product space of the space of policies
(\Pi,\mathcal{F})
with the Dec-POMDP environment
(\Omega,\mathcal{P}(\Omega))
. On this space, for a given distribution
\mu
, we define a probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
which represents the procedure outlined above, i.e., in which a policy
\pi
is chosen according to
\mu
, and then samples in
\Omega
are distributed according to
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}
. Formally, we define
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
as the unique measure such that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{Q})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim\mu}\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\mathcal{Q})\right]

(119)

for any measurable sets
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\Pi
and
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\Omega
.
Note that the product sets
\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{Q}
for measurable
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\Pi
and
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\Omega
are a
\pi
-system and generate the product
\sigma
-Algebra
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
. Hence, by Carathéodory’s extension theorem, there is a unique measure satisfying this definition
(see
Williams 1991
, ch. 1)
.

On this new product space
\Pi\times\Omega
, define the random variable
Z
as the projection onto
\Pi
. We can define histories
H
and all other random variables defined on the space
(\Omega,\mathcal{P}(\Omega))
by composing them with the projection onto
\Omega
(for notational convenience, we denote these random variables using the same symbols in both spaces).

Remark 57
.

Note that the conditional probability of a particular trajectory
\tau
given the policy
Z
is just the probability of that history under
\mathbb{P}_{Z}
, that is,

\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(H=\tau\mid Z)=\mathbb{P}_{Z}(H=\tau)

(120)

for any
\tau\in\mathcal{H}
.
Here, the conditional probability is a random variable, defined via the conditional expectation

\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(H=\tau\mid Z)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathds{1}_{H=\tau}\mid Z\right].

Intuitively, for a given sample
(\pi,\omega)
, the value of that random variable is the best estimate of the probability of
\{H=\tau\}
given
Z=\pi
, ignoring
\omega
. As, in general,
\{Z=\pi\}
may have zero probability, it is impossible to define the conditional probability
\mathbb{P}(H=\tau\mid Z=\pi)
via
\mathbb{P}(H=\tau\mid Z=Z)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{\mathbb{P}(H=\tau,Z=\pi)}{\mathbb{P}(Z=\pi)}
. It is still possible to define the
conditional expectation

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathds{1}_{H=\tau}\mid Z\right]
, though.

Here, we briefly give the definition of the conditional expectation and show that (
120
) is correct. For a reference on conditional expectations, refer to
(
Williams 1991
, ch. 9)
. Applied to our setup, the conditional expectation of
\mathds{1}_{H=\tau}
given
Z
is any random variable (which can be shown to be almost surely unique), denoted by
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathds{1}_{H=\tau}\mid Z\right]
, that is measurable with respect to

\sigma(Z)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{Z^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})\mid\mathcal{Z}\in\mathcal{F}\}=\{\mathcal{Z}\times\Omega\mid\mathcal{Z}\in\mathcal{F}\}

such that

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathds{1}_{H=\tau}\mid Z\right]\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathds{1}_{H=\tau}\right].

(121)

for all
\mathcal{X}\in\sigma(Z)
. The fact that the random variable is measurable with respect to
\sigma(Z)
can be equivalently expressed as saying that it can be written as a function of
Z
. Moreover, Equation
121
says that the conditional expectation should represent correct averages of the random variable
\mathds{1}_{H=\tau}
over the level-sets in
\sigma(Z)
.

To show that (
120
) is correct, let
\mathcal{X}\in\sigma(Z)
arbitrary.
Since
\mathcal{X}
is of the form
\mathcal{Z}\times\Omega
, and
\{H=\tau\}=\Pi\times\mathcal{Q}
for some
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\Omega
, it is
(\mathcal{Z}\times\Omega)\cap\{H=\tau\}=\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{Q}
.
Using Equation
119
, it follows that

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{P}_{Z}(H=\tau)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{Z}\times\Omega}\mathbb{P}_{Z}(\mathcal{Q})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim\mu}\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\pi)\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\mathcal{Q})\right]\\
\overset{(\ref{defn-semidirect-product})}{=}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{Q})=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathds{1}_{\Pi\times\Omega}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{Q}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathds{1}_{H=\tau}\right].

(122)

Hence, letting
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\mathds{1}_{H=\tau}\mid Z]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{P}_{Z}(H=\tau)
satisfies condition (
121
).
\mathbb{P}_{Z}(H=\tau)
is also
\sigma(Z)
-measurable, since it is just a function of
Z
.

We will repeatedly make use of (
120
) in the following, together with the tower property, which, applied to our case, says that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(H=\tau)=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathbb{P}_{\mu}\left(H=\tau\mid Z\right)\right]\overset{(\ref{conditional-probability-mixture})}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathbb{P}_{Z}\left(H=\tau\right)\right]

(123)

for any history
\tau\in\mathcal{H}

(see
Williams 1991
, ch. 9.7)
.

Using the measure space defined above, we now define a local policy
\pi_{i}^{\mu_{i}}
for an agent
i\in\mathcal{N}
, corresponding to a distribution
\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\Pi_{i})
. For an action
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and an action-observation history
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i,t}
, we define
\pi_{i}^{\mu_{i}}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})
as the probability that agent
i
, who follows a policy that is sampled from
\mu_{i}
, plays action
a_{i}
, conditional on
\{\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\}
.

Definition 58
.

Let
i\in\mathcal{N}
and let
\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\Pi_{i})
be a distribution over local policies of agent
i
. We define the local policy
\pi_{i}^{\mu_{i}}
corresponding to
\mu_{i}
in the following way. For
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
,
t\in\{0,\dotsc,T\}
and
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i,t}
, let

\pi^{\mu_{i}}_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{i}\otimes\mu_{-i}}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}),

(124)

where
\mu_{-i}
is any distribution over
\Pi_{-i}
with independent local policies such that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{i}\otimes\mu_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})>0.

If no such distribution exists, we let
\pi^{\mu}_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}_{i}|}
.

Note that if
\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{i}\otimes\mu_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})=0
for all the distributions
\mu_{-i}\in\Delta(\Pi_{-i})
over opponent policies, then agent
i
’s action-observation history
\tau_{i,t}
is almost never reached, independent of the other agents’ policies. In that case, we can define the policy arbitrarily and this will never matter for the distribution over histories under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{i}\otimes\mu_{-i}}
, and as we will see, neither for the distribution under
\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i}^{\mu_{i}},\pi_{-i}}
for arbitrary
\pi_{-i}
.

First, we need to make sure that
\pi_{i}^{\mu_{i}}
is well-defined, i.e., it does not depend on the chosen distribution
\mu_{-i}
. Unfortunately, the proof for the following Lemma is somewhat technical.

Lemma 59
.

Let
i\in\mathcal{N}
,
\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\Pi_{i})
,
t\in\{0,\dotsc,T\}
,
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i}
, and
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Let
\mu_{-i},\mu^{\prime}_{-i}\in\Delta(\Pi_{-i})
be any two distributions with independent local policies such that
\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{i}\otimes\mu_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})>0
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{i}\otimes\mu^{\prime}_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})>0
. Then it is

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{i}\otimes\mu_{-i}}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})=\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{i}\otimes\mu^{\prime}_{-i}}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}).

Proof.

In the following, let
i\in\mathcal{N},t\in\{0,\dotsc,T\}
be fixed.
Our goal is to find an expression for the distribution of
Z_{i}
given
\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}
that only depends on
\mu_{i}
. If we can do that, we can also show that the probability of a particular action chosen by an agent is independent of the distributions over other agents’ policies. To begin, we analyze the joint distribution of
Z_{i}
and
\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}
for an arbitrary distribution
\mu\in\Delta(\Pi)
with independent local policies. Note that by assumption, the
Z_{j}
are independent for
j\in\mathcal{N}
under
\mu
.
We now show that conditioning on
\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}
still leaves
Z_{i}
independent from
Z_{-i}
.

Z_{i}
A_{i,0}
\dots
O_{i,1}
\dots
A_{i,1}
\dots
O_{i,2}
\dots
A_{i,2}
\dots
\dots

Figure 8
:
Part of a Bayesian graph of the random variables on the space
\Pi\times\Omega
. Here, the gray-marked nodes are part of the action-observation history
\overline{A\!O}_{i,2}
. Conditional on
\overline{A\!O}_{i,2}
, there is an unblocked path, marked in teal, from
Z_{i}
to
A_{i,2}
and to nodes below
A_{i,2}
not displayed here, making them
d
-connected and thus dependent. The path marked in a lighter magenta, on the other hand, is blocked, illustrating that the part of the graph below
O_{i,2}
may be independent of
Z_{i}
.

To see this, one can consider a Bayesian graph of the random variables on the space
\Pi\times\Omega
. For a reference on Bayesian graphs and the concepts discussed below, refer to
Pearl 2009
. In Figure
8
, we have displayed a part of this Bayesian graph with nodes for the agent
i
, indicating left-out parts of the graph with dots. The arrows in the graph illustrate the dependence relationships between the different variables: if there is an arrow from one node to another, this means that the other node depends on that node. Nodes belonging to the action-observation history
\overline{A\!O}_{i,2}
of agent
i
are marked in gray.

Given such a Bayesian graph, the
d
-separation criterion tells us which variables are dependent after conditioning on a set
\mathcal{V}
of variables. The criterion specifies valid, “unblocked” paths in the graph, depending on the graph structure and the nodes that are being conditioned on. The
d
-separation criterion says that two variables are dependent conditional on the variables in
\mathcal{V}
if and only if there is an unblocked path in the graph between them; the variables are then said to be
d
-connected. If there is no path, then the variables are
d
-separated.

An unblocked path can contain a
chain

X\rightarrow W\rightarrow Y
or a
fork

X\leftarrow W\rightarrow Y
if
W
is not being conditioned on. If it contains a
collider

X\rightarrow W\leftarrow Y
, on the other hand, i.e., the two incident edges to
W
in the path are both directed towards the node, then the path is blocked, unless
W
or a descendant of
W
in the graph is in
\mathcal{V}
. For instance, the path that is marked in a lighter magenta in Figure
8
is blocked—the path cannot go from
Z_{i}
over
A_{i,1}
to
O_{i,2}
. An unblocked path is marked in teal.

Using the
d
-separation criterion, one can tell that
Z_{i}
may be
d
-separated from left-out parts of the graph in Figure
8
indicated by the dots below
A_{i,0}
, for instance, but that it is
d
-connected to some variables in the part below
A_{i,2}
. We do not work this out here completely, but once considering the entire graph, one can see that there is no unblocked path from
Z_{i}
to the
Z_{-i}
, because such a path would inevitably have to traverse a collider that is not being conditioned on and that has no descendants that are being conditioned on (for instance, the observations of all other agents are colliders that connect the
Z_{-i}
with
Z_{i}
). Generalizing from the example of
\tau_{i,2}
, we can conclude that
Z_{i}
and
Z_{-i}
are independent given
\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}
.

Now let
\mathcal{Z}_{j}\subseteq\Pi_{j}
be measurable sets for
j\in\mathcal{N}
, and let
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i,t}
arbitrary. Then, using the above, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{-i}\sim\mu_{-i}}[\mathds{1}_{\pi_{-i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{-i}}\mathbb{E}_{Z_{i}\sim\mu_{i}}[\mathds{1}_{\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]]

(125)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i},\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t},Z_{-i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{-i})

(126)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t},Z_{-i}\in C_{-i})\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t},Z_{-i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{-i})

(127)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t},Z_{-i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{-i})

(128)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{-i}\sim\mu_{-i}}[\mathds{1}_{\pi_{-i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{-i}}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\mu_{i}}[\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]],

(129)

where we use the argument about conditional independence in (
128
) and the definition of
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
from Equation
119
in (
126
) and (
129
).

Since the sets
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
for
j\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\{i\}
were arbitrary, it follows that
\mu_{-i}
-almost surely, it is

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\mu_{i}}[\mathds{1}_{\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}}\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},Z_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]\\
=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\mu_{i}}[\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},Z_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})].

(130)

Moreover, since
\mu
was arbitrary, Equation
130
holds for any distribution with independent local policies. If we divide by the term
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\mu_{i}}[\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},Z_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]
, this becomes

\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\mu_{i}}[\mathds{1}_{\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}}\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},Z_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]}{\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\mu_{i}}[\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},Z_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]},

(131)

which gives us a formula for the distribution of
Z_{i}
given
\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}
under the measure
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
that is independent of the distributions
\mu_{-i}
. But to be able to do so, we have to find a value for
Z_{-i}
such that
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\mu_{i}}[\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},Z_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]
is nonzero under
\mu
.

Now let
\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\Pi_{i})
and let
\mu_{-i},\mu^{\prime}_{-i}\in\Delta(\Pi_{-i})
be any two distributions with independent local policies such that
\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{i}\otimes\mu_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})>0
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{i}\otimes\mu^{\prime}_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})>0
. Define
\mu_{i}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mu_{i}
,
\mu\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mu_{i}\otimes\mu_{-i}
, and
\mu^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mu_{i}\otimes\mu^{\prime}_{-i}
.
Our goal is to show that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})=\mathbb{P}_{\mu^{\prime}}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}).

To that end, we define a third distribution
\hat{\mu}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\otimes_{j\in\mathcal{N}}(\frac{1}{2}\mu_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\mu_{j}^{\prime})
. Apparently, it is then
\mu_{i}=\hat{\mu}_{i}=\mu^{\prime}_{i}
and also
\hat{\mu}
has independent local policies.
We now prove separately for
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
that the distribution of
Z_{i}
given
\{\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\}
under
\mu
respectively
\mu^{\prime}
is equal to the one under
\hat{\mu}
. To do so, we use that
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are absolutely continuous with respect to
\hat{\mu}
to find desired values for
Z_{-i}
such that we can apply Equation
131
.

First, let
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\in\Pi_{i}
be an arbitrary measurable set. Using Equation
130
, we can find measurable sets
\mathcal{Z}_{-i},\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{-i}\subseteq\Pi_{-i}
such that
\mu_{-i}(\mathcal{Z}_{-i})=1=\hat{\mu}_{-i}(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{-i})
, and such that

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\mu_{i}}[\mathds{1}_{\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}}\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},\pi_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]\\
=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\mu_{i}}[\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},\pi_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})].

(132)

for any
\pi_{-i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{-i}

and

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\hat{\mu}_{i}}[\mathds{1}_{\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}}\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},\hat{\pi}_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]\\
=\mathbb{P}_{\hat{\mu}}(Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\mu_{i}}[\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},\hat{\pi}_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})].

(133)

for any
\hat{\pi}_{-i}\in\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{-i}
.

Next, it follows that
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\{Z_{-i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{-i}\}\cap\{\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\})>0
,
which by definition of
\hat{\mu}
implies

\mathbb{P}_{\hat{\mu}}(\{Z_{-i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{-i}\}\cap\{\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\})>0

and thus by definition of
\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{-i}
also
\mathbb{P}_{\hat{\mu}}(\{Z_{-i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{-i}\cap\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{-i}\}\cap\{\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\})>0
. Since

\mathbb{P}_{\hat{\mu}}(\{Z_{-i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{-i}\cap\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{-i}\}\cap\{\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\})\\
=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{-i}\sim\hat{\mu}_{-i}}\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{Z}_{-i}\cap\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{-i}}(\pi_{-i})\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\hat{\mu}_{i}}[\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},\pi_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]\right],

(134)

there must be

\pi_{-i}\in\mathrm{proj}_{\Pi_{-i}}(\{Z_{-i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{-i}\cap\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{-i}\}\cap\{\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\})

such that

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\hat{\mu}_{i}}[\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},\pi_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]>0.

Lastly, using that
\hat{\mu}_{i}=\mu_{i}
, it follows that

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\mu_{i}}[\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},\pi_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\hat{\mu}_{i}}[\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},\pi_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]>0.

(135)

Hence, we can use Equations
132
and
133
to conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:28a})}}{=}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\mu_{i}}[\mathds{1}_{\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}}\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},\pi_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]}{\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\mu_{i}}[\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},\pi_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]}

(136)

\displaystyle\overset{\mu_{i}=\hat{\mu}_{i}}{=}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\hat{\mu}_{i}}[\mathds{1}_{\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}}\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},\pi_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]}{\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}\sim\hat{\mu}_{i}}[\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{i},\pi_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})]}

(137)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:28b})}}{=}\mathbb{P}_{\hat{\mu}}(Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}).

(138)

Now note that one can make an exactly analogous argument for
\mu^{\prime}
and
\hat{\mu}
, potentially using a different
\pi_{-i}
. Hence, it follows that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})=\mathbb{P}_{\hat{\mu}}(Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})=\mathbb{P}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}).

(139)

Since
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\in\Pi_{i}
was arbitrary, it follows that the distribution of
Z_{i}
given
\{\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\}
is equal under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mu^{\prime}}
.

To conclude the proof, we can use this to show that also the distribution of actions under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mu^{\prime}}
are equal, conditional on
\{\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\}
. For
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, it is

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid Z,\overline{A\!O}_{i,t})\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\right]

(140)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathbb{P}_{Z}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t})\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\right]

(141)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[Z_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\right]

(142)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{equation-marginals-are-equal-2})}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\prime}}\left[Z_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\right]

(143)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\prime}}\left[\mathbb{P}_{Z}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t})\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\right]

(144)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\prime}}\left[\mathbb{P}_{\mu^{\prime}}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid Z,\overline{A\!O}_{i,t})\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\right]

(145)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\mu^{\prime}}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}),

(146)

where we have used the tower property in (
140
) and (
146
), and Equation
120
in (
141
) and (
145
). This is what we wanted to show.
∎

In the following, we write
\pi^{\mu}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\pi_{i}^{\mu_{i}})_{i\in\mathcal{N}}
for the joint policy corresponding to a distribution
\mu
with independent local policies.
Our next goal is to prove that the distribution over histories is the same under
\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}
as under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
.

Proposition 60
.

Consider any distribution
\mu\in\Delta(\Pi)
with independent local policies. Let
\pi^{\mu}
be the joint policy corresponding to
\mu
, as defined above. Then the history
H
has the same distribution under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
as under
\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}
. In particular,
it is
J^{D}(\mu)=J^{D}(\pi^{\mu})
.

Proof.

Fix a distribution
\mu\in\Delta(\Pi)
with independent local policies. We show by induction that for all
t\in\{0,\dotsc,T\}
, it is
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(H_{t}=\tau_{t})=\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}(H_{t}=\tau_{t})
for any
\tau_{t}\in\mathcal{H}_{t}
.

First, note that by Definition
58
, for any
i\in\mathcal{N},a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i},t\in\{0,\dotsc,h\}
, and
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i}
such that
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})>0
, it is

\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})=\pi^{\mu_{i}}_{i}(a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}).

(147)

In particular, this holds for
t=0
, in which case it is
\overline{A\!O}_{i,0}=\emptyset
for
i\in\mathcal{N}
. Hence, for
a\in\mathcal{A},s\in\mathcal{S}
, it is

\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(S_{0}=s,A_{0}=a)\overset{(\ref{eq:equation-tower-prop-z})}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\mathbb{P}_{Z}(S_{0}=s,A_{0}=a)]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\mathbb{P}_{Z}(S_{0}=s)\mathbb{P}_{Z}(A_{0}=a)]\\
\overset{\text{(i)}}{=}b_{0}(s)\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\mathbb{P}_{Z}(A_{0}=a)]=b_{0}(s)\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}Z_{i}(A_{i,0}\mid\emptyset)]\overset{\text{(ii)}}{=}b_{0}(s)\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[Z_{i}(a_{i}\mid\emptyset)]\\
\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:equation-tower-prop-z})}}{=}b_{0}(s)\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i,0}=a_{i})=b_{0}(s)\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i,0}=a_{i})\overset{(\ref{eq:5})}{=}b_{0}(s)\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}(A_{i,0}=a_{i})\\
=b_{0}(s)\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}(A_{0}=a)=\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}(S_{0}=s,A_{0}=a).

(148)

Here, we also use (i) the fact that that the initial state distribution does not depend on the policy, and (ii) the fact that
\mu
has independent local policies and thus also the
Z_{i}
are independent in the probability space
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
.

Next, assume that
0\leq t-1\leq T
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})=\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}(H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})
for any
\tau_{t-1}\in\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
. Let
\tau_{t-1}=(s_{0},a_{0},r_{0},s_{1},\dotsc,r_{t-1})\in\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
arbitrary such that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})=\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}(H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})>0
. As in the proof of Lemma
59
, it follows from considering the
d
-separation criterion on a Bayesian graph of the random variables defined on
\Pi\times\Omega
that conditioning on
H_{t-1}
does not make the
Z_{i}
dependent
(see
Pearl 2009
, ch. 1.2)
. The same criterion also says that, after conditioning on
A_{i,t-1}
and
\overline{A\!O}_{i,t-1}
, one cannot gain additional information about
Z_{i}
from the other components of
H_{t-1}
or from
O_{i,t}
(that is,
A_{i,t-1}
and
\overline{A\!O}_{i,t-1}

d
-separate
Z_{i}
from the rest of the history).
Using this in (
152
), it follows for arbitrary
s_{t},o_{t},a_{t},r_{t}
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(S_{t}=s_{t},O_{t}=o_{t},A_{t}=a_{t},R_{t}=r_{t}\mid H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})

(149)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(S_{t}=s_{t},O_{t}=o_{t},A_{t}=a_{t},R_{t}=r_{t}\mid H_{t-1},Z)\mid H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1}]

(150)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[P(s_{t}\mid s_{t-1},a_{t-1})O(o_{t}\mid s_{t},a_{t-1})\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}Z_{i}(a_{i,t}\mid\tau_{i,t})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{R}(s,a)=r}\mid H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1}]

(151)

\displaystyle=P(s_{t}\mid s_{t-1},a_{t-1})O(o_{t}\mid s,a_{t-1})

\displaystyle\quad\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[Z_{i}(a_{i,t}\mid\tau_{i,t})\mid A_{i,t-1}=a_{i,t-1},\overline{A\!O}_{i,t-1}=\tau_{i,t-1}]\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{R}(s_{t},a_{t})=r_{t}}

(152)

\displaystyle=P(s_{t}\mid s_{t-1},a_{t-1})O(o_{t}\mid s,a_{t-1})\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i,t}=a_{i,t}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{R}(s_{t},a_{t})=r_{t}}

(153)

\displaystyle=P(s_{t}\mid s_{t-1},a_{t-1})O(o_{t}\mid s,a_{t-1})\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}(A_{i,t}=a_{i,t}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{R}(s_{t},a_{t})=r_{t}}

(154)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}(S_{t}=s_{t},O_{t}=o_{t},A_{t}=a_{t},R_{t}=r_{t}\mid H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1}).

(155)

In (
154
), we again use Equation
147
, which is possible since
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})>0
implies that also
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t-1}=\tau_{i,t-1})>0
, where
\tau_{i,t-1}
is defined as the projection of
\tau_{t-1}
onto
\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i,t-1}
.

To conclude the inductive step, let
\tau_{t}\in\mathcal{H}_{t}
and choose
\tau_{t-1}
as the projection of
\tau_{t}
onto
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
. If

\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})=\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}(H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})=0,

necessarily also
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(H_{t}=\tau_{t})=\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}(H_{t}=\tau_{t})=0
and there is nothing more to show. Assume now that this is not the case. Using the inductive hypothesis and Equations (
149
)–(
155
) in (*), it then follows that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(H_{t}=\tau_{t})=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(H_{t}=\tau_{t}\mid H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})\\
=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(S_{t}=s_{t},O_{t}=o_{t},A_{t}=a_{t},R_{t}=r_{t}\mid H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})\\
\overset{\text{(*)}}{=}\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}(S_{t}=s_{t},O_{t}=o_{t},A_{t}=a_{t},R_{t}=r_{t}\mid H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}(H_{t-1}=\tau_{t-1})\\
=\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}(H_{t}=\tau_{t}).

(156)

This concludes the induction. In particular, it follows that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(H_{T}=\tau_{T})=\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}(H_{T}=\tau_{T}).

Hence,
H=H_{T}
has the same distribution under both
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\mu}}
.

Turning to the “in particular” statement, we can use the tower property (i) and Equation
120
(ii) to follow that

J(\pi^{\mu})=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\mu}}[\sum_{t=1}^{h}R_{t}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\sum_{t=1}^{h}R_{t}]\\
\overset{\text{(i)}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{h}R_{t}\,\middle|\,Z\right]\right]\overset{\text{(ii)}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathbb{E}_{Z}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{h}R_{t}\right]\right]\\
=\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim\mu}[J(\pi)]=J(\mu).

(157)

∎

Before we turn to the OP distribution, we prove another useful Lemma, stating that the mapping from distributions to corresponding joint policies and the pushforward by isomorphisms commute. That is, the policy corresponding to the pushforward of a distribution and the pushforward of the policy corresponding to that distribution are the same.

Lemma 61
.

Let
D,E
be isomorphic Dec-POMDPs with isomorphism
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
. Let
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
and let
\mu\in\Delta(\Pi^{D})
be any distribution with independent local policies. Then

f^{*}\pi^{\mu}=\pi^{f^{*}\mu}.

Proof.

First, we have to find an expression for the marginal distributions
(f^{*}\mu)_{i}
and prove that
f^{*}\mu
is a distribution with independent local policies. To that end, consider measurable sets
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\subseteq\Pi^{E}_{i}
for
i\in\mathcal{N}^{E}
and define
\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{E}}\mathcal{Z}_{i}
. In the following, we adopt the notation
\pi_{i}\circ f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\pi_{i}(f\cdot\mid f\cdot)
and
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\circ f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{\pi_{i}\circ f\mid\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\}
. Note that

(f^{*})^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})=\{\pi\mid f^{*}\pi\in\mathcal{Z}\}=\{\pi\mid\forall i\in\mathcal{N}^{E}\colon\pi_{f^{-1}i}\circ f^{-1}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i})\}\\
=\{\pi\mid\forall j\in\mathcal{N}^{D}\colon\pi_{j}\in\mathcal{Z}_{fj}\circ f\}=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}\mathcal{Z}_{fj}\circ f.

(158)

Hence, using in (i) that
\mu
has independent local policies, it follows that

(f^{*}\mu)(\mathcal{Z})=\mu((f^{*})^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}))\overset{(\ref{eq:29})}{=}\mu(\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}\mathcal{Z}_{fj}\circ f)\overset{\text{(i)}}{=}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}\mu_{j}(\mathcal{Z}_{fj}\circ f)=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{E}}\mu_{f^{-1}i}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}\circ f).

(159)

Now let
i\in\mathcal{N}^{E}
arbitrary. With the choice of
\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{Z}_{i}
and
\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Pi_{k}^{E}
for all
k\in\mathcal{N}^{E}\setminus\{i\}
, it is

(f^{*}\mu)_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})=f^{*}\mu(\mathrm{proj}_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}))=f^{*}\mu(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}\times\dotsb\times\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}))\\
\overset{(\ref{eq:30})}{=}\prod_{k\in\mathcal{N}^{E}}\mu_{f^{-1}k}(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{k}\circ f)=\mu_{f^{-1}i}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}\circ f),

(160)

as

\mu_{f^{-1}k}(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{k}\circ f)=\mu_{f^{-1}k}(\Pi_{k}\circ f)=\mu_{f^{-1}k}(\Pi_{f^{-1}k})=1

for any
k\in\mathcal{N}^{E}\setminus\{i\}
.

Since
i
was arbitrary, this shows that
f^{*}\mu(\mathcal{Z})=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{E}}(f^{*}\mu)_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})
.
Since the sets
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
were arbitrary and the Cartesian products of these sets are a
\pi
-system and generate the product
\sigma
-Algebra
\mathcal{F}
, this shows that
f^{*}\mu
has independent local policies.

Next, let
i\in\mathcal{N}^{E}
,
t\in\{0,\dotsc,T\}
and
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i,t}^{E}
. Note that
\mathrm{proj}_{i}(f^{*}\pi^{\mu})=\pi_{f^{-1}i}^{\mu_{f^{-1}i}}\circ f^{-1}
and

\mathrm{proj}_{i}(\pi^{f^{*}\mu})=\pi_{i}^{(f^{*}\mu)_{i}}
.
Hence, it remains to prove that
\pi_{f^{-1}i}^{\mu_{f^{-1}i}}\circ f^{-1}=\pi_{i}^{(f^{*}\mu)_{i}}
.

To that end, let
j\in\mathcal{N}^{D}
such that
fj=i
. By Theorem
22
, it is

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{j,t}=f^{-1}\tau_{i,t})=\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})

(161)

for any
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
. Letting,
\mu_{-j}\in\Delta(\Pi_{-j}^{D})
with independent local policies arbitrary and defining
\mu\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mu_{i}\otimes\mu_{-i}
, this means that also

\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\overline{A\!O}_{j,t}=f^{-1}\tau_{i,t})\overset{\text{(ii)}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathbb{P}_{Z}(\overline{A\!O}_{j,t}=f^{-1}\tau_{i,t})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}Z}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})\right]\\
=\mathbb{E}_{f^{*}\mu}\left[\mathbb{P}_{Z}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})\right]\overset{\text{(ii)}}{=}\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\mu}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}),

(162)

where we use Equation
123
in (ii).
Since
f^{*}
is a bijection on the space of policies, it is

\{f^{*}(\mu_{j}\otimes\mu_{-j})\mid\mu_{-j}\in\Delta(\Pi_{-j}^{D})\}=\{(f^{*}\mu)_{i}\otimes\hat{\mu}_{-i}\mid\hat{\mu}_{-i}\in\Delta(\Pi_{-i}^{E})\}.

Hence, (
162
) implies that there is some
\mu_{-j}\in\Delta(\Pi_{-j}^{D})
with independent local policies such that
\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{j}\otimes\mu_{-j}}(\overline{A\!O}_{j,t}=f^{-1}\tau_{i,t})>0
if and only if there is
\hat{\mu}_{-i}\in\Delta(\Pi_{-i}^{E})
with independent local policies such that
\mathbb{P}_{(f^{*}\mu)_{i}\otimes\hat{\mu}_{-i}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})>0
.

Using this fact, it suffices to distinguish the two cases where such a distribution does exist and where it does not exist. First, assume that it does not exist. Then by Definition
58
, both
\pi_{j}^{\mu_{j}}(\cdot\mid f^{-1}\tau_{i,t})
and
\pi_{i}^{(f^{*}\mu)_{i}}(\cdot\mid\tau_{i,t})
are uniform distributions. Since
f^{-1}
is a bijection on
\mathcal{A}_{i}^{E}
, also
\pi_{j}^{\mu_{j}}(f^{-1}\cdot\mid f^{-1}\tau_{i,t})
is a uniform distribution, and hence

\pi_{j}^{\mu_{j}}(f^{-1}\cdot\mid f^{-1}\tau_{i,t})=\pi_{i}^{(f^{*}\mu)_{i}}(\cdot\mid\tau_{i,t}).

Second, consider the case in which a distribution
\mu_{-j}\in\Delta(\Pi_{-j}^{D})
with independent local policies exists such that

\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{j}\otimes\mu_{-j}}(\overline{A\!O}_{j,t}=f^{-1}\tau_{i,t})>0,

and define
\mu\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mu_{j}\otimes\mu_{-j}
.
Then for
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}^{E}_{i}
, it is

\displaystyle\pi^{\mu_{j}}_{j}(f^{-1}a_{i}\mid f^{-1}\tau_{i,t})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{j,t}=f^{-1}a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{j,t}=f^{-1}\tau_{i,t})

(163)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{j,t}=f^{-1}a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{j,t},Z)\mid\overline{A\!O}_{j,t}=f^{-1}\tau_{i,t}]

(164)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[Z_{j}(f^{-1}a_{i}\mid f^{-1}\tau_{i,t})\mid\overline{A\!O}_{j,t}=f^{-1}\tau_{i,t}]

(165)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\Pi^{D}}\int_{\Omega}(f^{*}Z)_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})\mathds{1}_{\overline{A\!O}_{j,t}={f^{-1}}\tau_{i,t}}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{Z}\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{\Pi^{D}}\int_{\Omega}\mathds{1}_{\overline{A\!O}_{j,t}={f^{-1}}\tau_{i,t}}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{Z}\mathrm{d}\mu}

(166)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\Pi^{D}}(f^{*}Z)_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})\mathbb{P}_{Z}({\overline{A\!O}_{j,t}={f^{-1}}\tau_{i,t}})\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{\Pi^{D}}\mathbb{P}_{Z}(\overline{A\!O}_{j,t}={f^{-1}}\tau_{i,t})\mathrm{d}\mu}

(167)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\Pi^{D}}(f^{*}Z)_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}Z}({\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}})\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{\Pi^{D}}\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}Z}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})\mathrm{d}\mu}

(168)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\Pi^{E}}Z_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})\mathbb{P}_{Z}({\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}})\mathrm{d}\mu\circ(f^{*})^{-1}}{\int_{\Pi^{E}}\mathbb{P}_{Z}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})\mathrm{d}\mu\circ(f^{*})^{-1}}

(169)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{f^{*}\mu}[Z_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}]

(170)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{f^{*}\mu}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})

(171)

\displaystyle=\pi_{i}^{(f^{*}\mu)_{i}}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t}),

(172)

where we have used Definition
58
in (
163
) and (
172
), and Theorem
22
in (
168
).
This concludes the second case and thus the proof.

∎

D.3
The other-play distribution and the symmetrizer

Using the idea of a policy corresponding to a distributions over policies introduced above, we can now define a policy corresponding to the distribution over policies used in the OP objective. In the following, fix again a Dec-POMDP
D
.

Definition 62

(Other-play distribution)
.

Let
\pi\in\Pi
. We define the
OP distribution
of
\pi

as the distribution

\mu\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}\delta_{\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi},

(173)

where
\delta
is the Dirac measure, i.e., for any measurable set
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\Pi
, it is

\delta_{\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi}(\mathcal{Z})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi\in\mathcal{Z}\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Intuitively, agent
i
chooses one of the automorphisms
\mathbf{g}_{i}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)

uniformly at random in the beginning of an episode and then follows the local policy
\mathrm{proj}_{i}(\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}\pi)
.
It can easily be shown that this distribution has independent local policies.

Lemma 63
.

Let
\pi\in\Pi
and let
\mu
be the OP distribution of
\pi
. Then
\mu
has independent local policies.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\subseteq\Pi_{i}
measurable for
i\in\mathcal{N}
and let
\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{Z}_{i}
. Note that for any
i\in\mathcal{N}
and
\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}
, it is

\delta_{\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi}(\mathrm{proj}_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}))=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\{i\}}\delta_{\mathrm{proj}_{j}(\mathbf{g}^{*}_{j}\pi)}(\Pi_{j})\delta_{\mathrm{proj}_{i}(\mathbf{g}^{*}_{i}\pi)}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})=\delta_{\mathrm{proj}_{i}(\mathbf{g}^{*}_{i}\pi)}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}).

(174)

Hence, it follows that

\mu(\mathcal{Z})=|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}\sum_{\mathbf{g}_{i}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}\delta_{\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi}(\mathcal{Z})=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{g}_{i}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)}|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-1}\delta_{\mathrm{proj}_{i}(\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}\pi)}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}))\\
=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-(N-1)}|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-1}\delta_{\mathrm{proj}_{i}(\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}\pi)}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}))\\
\overset{(\ref{eq:300})}{=}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}\delta_{\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi}(\mathrm{proj}_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}))=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\mu_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}).

(175)

This shows that
\mu=\otimes_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\mu_{i}
.
∎

Using the OP distribution of a policy, we can define the symmetrizer
\Psi^{D}
for
D
, which maps a policy
\pi
to a policy
\Psi^{D}(\pi)
that corresponds to the OP distribution of
\pi
. If it is clear which Dec-POMDP is considered, we also write
\Psi(\pi)
.

Definition 64

(Symmetrizer)
.

We define the
symmetrizer
for the Dec-POMDP
D
as the map
\Psi^{D}\colon\Pi^{D}\rightarrow\Pi^{D}
such that for any policy
\pi\in\Pi
and OP distribution

\mu
of
\pi
, it is

\Psi^{D}(\pi)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\pi^{\mu}.

It is clear that if a policy is already invariant to automorphism, then
\pi_{i}=\Psi_{i}(\pi)
for
i\in\mathcal{N}
, excluding action-observation histories that can never be reached under
\pi_{i}
. We formulate a slightly weaker proposition below, which is easier to prove.

Proposition 65
.

Let
\pi\in\Pi
be invariant to automorphism, and assume that, for all
t\in\{0,\dotsc,T\}
and
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i,t}
, it is
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})>0
. Then it is
\Psi(\pi)=\pi
.

Proof.

Let
\mu
be the OP distribution of
\pi
. Since
\pi
is invariant to automorphism, it is

\mu=|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}\delta_{\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi}=\delta_{\pi}.

Now let
i\in\mathcal{N}
,
t\in\{0,\dotsc,T\}
,
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\tau_{i,t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A\!O}}_{i,t}
arbitrary.
Using Equation
123
, it follows that

\mathbb{P}_{\delta_{\pi}}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})\overset{(\ref{eq:equation-tower-prop-z})}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\delta_{\pi}}\left[\mathbb{P}_{Z}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})\right]=\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})>0

and

\mathbb{P}_{\delta_{\pi}}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})\overset{(\ref{eq:equation-tower-prop-z})}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\delta_{\pi}}\left[\mathbb{P}_{Z}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t})\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}\right]\\
=\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t}).

(176)

Hence, we can apply Definition
58
and conclude that

\Psi_{i}(\pi)(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})=\pi^{\mu_{i}}_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t})\overset{\text{Definition~\ref{policy-mixture}}}{=}\mathbb{P}_{\delta_{\pi}}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})\\
\overset{(\ref{eq:40})}{=}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(A_{i,t}=a_{i}\mid\overline{A\!O}_{i,t}=\tau_{i,t})=\pi_{i}(a_{i}\mid\tau_{i,t}).

(177)

∎

It will be helpful to refer to policies as equivalent if they have the same image under
\Psi
.

Definition 66
.

Let
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{D}
. We say that
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are equivalent, denoted as
\pi\equiv_{D}\pi
, if
\Psi^{D}(\pi)=\Psi^{D}(\pi^{\prime})
. Moreover, we write
[\pi]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{\pi^{\prime}\mid\pi^{\prime}\equiv_{D}\pi\}
for the equivalence class of
\pi
.

It is clear that
\equiv_{D}
is an equivalence relation, since it is induced by the function
\Psi^{D}
. It follows that under
\equiv_{D}
,
\Pi^{D}
decomposes into a partition of equivalence classes, denoted by
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\Pi^{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\displaystyle{\equiv_{D}}$}}{\raisebox{4.20613pt}{$\textstyle{\Pi^{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\textstyle{\equiv_{D}}$}}{\raisebox{3.21724pt}{$\scriptstyle{\Pi^{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.72855pt}{$\scriptstyle{\equiv_{D}}$}}{\raisebox{2.43056pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\Pi^{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.287pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\equiv_{D}}$}}}
.

Applying Lemma
61
to the symmetrizer in particular, we can show that it commutes with isomorphisms, and that the policy
\Psi(\pi)
is invariant to automorphism.

Corollary 67
.

Let
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
and
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
. Then it is

f^{*}\Psi^{D}(\pi)=\Psi^{E}(f^{*}\pi).

If
E=D
, then

f^{*}\Psi^{D}(\pi)=\Psi^{D}(\pi),

i.e.,
\Psi^{D}(\pi)
is invariant to automorphism.

Proof.

Let
\mu
be the other-play distribution of
\pi
and
\hat{\mu}
the other-play distribution corresponding to
f^{*}\pi
.
Then, using the associativity of function composition and pushforward proven in Lemma
21
, and using the “in particular” part of Lemma
19
, it follows that

\hat{\mu}=|\mathrm{Aut}(E)|^{-N}\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(E)^{\mathcal{N}}}\delta_{\mathbf{g}^{*}(f^{*}\pi)}=|\mathrm{Aut}(E)|^{-N}\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(E)^{\mathcal{N}}}\delta_{(\mathrm{proj}_{i}(\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}f^{*}\pi))_{i\in\mathcal{N}}}\\
\overset{\text{Lemma~\ref{decompose-isomorphisms}}}{=}|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}\delta_{(\mathrm{proj}_{i}(f^{*}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}\pi))_{i\in\mathcal{N}}}=|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}\delta_{f^{*}(\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi)}\\
=|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}\delta_{\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi}\circ(f^{*})^{-1}=\mu\circ(f^{*})^{-1}.

(178)

Thus, using Lemma
61
, it is

f^{*}\Psi^{D}(\pi)=f^{*}\pi^{\mu}\overset{\text{Lemma~\ref{lemma-isomorphism-mixture-commute}}}{=}\pi^{f^{*}\mu}=\pi^{\mu\circ(f^{*})^{-1}}\overset{(\ref{equation-image-measure-pull-back})}{=}\pi^{\hat{\mu}}=\Psi^{E}(f^{*}\pi)

(179)

Finally, assume
E=D
. Then
f
is an automorphism and
\mathrm{Aut}(E)=\mathrm{Aut}(D)=\mathrm{Aut}(D)\circ f
by Lemma
19
. Hence,

\mu\circ(f^{*})^{-1}=|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}\delta_{\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi}\circ(f^{*})^{-1}\\
=|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}\delta_{(\mathrm{proj}_{i}(f^{*}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}\pi))_{i\in\mathcal{N}}}\\
\overset{\text{Lemma~\ref{decompose-isomorphisms}}}{=}|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}\delta_{\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi}=\mu.

(180)

By (
179
), it follows that

f^{*}\Psi^{D}(\pi)\overset{(\ref{equation-psi-isom-commute})}{=}\pi^{\mu\circ(f^{*})^{-1}}\overset{(\ref{equation-image-measure-pull-back-auto})}{=}\pi^{\mu}=\Psi^{D}(\pi),

which concludes the proof.
∎

A direct corollary is that we can define the pushforward purely in terms of equivalence classes of policies. This will also be useful later.

Definition 68
.

Let
D,E
be isomorphic Dec-POMDPs with
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
. Let
[\pi]\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\Pi^{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\displaystyle{\equiv_{D}}$}}{\raisebox{4.20613pt}{$\textstyle{\Pi^{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\textstyle{\equiv_{D}}$}}{\raisebox{3.21724pt}{$\scriptstyle{\Pi^{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.72855pt}{$\scriptstyle{\equiv_{D}}$}}{\raisebox{2.43056pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\Pi^{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.287pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\equiv_{D}}$}}}
. We define the pushforward equivalence class
f^{*}[\pi]\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{\Pi_{E}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\displaystyle{\equiv_{E}}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{\Pi_{E}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\textstyle{\equiv_{E}}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{\Pi_{E}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.72855pt}{$\scriptstyle{\equiv_{E}}$}}{\raisebox{1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\Pi_{E}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.287pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\equiv_{E}}$}}}
via

f^{*}[\pi]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=[f^{*}\pi].

The following corollary show that this is well-defined, that the pushforward of an equivalence class does not depend on the particular chosen isomorphism, and that it is compatible with function composition.

Corollary 69
.

(i)

The pushforward of an equivalence class is well-defined, i.e., for any
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{D}
such that
\pi\equiv_{D}\pi^{\prime}
, it is
[f^{*}\pi]=[f^{*}\pi^{\prime}]
.

(ii)

Any two isomorphisms
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
induce the same pushforward.

(iii)

Analogous results to those in Lemma
21
apply to the pushforward of equivalence classes.

Proof.

First, let
\pi\equiv_{D}\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{D}
and
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
arbitrary. Then, using Corollary
67
and the definition of
\equiv_{D}
, it is

\Psi^{E}(f^{*}\pi)=f^{*}\Psi^{D}(\pi)=f^{*}\Psi^{D}(\pi^{\prime})=\Psi^{E}(f^{*}\pi^{\prime}).

Thus,
[f^{*}\pi]=[f^{*}\pi^{\prime}]
, which proves the first part.

Second, let
f,\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
and
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
arbitrary. By Lemma
19
, there then exists
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(E)
such that
\tilde{f}=g\circ f
. Hence, using the second and first part of Corollary
67
and Lemma
21
, it is

\Psi^{E}(f^{*}\pi)\overset{\text{Corollary~\ref{corollary-psi-isom-commute}}}{=}g^{*}\Psi^{E}(f^{*}\pi)\overset{\text{Corollary~\ref{corollary-psi-isom-commute}}}{=}\Psi^{E}(g^{*}(f^{*}\pi))\overset{\text{Lemma~\ref{lemma-pull-back-function-composition-compatible}}}{=}\Psi^{E}((g\circ f)^{*}\pi)=\Psi^{E}(\hat{f}^{*}\pi).

(181)

Finally, it follows that

f^{*}[\pi]=[f^{*}\pi]\overset{\ref{eq:340}}{=}[\tilde{f}^{*}\pi]=\tilde{f}^{*}[\pi],

which concludes the second part.

The third part follows directly from Lemma
21
by using the definition of the pushforward of equivalence classes.
∎

In the following, we say that two equivalence classes
[\pi],[\pi^{\prime}]
for
\pi\in\Pi^{D},\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{E}
correspond to each other if there exists an isomorphism
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
such that
f^{*}[\pi]=[\pi^{\prime}]
. In that case, in a slight abuse of the terms, we also say that
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are equivalent, extending the equivalence between policies defined above to policies for different Dec-POMDPs. Using Corollary
69
, one can see that two policies
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{D}
are equivalent in the sense that
[\pi]=[\pi^{\prime}]
if and only if there exists an isomorphism
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,D)
such that
f^{*}[\pi]=[\pi^{\prime}]
, so this extended notion is equivalent to the old one for two policies
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{D}
. We continue to reserve the notation
[\pi]
and
\equiv
for policies from the same Dec-POMDP.

D.4
Main characterizations

Having defined the symmetrizer
\Psi
, we can now characterize the OP objective as transforming a policy
\pi
into an invariant policy
\Psi(\pi)
and evaluating the expected return of that policy.
This means that we can “pass to the quotient” and consider the OP objective as a map
\tilde{J}_{\mathrm{OP}}
of equivalence classes,
\tilde{J}_{\mathrm{OP}}([\pi])\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)
for
[\pi]\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{\Pi}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\displaystyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{\Pi}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\textstyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{\Pi}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.72855pt}{$\scriptstyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\Pi}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.287pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\equiv}$}}}
, using the equivalence relation on policies introduced above. The result is essentially a rigorous version of
Hu et al. 2020
’s Proposition 1 in our setup. It will help us later to analyze the OP-optimal policies in a given example, as it implies that we can restrict ourselves to considering representatives
\Psi(\pi)
of equivalence classes.

Theorem 70
.

Let
D
be a Dec-POMDP, let
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
, and let
\Psi
be the symmetrizer for
D
. Then
\Psi(\pi)
is invariant to automorphism, and it is

J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)=J^{D}(\Psi(\pi)).

(182)

In particular, we can consider the OP objective as a function of equivalence classes
[\pi]\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\Pi^{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\displaystyle{\equiv_{D}}$}}{\raisebox{4.20613pt}{$\textstyle{\Pi^{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\textstyle{\equiv_{D}}$}}{\raisebox{3.21724pt}{$\scriptstyle{\Pi^{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.72855pt}{$\scriptstyle{\equiv_{D}}$}}{\raisebox{2.43056pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\Pi^{D}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.287pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\equiv_{D}}$}}}
, and if there exists an optimal policy for the OP objective, then there also exists an optimal policy that is invariant to automorphism.

Proof.

In the following, fix a Dec-POMDP
D
.
Let
\pi\in\Pi
and let
\mu
be the OP distribution of
\pi
, such that
\Psi(\pi)=\pi^{\mu}
. Then by the second part of Corollary
67
,
\Psi(\pi)
is invariant to automorphism.
Moreover, using Proposition
60
, it is

J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[J(\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi)\right]=\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}J(\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi)\\
=\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}\int_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi}J(\pi^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\left(\delta_{\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi}\right)\\
=\int_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi}J(\pi^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}\delta_{\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi}\right)\\
\overset{(\ref{equation-other-play-mixture})}{=}\int_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi}J(\pi^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mu\overset{(\ref{expected-return-mixture})}{=}J(\mu)\overset{\text{Proposition~\ref{mixture-lemma}}}{=}J(\Psi(\pi)),

(183)

which proves Equation
182
.

Turning to the “in particular” statement, let
\pi^{\prime}\equiv\pi
for a second policy
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi
.
By Definition
66
, this means that
\Psi(\pi^{\prime})=\Psi(\pi)
.
Hence, by Equation
183
, it follows that
J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)=J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi^{\prime})
, which shows that the function
\tilde{J}_{\mathrm{OP}}\colon{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{\Pi}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\displaystyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{\Pi}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\textstyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{\Pi}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.72855pt}{$\scriptstyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\Pi}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.287pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\equiv}$}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},[\pi]\mapsto J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)
is well-defined.

Lastly, assume that there is
\pi\in\argmax_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi}J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi^{\prime})
(see Remark
53
regarding the existence of such a policy). Then by Equation
183
, it is also
\Psi(\pi)\in\argmax_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi}J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi^{\prime})
, so
\Psi(\pi)
is an OP-optimal policy that is invariant to automorphism.
∎

As a corollary, we can show that isomorphisms do not affect the OP value of a policy (we already know this about SP from Corollary
23
). In the following, we define
\Pi_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)
for a Dec-POMDP
D
.

Corollary 71
.

Let
D
,
E
be isomorphic Dec-POMDPs with
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
, and let
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
. Then it is

J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}(\pi)=J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{E}(f^{*}\pi).

In particular, if
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}
, then also
f^{*}\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{OP}}^{E}
.

Proof.

Using Theorem
70
, Corollary
67
, and Corollary
23
, it is

J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{E}(f^{*}\pi)\overset{\text{Theorem~\ref{thm-op-mixture}}}{=}J^{E}(\Psi^{E}(f^{*}\pi))\overset{\text{Corollary~\ref{corollary-psi-isom-commute}}}{=}J^{E}(f^{*}\Psi^{D}(\pi))\\
\overset{\text{Corollary~\ref{cor-sp-invariant-to-pullback}}}{=}J^{D}(\Psi^{D}(\pi))\overset{\text{Theorem~\ref{thm-op-mixture}}}{=}J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}(\pi).

(184)

Turning to the “in particular” statement, assume that
\pi\in\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}
. By Lemma
18
, it is
f^{-1}\in\mathrm{Iso}(E,D)
. Hence, for any
\tilde{\pi}\in\Pi^{E}
, it follows from the preceding that

J^{E}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\tilde{\pi})=J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}((f^{-1})^{*}\tilde{\pi})\leq J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)=J^{E}_{\mathrm{OP}}(f^{*}\pi).

(185)

This shows that
f^{*}\pi\in\Pi^{E}_{\mathrm{OP}}
.
∎

Finally, we turn to the connection between OP and the payoff in an LFC game.
The following result will be helpful in both showing the inadequacy of OP and in proving that OP with tie-breaking is optimal. It shows that equivalent policies in
[\pi]\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{\Pi}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\displaystyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{\Pi}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\textstyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{\Pi}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.72855pt}{$\scriptstyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\Pi}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.287pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\equiv}$}}}
are all compatible when played against each other by different principals in the LFC game. The proof is based on Lemma
41
and Proposition
60
.

Theorem 72
.

Let
D
be a Dec-POMDP, let
\Psi
be the symmetrizer for
D
, and define
{{\mathcal{D}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{f^{*}D\mid f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)\}}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
.
For any
E\in{\mathcal{D}}
, choose
f_{D,E}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
arbitrarily. Then it is

U^{D}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})=\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim U({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim f_{D_{j},D}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}(D_{j}),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}\left(\left(\Psi_{k}(\pi^{(k)})\right)_{k\in\mathcal{N}}\right)\right]\right].

(186)

Proof.

First, consider arbitrary joint policies
\pi^{(1)},\dots,\pi^{(N)}\in\Pi^{D}
and let
\mu^{(i)}
be the OP distribution of
\pi^{(i)}
, so that
\pi^{\mu^{(i)}}=\Psi(\pi^{(i)})
for
i\in\mathcal{N}
. Define the distribution

\hat{\mu}(\pi^{(1)},\dotsc,\pi^{(N)})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}\otimes_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\delta_{\mathrm{proj}_{i}(\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}\pi^{(i)})}

(187)

as a function of
\pi^{(1)},\dotsc,\pi^{(N)}
.
It can easily be seen that
\hat{\mu}_{(}\pi^{(1)},\dotsc,\pi^{(N)})\in\Delta(\Pi^{D})
and that it has independent local policies. Moreover,
\hat{\mu}(\pi^{(1)},\dotsc,\pi^{(N)})_{i}=\mu^{(i)}_{i}
, i.e., the marginal distribution for agent
i\in\mathcal{N}
is equal in
\hat{\mu}(\pi^{(1)},\dotsc,\pi^{(N)})
and
\mu^{(i)}
. Hence, also the corresponding local policies are identical, that is,

\pi_{i}^{\hat{\mu}(\pi^{(1)},\dotsc,\pi^{(N)})_{i}}=\pi^{\mu^{(i)}_{i}}_{i}=\Psi_{i}(\pi^{(i)})

(188)

for
i\in\mathcal{N}
.

It follows that

\displaystyle U^{D}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N})

(189)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim U({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim f_{D_{j},D}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}(D_{j}),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}\left[J^{D}((\mathrm{proj}_{k}(\mathbf{g}_{k}^{*}\pi^{(k)}))_{k\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]\right]

(190)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim U({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim f_{D_{j},D}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}(D_{j}),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\Bigg[

(191)

\displaystyle\quad\quad|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}\int_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}(\pi)\mathrm{d}\left(\otimes_{k\in\mathcal{N}}\delta_{\mathrm{proj}_{k}(\mathbf{g}_{k}^{*}\pi^{(k)})}\right)\Bigg]\Bigg]

(192)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim U({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim f_{D_{j},D}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}(D_{j}),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\Bigg[

(193)
