\displaystyle\quad\quad\int_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}(\pi)\mathrm{d}\left(|\mathrm{Aut}(D)|^{-N}\sum_{\mathbf{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}\otimes_{k\in\mathcal{N}}\delta_{\mathrm{proj}_{k}(\mathbf{g}_{k}^{*}\pi^{(k)})}\right)\Bigg]\Bigg]

(194)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:37})}{=}\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim U({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim f_{D_{j},D}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}(D_{j}),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\int_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}(\pi)\mathrm{d}\hat{\mu}(\pi^{(1)},\dotsc,\pi^{(N)})\right]\right]

(195)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{expected-return-mixture})}{=}\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim U({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim f_{D_{j},D}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}(D_{j}),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}(\hat{\mu}(\pi^{(1)},\dotsc,\pi^{(N)}))\right]\right]

(196)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim U({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim f_{D_{j},D}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}(D_{j}),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}((\pi_{k}^{\hat{\mu}(\pi^{(1)},\dotsc,\pi^{(N)})_{k}})_{k\in\mathcal{N}})\right]\right]

(197)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{equation-mixture-hat-equal-psi})}{=}\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim U({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim f_{D_{j},D}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}(D_{j}),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}\left(\left(\Psi_{k}(\pi^{(k)})\right)_{k\in\mathcal{N}}\right)\right]\right],

(198)

where we have used
Lemma
41
in (
190
) and Proposition
60
in (
197
).
This concludes the proof.

∎

Based on this result, the LFC game for
D
can be understood in the following way. Principal
i\in\mathcal{N}
observes a randomly relabeled problem
D_{i}\in{\mathcal{D}}
and trains a joint policy
\pi^{(i)}\sim\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(D_{i})
on this problem. The resulting policy
\pi^{(i)}
is then translated back into a policy
f_{D_{i},D}^{*}\pi^{(i)}
for the original problem, using any isomorphism
f_{D_{i},D}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D_{i},D)
. Finally, this joint policy is made invariant to automorphism by applying the symmetrizer, and agent
i
in the original problem
D
is assigned the local policy
\Psi_{i}(f_{D_{i},D}^{*}\pi^{(i)})
.

D.5
Other-play is not self-play in a different Dec-POMDP

Hu et al. 2020
show that the OP objective
\tilde{J}^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}
introduced by them can be understood of as the SP objective in a special Dec-POMDP. That is, for every Dec-POMDP
D
, there is a second Dec-POMDP
E
with
\Pi^{E}=\Pi^{D}
such that for any
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
, it is
\tilde{J}^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)=\max_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{D}}\tilde{J}^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi^{\prime})
if and only if
J^{E}(\pi)=\max_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{E}}J^{E}(\pi^{\prime})
.
Interestingly, when including player permutations, this is not the case anymore. We will prove this here, using the characterization of OP from the last section.

Intuitively, if agents are symmetric, then under OP, they will always act according to the same local policy in the environment. In some Dec-POMDPs, this means that it is optimal for the agents to randomize their actions, to end up with different actions some of the time. For instance, consider the following game:

Example 73
.

There are two players with two actions
\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{a_{i,1},a_{i,2}\}
for
i=1,2
each, and an episode lasts only one step, making this a simple normal-form game. Rewards for each joint action are displayed in Table
6
.

Table 6
:
Rewards for each joint action in Example
73
.

a_{2,1}

a_{2,2}

a_{1,1}

-\frac{1}{2}

1

a_{1,2}

1

-1

This example demonstrates that sometimes there does not exist a deterministic policy that is optimal under the OP objective.

Lemma 74
.

In Example
73
, for any deterministic policy
\pi
, it is
J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)<\max_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{D}}J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
R\in\mathbb{R}^{2,2}
denote a matrix containing rewards as in Table
6
.

First, note that in this game, players are symmetric, but actions are not. Moreover, there are no observations and only one state. Hence,
\mathrm{Aut}(D)=\{g,e\}
where
g_{N}1=2,g_{N}2=1
and
e
is the identity.

Now consider any deterministic policy
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
, corresponding to two vectors of action-probabilities

x,y\in\left\{\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\right\}

for the two players. Due to the symmetry of both players, the OP distribution
\mu
of
\pi
assigns each policy
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
to either player with probability
\frac{1}{2}
, so in
\Psi(\pi)=\pi^{\mu}
, both players play the distribution
z\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{2}x+\frac{1}{2}y
and receive a reward of

J^{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)\overset{\text{Theorem~\ref{thm-op-mixture}}}{=}J(\Psi(\pi))=z^{\top}Rz.

It follows by the definition of
x,y
that

z\in\left\{\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\\
\frac{1}{2}\end{bmatrix}\right\}.

Clearly, then
z^{\top}Rz
is maximized at
z=[\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]^{\top}
, yielding a reward of

J^{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)=z^{\top}Rz=\frac{1}{8}.

(199)

Next, define
x=[\frac{4}{7},\frac{3}{7}]^{\top}
and let
\pi^{*}
be a policy such that
\pi^{*}_{1}=\pi^{*}_{2}
and the two action-probabilities of both local policies are given by the vector
x
. Note that since
g^{*}\pi^{*}=(\pi^{*}_{gi})_{i=1,2}=\pi^{*}
, it follows from the above that
\pi^{*}
is invariant to automorphism.

It follows by Proposition
56
that we can evaluate the OP value of
\pi^{*}
by evaluating its expected return. That is,

J^{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi^{*})\overset{\text{Proposition~\ref{prop-invariant-policy-self-play-value-equals-other-play-value}}}{=}J(\pi^{*})=x^{\top}Rx=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{4}{7}\right)^{2}+2\frac{4}{7}\frac{3}{7}-1\left(\frac{3}{7}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{7}.

(200)

It follows that

J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)\overset{(\ref{eq:510})}{=}\frac{1}{8}<\frac{1}{7}=J^{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi^{*})\leq\max_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{D}}J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi^{\prime}),

(201)

which concludes the proof.
∎

The fact that there is no deterministic optimal policy in Example
73
is in conflict with a canonical result about Dec-POMDPs.

Theorem 75

(
Oliehoek et al. 2008
,
Oliehoek et al. 2008
, sec. 2.4.4)
.

In every Dec-POMDP
D
, there is a deterministic policy
\pi\in(\Pi^{0})^{D}
such that
J^{D}(\pi)=\max_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}(\pi^{\prime})
.

As a result, we can prove the following.

Proposition 76
.

There exists a Dec-POMDP
D
such that for any other Dec-POMDP
E
with
\Pi^{E}=\Pi^{D}
, there exists a policy
\pi\in\Pi^{E}
that is optimal for the SP objective of
E
, but not optimal for the OP objective of
D
.

Proof.

Let
D
be the Dec-POMDP as described in Example
73
. Assume, towards a contradiction, that there exists a Dec-POMDP
E
with
\Pi^{E}=\Pi^{D}
such that any optimal policy in that Dec-POMDP is optimal under the OP objective of
D
. Then by Theorem
75
, there exists a deterministic policy
\pi\in(\Pi^{0})^{E}
such that
J^{E}(\pi)
is maximal. Hence, by the assumption, also
J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)
is maximized. But by Lemma
74
, it must be
\max_{\tilde{\pi}\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\tilde{\pi})>J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)
. This is a contradiction, which means that
D
is an example of a Dec-POMDP that has the desired properties.
∎

This shows that to optimize the OP objective, we have to directly consider that objective and we cannot simply apply an RL algorithm to a different Dec-POMDP. Also, the fact that we need stochastic policies means that it is not immediately clear how to apply a Bellman equation to the objective.

Appendix E
Other-play is not optimal in the label-free coordination problem

In this section, our goal is to prove a rigorous version of Theorem
7
from the main text.

Recall that in Appendix
D
, we introduced the symmetrizer
\Psi\colon\Pi\rightarrow\Pi
, which maps a joint policy
\pi
to the policy corresponding to agents following randomly permuted local policies
(\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}\pi)_{i}
where
\mathbf{g}_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(D))
. This represents the random permutations employed in the OP objective, and hence by Theorem
70
, it is
J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)=J(\Psi(\pi))
, i.e., the OP value of
\pi
is equal to the SP value of
\Psi(\pi)
. Moreover, we defined the equivalence classes
[\pi]=\Psi^{-1}(\{\Psi(\pi)\})
of policies that get mapped to the same policy under
\Psi
.

As defined in Appendix
D.1
, an OP learning algorithm is any learning algorithm such that the policies that it learns achieve optimal OP value in expectation. In particular, it can be a learning algorithm that learns different policies in different training runs, as long as it chooses OP-optimal policies with probability
1
.
As we have seen in Theorem
72
, in an LFC game, it does not matter which policy from an equivalence class
[\pi]
is chosen. Unfortunately, though, there can also be different OP-optimal policies that are not equivalent. In this case, if an OP learning algorithm is not concentrated on only compatible policies, it is not optimal in the corresponding LFC problem.

In the remainder of this section, we will prove this statement. Concretely, in Appendix
E.1
, we will recall the two policies
\pi^{R},\pi^{S}
in the two-stage lever game that we introduced in Section
4.4
. We will show that both are optimal under OP, but that their cross-play value is inferior to the optimal OP value. In Appendix
E.2
, we will then formally state and prove the result that, if an OP algorithm is not concentrated on only one of the two incompatible equivalence classes of policies
[\pi^{R}],[\pi^{S}]
in the two-stage lever game, then the algorithm is suboptimal in the LFC problem for that game.

E.1
Two incompatible optimal policies in the two-stage lever game

We begin by mapping out the space of OP-optimal policies in the two-stage lever game. Recall that this was a game with two agents, which proceeds in two rounds. Both agents have two actions, and their goal in both rounds is to choose the same action, for a reward of
1
. Failure of coordination leads to a reward of
-1
. Moreover, in the second round, agents observe the actions of the other agent from the first round.
In the following, let
D
stand for the Dec-POMDP associated to this game as described in Example
3
.

Recall the two policies
\pi^{R}
and
\pi^{S}
introduced in Section
4.4
. In both policies, agents randomize uniformly between both levers in the first round. They also both randomize in the second round if coordination was unsuccessful in the first one. If coordination in the first round was successful, there are two different strategies: in
\pi^{R}
, both agents repeat their respective actions from round one. In
\pi^{S}
, both agents switch to the action they did not play in round one.

The following lemma shows that both policies are optimal under OP.

Lemma 77
.

Both
\pi^{R}
and
\pi^{S}
as described above are invariant to automorphism, and they maximize the OP objective, where

J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi^{R})=J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi^{S})=\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)=\frac{1}{2}.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\pi}\in\Pi^{D}
arbitrary and define
\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Psi(\tilde{\pi})
. By
Theorem
70
,
\pi
must be invariant to automorphism, and it must be

J_{\mathrm{OP}}(\tilde{\pi})=J(\pi).

Moreover, if we show that
\pi^{R}
and
\pi^{S}
are invariant to automorphism, then by Proposition
56
, their OP value equals their expected return. Hence, to show that
\pi^{R}
and
\pi^{S}
are optimal, it suffices to show that they are invariant to automorphism, and then compare their expected returns to the expected return of
\pi
.

To begin, consider the set of automorphisms in the game, as described in Example
17
. Let
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
. Recall that the state permutation is the trivial identity map, and the permutations for actions and observations of both players have to be equal,
g_{A_{1}}=g_{A_{2}}=g_{O_{1}}=g_{O_{2}}
. Hence, we can represent
g
as a tuple
(g_{N},g_{A})
. There are four possible combinations of choices for permutations
g_{A},g_{N}\colon\{1,2\}\rightarrow\{1,2\}
. Either permutation can either be the identity map
\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\
1&2\end{pmatrix}
or the inversion
\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\
2&1\end{pmatrix}
.

Now consider
\pi^{R}
and
\pi^{S}
. Note that both policies are symmetric in the agents, so
\pi_{1}^{R}=\pi_{2}^{R}
and
\pi_{1}^{S}=\pi_{2}^{S}
. Invariance to automorphism is trivially fulfilled in the first stage, i.e.,
\pi_{i}^{R}(a_{i}\mid\emptyset)=\pi^{R}_{g^{-1}_{N}i}(g^{-1}_{A}a_{i}\mid\emptyset)
and
\pi_{i}^{S}(a_{i}\mid\emptyset)=\pi^{S}_{g^{-1}_{N}i}(g^{-1}_{A}a_{i}\mid\emptyset)
for any
i\in\{1,2\},a_{i}\in\{1,2\}
, since both agents randomize uniformly between actions. Now consider the second stage. Note that since automorphisms for the actions and observations of both players have to be identical, an automorphism can never map an action-observation history with
O_{i,1}\neq A_{i,0}
, i.e., such that one’s own action and the observed action from the other agent differ, onto one in which they are the same, and vice versa. So we can consider the condition of invariance to automorphism separately for the case in which players achieved coordination in the first stage and for the case where they did not.

In the latter case, players randomize their actions uniformly, so in this case the policy is also trivially invariant. Now consider the former case, i.e., for
i=1,2
, it is either
\overline{A\!O}_{i,1}=(1,1)
or
\overline{A\!O}_{i,1}=(2,2)
. If both players repeat their action, then for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
a_{i}=o_{i}\in\{1,2\}
, and any automorphism
g=(g_{N},g_{A})
, it is

\pi^{R}_{i}(a_{i}\mid a_{i},o_{i})=1=\pi^{R}_{g^{-1}_{N}i}(g_{A}^{-1}a_{i}\mid g^{-1}_{A}a_{i},g_{A}^{-1}o_{i}).

In the case where they change their action, for
a_{i}^{\prime}\in\{1,2\}\setminus\{a_{i}\}
, it is
g_{A}^{-1}a_{i}^{\prime}\neq g_{A}^{-1}a_{i}
, and thus

\pi^{S}_{i}(a_{i}^{\prime}\mid a_{i},o_{i})=1=\pi^{S}_{g^{-1}_{N}i}(g_{A}^{-1}a^{\prime}_{i}\mid g_{A}^{-1}a_{i},g_{A}^{-1}o_{i}).

In conclusion, this shows that
g^{*}\pi^{S}=\pi^{S}
and
g^{*}\pi^{R}=\pi^{R}
.
Both
\pi^{S}
and
\pi^{R}
have an expected return of

J(\pi^{S})=J(\pi^{R})=\mathbb{E}[R_{1}+R_{2}]=\frac{1}{2}(1+1)+\frac{1}{2}(-1+\frac{1}{2}\cdot 1+\frac{1}{2}\cdot(-1))=\frac{1}{2}

i.e., in the first round, they coordinate in half of the cases, in which case they coordinate again, and if they do not coordinate in the first round, they have an equal chance of achieving coordinating or not in the second round.

Now consider
\pi
. First, choosing
g_{N}
as the inversion and
g_{A}
as the identity, it follows that

\pi_{1}\overset{\text{(!)}}{=}\pi_{g_{N}^{-1}1}(g_{A}^{-1}\cdot\mid g_{A}^{-1}\cdot)=\pi_{2}(\cdot\mid\cdot)=\pi_{2},

where in (!) we use that
\pi
is invariant to automorphism. This shows that
\pi
must by symmetric in players, so
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}
. Moreover, choosing
g_{N}
as the identity and
g_{A}
as the inversion, it is

\pi_{i}(1\mid\emptyset)\overset{\text{(!)}}{=}\pi_{g_{N}^{-1}i}(g_{A}^{-1}1\mid\emptyset)=\pi_{i}(2\mid\emptyset),

so under
\pi
, too, both agents must choose both actions with equal probability in the first stage.

Turning to the second stage, we assume that
\pi
receives a maximal reward of
1
in the second stage if both agents coordinated in the first stage. Now consider the case in which coordination failed in the first step. Note that since
\pi
is symmetric in the agents, it is not possible for agents to consistently choose to play either the action of agent
1
or of agent
2
in the second round if those actions did not coincide in the first round. Moreover, due to invariance to action and observation permutations, the probability
p
that an agent repeats their action from the first round must be the same, no matter whether that action was a
1
or
2
, as for
a\neq a^{\prime}\in\{1,2\}
, it must be
\pi_{i}(a\mid a,a^{\prime})=\pi_{i}(a^{\prime}\mid a^{\prime},a)
for
i=1,2
due to invariance to automorphism. Due to the symmetry of agents, that probability must also be the same for both agents. Hence, we can define

p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\pi_{i}(a\mid a,a^{\prime})=1-\pi_{i}(a^{\prime}\mid a,a^{\prime})

for
i=1,2
and
a\neq a^{\prime}\in\{1,2\}
. The return in the second stage in this case is then

\mathbb{E}[R_{2}\mid A_{2,1}\neq A_{1,1}]\\
=1\cdot\bigg(\pi_{1}(a_{1,1}\mid a_{1,1},a_{2,1})\pi_{2}(a_{1,1}\mid a_{2,1},a_{1,1})+\pi_{1}(a_{2,1}\mid a_{1,1},a_{2,1})\pi_{2}(a_{2,1}\mid a_{2,1},a_{1,1})\bigg)\\
-1\cdot\bigg(\pi_{1}(a_{1,1}\mid a_{1,1},a_{2,1})\pi_{2}(a_{2,1}\mid a_{2,1},a_{1,1})+\pi_{1}(a_{2,1}\mid a_{1,1},a_{2,1})\pi_{2}(a_{1,1}\mid a_{2,1},a_{1,1})\bigg)\\
=1\cdot\bigg(p(1-p)+(1-p)p\bigg)-1\cdot\bigg(pp+(1-p)(1-p)\bigg)=0,

(202)

where
a_{1,1}\neq a_{2,1}\in\{1,2\}
are arbitrary.
This shows that also

J(\pi)\leq\frac{1}{2}(1+1)+\frac{1}{2}(-1+0)=\frac{1}{2}.

Thus, it follows that
J(\pi)\leq\frac{1}{2}=J(\pi^{R})=J(\pi^{S})
, which shows that
\pi^{R},\pi^{S}
are both optimal and thus concludes the proof.
∎

Now consider the case in which one agent chooses a local policy from
\pi^{R}
and another agent chooses a local policy from
\pi^{S}
. It is clear that this will yield a suboptimal expected return compared with
\pi^{R}
or
\pi^{S}
, as agents will always fail to coordinate in the second round, if they coordinated in the first round.

Lemma 78
.

J(\pi^{R}_{1},\pi^{S}_{2})=J(\pi^{S}_{1},\pi^{R}_{2})=-\frac{1}{2}<\frac{1}{2}.

Proof.

Both policies are equal in the first round, in which there is a 50% chance that agents coordinate. If agents do not coordinate in the first round, then they both randomize uniformly in the second stage. If agents do coordinate in the first round, then agent
1
using
\pi^{R}_{1}
repeats their action from the first round, while agent
2
, using
\pi^{S}_{2}
, will switch to a different action. In this case, the reward is thus always
-1
. As a result, it is

Jwe(\pi_{1}^{R},\pi_{2}^{S})=\frac{1}{2}(1-1)+\frac{1}{2}(-1+\frac{1}{2}\cdot 1+\frac{1}{2}\cdot(-1))=-\frac{1}{2}<\frac{1}{2}.

The same argument applies if agent
1
uses
\pi^{S}_{1}
and agent
2
uses
\pi^{R}_{2}
.
∎

E.2
Proof that other-play is suboptimal

Using the two lemmas from the last section, we can now show that if an OP learning algorithm is not concentrated on only one of
[\pi^{S}]
or
[\pi^{R}]
, it is not optimal in the LFC problem for
D
. Note that we could just choose a particular learning algorithm, for instance, one that randomizes uniformly between
\pi^{R}
and
\pi^{S}
, and show that that algorithm is suboptimal. This would then prove that a suboptimal OP learning algorithm exists. However, we show a more general result. Specifically, take any learning algorithm that learns with positive probability a policy equivalent to
\pi^{R}
in one relabeling of
D
and, also with positive probability, a policy equivalent to
\pi^{L}
in some potentially different relabeling. Then this learning algorithm is suboptimal in the LFC problem for
D
.

In the following, let
{\mathcal{D}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{f^{*}D\mid f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)\}
be the set of different relabeled Dec-POMDPs of the two-stage lever game
D
. For any Dec-POMDP
E\in{\mathcal{D}}
, choose
f_{D,E}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
arbitrarily. Below, we define the class of algorithms relevant to our theorem.

Definition 79
.

Let
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
be a learning algorithm such that for any
{E}\in{\mathcal{D}}
,
{E}=f^{*}D
, it is

\sigma({E})=\alpha^{E}\mu^{{E}}+(1-\alpha^{E}){\mu^{\prime}}^{{E}},

where
\alpha_{E}\in[0,1]
and
\mu^{{E}},{\mu^{\prime}}^{{E}}\in\Delta(\Pi_{E})
are chosen such that

{\mu}^{E}(f_{D,E}^{*}[\pi^{R}])=1={\mu^{\prime}}^{E}(f_{D,E}^{*}[\pi^{S}]).

That is,
\sigma(E)
is a mixture of a distribution
\mu^{E}
with weight only on the equivalence class of policies corresponding to
\pi^{R}
, and a distribution
{\mu^{\prime}}^{E}
that only puts weight on policies corresponding to
\pi^{S}
. We say that
\sigma
learns both
[\pi^{R}]
and
[\pi^{S}]
if there exist
{\tilde{D}},{\tilde{D}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}
such that
\alpha_{{\tilde{D}}}>0
and
\alpha_{{\tilde{D}}^{\prime}}<1
.

Note that Corollary
69
(ii) ensures that this definition does not depend on the chosen isomorphisms
f_{D,E}
.

The above condition is very weak: we only require there to be some relabeled Dec-POMDP on which the learning algorithm chooses an equivalent policy of
\pi^{R}
some of the time, and some potentially different relabeled Dec-POMDP where the algorithm chooses a policy equivalent to
\pi^{S}
some of the time. We now show that a learning algorithm with this property is an OP learning algorithm per Definition
54
, but that it is not optimal in the LFC problem for
D
.

Theorem 80
.

In the two-stage lever game, there are two classes of OP-optimal policies, denoted by
[\pi^{R}]
and
[\pi^{S}]
. Any learning algorithm that learns both
[\pi^{R}]
and
[\pi^{S}]
in the sense of Definition
79
is an OP learning algorithm, but it is not optimal in the LFC problem for that game.

Proof.

First, note that under
\pi^{R},\pi^{S}
, all action-observation histories are reached with positive probability, and by Lemma
77
, they are both invariant to automorphism. Hence, by Proposition
65
, it is
\Psi(\pi^{R})=\pi^{R}
and
\Psi(\pi^{S})=\pi^{S}
(*). This also shows that
[\pi^{R}]
and
[\pi^{S}]
are distinct. Let
\sigma
be a learning algorithm as specified in Definition
79
, and define
\mu^{E},{\mu^{\prime}}^{E}
and
\alpha^{E}
for
E\in{\mathcal{D}}
as in that definition. Also, let
{\tilde{D}},{\tilde{D}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}
such that
\alpha_{\tilde{D}}>0
and
\alpha_{{\tilde{D}}^{\prime}}<1
.

We begin by showing that
\sigma
is an OP learning algorithm.
For any
{E}\in{\mathcal{D}}
, it is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim\sigma({E})}[J^{E}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)]

\displaystyle=\alpha_{E}\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim\mu^{{E}}}[J^{E}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)]+(1-\alpha_{E})\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim{\mu^{\prime}}^{{E}}}[J^{E}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)]

(203)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{E}\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim\mu^{{E}}}[\mathds{1}_{f_{D,E}^{*}[\pi^{R}]}J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{E}(\pi))]+(1-\alpha_{E})\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim{\mu^{\prime}}^{{E}}}[\mathds{1}_{f_{D,E}^{*}[\pi^{S}]}J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{E}(\pi)]

(204)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{E}\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim\mu^{{E}}}[\mathds{1}_{f_{D,E}^{*}[\pi^{R}]}J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}(f_{E,D}^{*}\pi))]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(1-\alpha_{E})\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim{\mu^{\prime}}^{{E}}}[\mathds{1}_{f_{D,E}^{*}[\pi^{S}]}J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}(f_{E,D}^{*}\pi)]

(205)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{E}\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim f_{E,D}^{*}\mu^{{E}}}[\mathds{1}_{f_{E,D}^{*}\left(f_{D,E}^{*}[\pi^{R}]\right)}J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}(\pi))]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(1-\alpha_{E})\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim f_{E,D}^{*}{\mu^{\prime}}^{{E}}}[\mathds{1}_{f_{E,D}^{*}\left(f_{D,E}^{*}[\pi^{S}]\right)}J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}(\pi)]

(206)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{E}\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim f_{E,D}^{*}\mu^{{E}}}[\mathds{1}_{[\pi^{R}]}J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}(\pi))]+(1-\alpha_{E})\mathbb{E}_{\pi\sim f_{E,D}^{*}{\mu^{\prime}}^{{E}}}[\mathds{1}_{[\pi^{S}]}J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}(\pi)]

(207)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{E}J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}(\pi^{R})+(1-\alpha_{E})J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}(\pi^{S})

(208)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{E}\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)+(1-\alpha_{E})\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)

(209)

\displaystyle=\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi),

(210)

using Definition
79
in (
203
) and (
204
); Corollary
71
in (
205
); a change of variables for pushforward measures in (
206
);
Lemma
18
and parts (ii) and (iii) of Corollary
69
in (
207
); the “in particular” part of Theorem
70
in (
208
); and Lemma
77
in (
209
).
This shows that
\sigma
is an OP learning algorithm.

Next, we turn to proving that
\sigma
is suboptimal in the LFC problem.
To that end, define the auxiliary function

X(D_{1},D_{2},\pi^{(1)},\pi^{(2)})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\delta_{D_{1},{\tilde{D}}}\delta_{D_{2},{\tilde{D}}^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{[\pi^{R}]}(\pi^{(1)})\mathds{1}_{[\pi^{S}]}(\pi^{(2)})

for any
D_{1},D_{2}\in{\mathcal{D}}
and
\pi^{(1)},\pi^{(2)}\in\Pi^{D}
. This function is
1
whenever the first Dec-POMDP is
{\tilde{D}}
and the policy
\pi^{(1)}
is such that its pushforward policy is in
[\pi^{R}]
, and when the second Dec-POMDP is
{\tilde{D}}^{\prime}
and the pushforward of
\pi^{(2)}
is in
[\pi^{S}]
. Otherwise, it is
0
.

Recall that by Lemma
18
, it is
(f_{E,D}^{*})^{-1}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
for
E\in{\mathcal{D}}
, and by Corollary
69
(ii), the pushforward of an equivalence class does not depend on the particular chosen isomorphism. Define

\beta\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{E}_{D_{1},D_{2}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{D}})}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim f_{D,D_{i}}^{*}\sigma(D_{i}),\,i=1,2}[X(D_{1},D_{2},\pi^{(1)},\pi^{(2)})]]

and note that
\beta\leq 1
.
This number is relevant to us as it is a lower bound on the probability that agents 1 and 2 will have incompatible joint policies in the objective of the LFC problem. By assumption about
\sigma
, we know that

(f_{{\tilde{D}},D}^{*}\sigma({\tilde{D}}))([\pi^{R}])=\sigma({\tilde{D}})((f_{{\tilde{D}},D}^{*})^{-1}[\pi^{R}])=\alpha_{\tilde{D}}>0

and similarly

(f_{{\tilde{D}}^{\prime},D}^{*}\sigma({\tilde{D}}^{\prime}))([\pi^{S}])=\sigma({\tilde{D}}^{\prime})((f_{{\tilde{D}}^{\prime},D}^{*})^{-1}[\pi^{S}])=1-\alpha_{{\tilde{D}}^{\prime}}>0,

and it is
\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{D}})(\{{\tilde{D}}\})=\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{D}})(\{{\tilde{D}}^{\prime}\})=\frac{1}{{\mathcal{D}}}
.
Hence, it follows that
0<\beta\leq 1
(**). That is, with nonzero probability, using
\sigma
leads to incompatible policies.

Our goal is now to prove that
U^{D}(\sigma)<\frac{1}{2}
. To that end, we also have to show that the value in the cases where
X(D_{1},D_{2},\pi^{(1)},\pi^{(2)})=0
is bounded by
\frac{1}{2}
.
Note that for any
E\in{\mathcal{D}}
, it is

(f_{E,D}^{*}\sigma(E))([\pi^{R}]\cup[\pi^{S}])=\sigma(E)(((f_{E,D}^{*})^{-1}[\pi^{R}])\cup((f_{E,D}^{*})^{-1}[\pi^{S}]))=1,

and for
\pi^{(1)},\pi^{(2)}\in[\pi^{R}]\cup[\pi^{S}]
, using the definition of equivalence, it is

J^{D}(\Psi(\pi^{(1)})_{1},\Psi(\pi^{(2)})_{2})=J^{D}(\Psi(\pi^{C})_{1},\Psi(\pi^{C^{\prime}})_{2})\overset{(*)}{=}J^{D}(\pi^{C}_{1},\pi^{C^{\prime}}_{2})\overset{\text{Lemma~\ref{lemma-two-classes-in-coop}}}{=}\frac{1}{2}

(211)

if both policies are from the same class, i.e.,
C=C^{\prime}\in\{R,S\}
, and

J^{D}(\Psi(\pi^{(1)})_{1},\Psi(\pi^{(2)})_{2})=J^{D}(\Psi(\pi^{C})_{1},\Psi(\pi^{C^{\prime}})_{2})\overset{(*)}{=}J^{D}(\pi^{C}_{1},\pi^{C^{\prime}}_{2})\overset{\text{Lemma~\ref{lemma-XP-suboptimal}}}{=}-\frac{1}{2}

(212)

if they come from different classes, i.e.,
C\neq C^{\prime}\in\{R,S\}
.
It follows that for any
D_{1},D_{2}\in{\mathcal{D}}
, it is

J^{D}(\Psi(\pi^{(1)})_{1},\Psi(\pi^{(2)})_{2})\leq\frac{1}{2}

(213)

almost surely if
\pi^{(1)}\sim f_{D_{1},D}^{*}\sigma(D_{1})
and
\pi^{(2)}\sim f_{D_{2},D}^{*}\sigma(D_{2})
.

Using this bound together with (**), it follows that

\displaystyle U^{D}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=U^{D}(\sigma,\dotsc,\sigma)

(214)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{D_{1},D_{2}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{D}})}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim f_{D_{i},D}^{*}\sigma(D_{i}),\,i=1,2}[J^{D}(\Psi_{1}(\pi^{(1)}),\Psi_{2}(\pi^{(2)}))]]

(215)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{D_{1},D_{2}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{D}})}\big[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim f_{D_{i},D}^{*}\sigma(D_{i}),\,i=1,2}\big[J^{D}(\Psi(\pi_{1}^{(1)}),\Psi_{2}(\pi^{(2)}))X(D_{1},D_{2},\pi^{(1)},\pi^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+J^{D}(\Psi_{1}(\pi^{(1)}),\Psi_{2}(\pi^{(2)}))(1-X(D_{1},D_{2},\pi^{(1)},\pi^{(2)}))\big]\big]

(216)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{D_{1},D_{2}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{D}})}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim f_{D_{i},D}^{*}\sigma(D_{i}),\,i=1,2}\Bigg[\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)X\left(D_{1},D_{2},\pi^{(1)},\pi^{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{1}{2}\left(1-X\left(D_{1},D_{2},\pi^{(1)},\pi^{(2)}\right)\right)\Bigg]\Bigg]

(217)

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)\beta+\frac{1}{2}(1-\beta)

(218)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(**)}}{<}\frac{1}{2},

(219)

where we use Theorem
72
in (
215
) and Equations
212
and
213
in (
217
).

To see that this is suboptimal in the LFC problem, consider
\sigma^{*}
defined via
\sigma^{*}(E)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\delta_{f_{D,E}^{*}\pi^{R}}
for any
E\in{\mathcal{D}}
. Since

f_{E,D}^{*}\sigma(E)=\delta_{f_{D,E}^{*}\pi^{R}}\circ(f_{E,D}^{*})^{-1}=\delta_{f_{E,D}^{*}(f_{D,E}^{*}\pi^{R})}=\delta_{\pi^{R}}

(220)

for any
E\in{\mathcal{D}}
, it follows that

U^{D}(\sigma^{*})=U^{D}(\sigma^{*},\dotsc,\sigma^{*})\\
\overset{\text{Theorem~\ref{thm-zero-shot-invariant}}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{D_{1},D_{2}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{D}})}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim f_{D_{i},D}^{*}\sigma^{*}(D_{i}),\,i=1,2}[J^{D}(\Psi_{1}(\pi^{(1)}),\Psi_{2}(\pi^{(2)}))]]\\
\overset{(\ref{eq:44})}{=}J^{D}(\Psi_{1}(\pi^{R}),\Psi_{2}(\pi^{R}))\overset{(\ref{eq:45})}{=}\frac{1}{2}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:41a})--(\ref{eq:41d})}}{>}U^{D}(\sigma),

(221)

which concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix F
Other-play with tie-breaking

In this section, we formally define OP with tie-breaking as introduced in Section
5
, state and prove a rigorous version of Theorem
8
, and provide an additional result about random tie-breaking functions. In Appendix
F.1
, we define OP with tie-breaking and discuss to what degree the formal definition is satisfied by our method. In Appendix
F.2
, we show that OP with tie-breaking is optimal in the LFC problem and that all principals using OP with tie-breaking is an optimal symmetric Nash equilibrium of any LFC game. In Appendix
F.3
we prove that a modification of the tie-breaking function introduced in Section
5
satisfies our definition of a tie-breaking function.

F.1
Definition of other-play with tie-breaking

Recall that OP with tie-breaking was introduced as an extension of OP, to fix the failure of OP in the LFC problem. A tie-breaking function ranks the different OP-optimal equivalence classes of policies in a given problem. For instance, a tie-breaking function could compare the two incompatible policies in the two-stage lever game,
\pi^{R}
and
\pi^{S}
, and choose the policy under which actions are more highly correlated, which is
\pi^{R}
. Another tie-breaking function would be one that samples from an OP learning algorithm and ranks policies in terms of their relative frequencies. If one policy is learned more often than another, that tie-breaking function would be able to distinguish between them. OP with tie-breaking is defined as an algorithm that chooses an OP-optimal policy that maximizes a tie-breaking function.

Note that an obvious alternative approach, making a learning algorithm deterministic by coordinating on a random seed, would not work in the LFC problem. This is because the learned policies have to be compatible even across different, relabeled Dec-POMDPs. A labeling modifies the representation of the data used to train the algorithm, which likely leads to the same effect as a resampling of the random seed. Hence, fixing a random seed fails to ensure that the chosen policies are compatible when principals do not coordinate on labels for the problem. (Note that a learning algorithm that outputs consistent policies on a given problem, but incompatible policies on different, relabeled problems is explicitly included as a non-optimal algorithm in Theorem
80
.) Additionally, this would be an unprincipled way to deal with the choice between different maximizers of the OP objective, leaving no possibility, e.g., for an explicit bias towards some policies over others.

Turning to the definitions, recall again from Appendix
D
that we say that for a Dec-POMDP
D
,
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{D}
are equivalent,
\pi\equiv\pi^{\prime}
, if
\Psi(\pi)=\Psi(\pi^{\prime})
, where
\Psi
is the symmetrizer for
D
that maps a joint policy
\pi
to the joint policy
\Psi(\pi)
corresponding to agents following randomly permuted local policies
(\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}\pi)
for
\mathbf{g}_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(D))
. Moreover, we defined
[\pi]
as the equivalence class of
\pi
, and
f^{*}[\pi]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=[f^{*}\pi]
for
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
.

In the following, define
\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)
as the set of OP-optimal policies for any Dec-POMDP
D
. Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be any set of Dec-POMDPs. A tie-breaking function takes in Dec-POMDPs
D\in{\mathcal{D}}
and policies
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
and outputs values in
[0,1]
that can be used to consistently break ties between policies, across different isomorphic Dec-POMDPs.

Definition 81

(Tie-breaking function)
.

Let
\chi\colon\{(D,\pi)\mid D\in{\mathcal{D}},\pi\in\Pi^{D}\}\rightarrow[0,1]
. Then

(a)

\chi
is called a tie-breaking function for
{\mathcal{D}}
if

(i)

for any
D\in{\mathcal{D}}
,
\chi
attains a maximum on the set

\{(D,\pi)\mid\pi\in\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}\}.

(ii)

for any
D\in{\mathcal{D}}
and
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{D}
, it is

\chi(D,\pi)=\chi(D,\pi^{\prime})\Rightarrow\pi\equiv_{D}\pi^{\prime}.

(b)

\chi
is called invariant to isomorphism if for any
D,E\in{\mathcal{D}}
,
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
, and
\pi\in\Pi^{D},\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{E}
, it is

f^{*}[\pi]=[\pi^{\prime}]\Rightarrow\chi(D,\pi)=\chi(E,\pi^{\prime}).

\chi
being a tie-breaking function ensures that there is always a unique equivalence class of policies that maximizes the function for a given Dec-POMDP. It being invariant to isomorphism ensures that
\chi
chooses corresponding equivalence classes of policies on different, isomorphic Dec-POMDPs in
{\mathcal{D}}
.

Using a tie-breaking function that is invariant to isomorphism, we can define OP with tie-breaking.

Definition 82

(Other-play with tie-breaking)
.

Let
\chi
be a tie-breaking function for
{\mathcal{D}}
that is invariant to isomorphism. Let
\sigma^{\chi}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
be a learning algorithm such that for any
D\in{\mathcal{D}}
, there exists a measurable set
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\Pi_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}
such that
\sigma^{\chi}(D)(\mathcal{Z})=1
and
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}}\chi(D,\pi)
. Then we say that
\sigma^{\chi}
is an OP with tie-breaking learning algorithm for
{\mathcal{D}}
.

This definition implies that no matter the problem
D\in{\mathcal{D}}
, the algorithm always learns the OP-optimal policy that achieves the highest tie-breaking value. Since the tie-breaking function is invariant to isomorphism, this means that policies learned in different training runs and when trained on relabeled Dec-POMDPs are always compatible.

Finally, recall that the practical method introduced in Section
5
consists of sampling
K\in\mathbb{N}
policies using an OP learning algorithm, applying a tie-breaking function to each policy, and then choosing the one with the highest value. Clearly, if all the OP-optimal equivalence classes of policies in any of the Dec-POMDPs in
{\mathcal{D}}
are among the first
K
learned policies, then this algorithm will satisfy our definition. This appears to be the case for the OP algorithm and toy examples used in our experiments, at least for large enough
K
, and when we ignore differences between policies that matter little for the agents’ expected returns. Moreover, it is easy to see that the algorithm will still always pick equivalent policies if for any two isomorphic Dec-POMDPs
D,E\in{\mathcal{D}}
, if
\sigma^{\mathrm{OP}}
learns a policy
\pi
with positive probability in
D
, it also learns an equivalent policy
\pi^{\prime}
in
E
with positive probability, in the sense that
f^{*}[\pi]=[\pi^{\prime}]
for
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
. That is, it does not matter if some policies are never learned, if this happens consistently across isomorphic problems. One way for an algorithm
\sigma^{\mathrm{OP}}
to have this property is by being equivariant (see Appendix
C.8
).

F.2
Other-play with tie-breaking is an optimal symmetric profile

In the following, fix a Dec-POMDP
D
and the set of relabeled Dec-POMDPs
{\mathcal{D}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{f^{*}D\mid f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D\}
, and let
\chi
be a tie-breaking function for
{\mathcal{D}}
that is invariant to isomorphism. Let
U(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=U^{D}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})
stand for the payoff in the LFC game for
D
given the profile of learning algorithms
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
. Recall that the learning algorithm
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
is optimal in the LFC problem for
D
if
U(\sigma)\geq U(\sigma^{\prime})
for all
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
, where
U(\sigma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=U(\sigma,\dotsc,\sigma)
.

Recall from Appendix
C.7
that we call a profile of learning algorithms
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
symmetric if
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{g^{-1}i}
for any principal
i\in\mathcal{N}
and
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
.
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is defined as an optimal symmetric profile if it is symmetric and for any other symmetric profile
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
, it is
U(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\geq U(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime})
.

Turning to our main theorem, we show that a profile
\sigma^{\chi},\dotsc,\sigma^{\chi}
in which all principals choose OP with tie-breaking is an optimal symmetric profile in the LFC game for
D
. That is, as long as symmetric principals choose the same learning algorithm, they cannot do better than all choosing OP with tie-breaking. In particular, this implies that
\sigma^{\chi}
is optimal in the LFC problem. Note, though, that we prove a stronger statement, as optimality for the LFC problem only requires that
U(\sigma^{\chi},\dotsc,\sigma^{\chi})\geq U(\sigma^{\prime},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime})
for all
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
, while we show that
U(\sigma^{\chi},\dotsc,\sigma^{\chi})\geq U^{D}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N})
for all symmetric profiles
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}
. Afterwards, we will apply Theorem
48
to conclude that all principals using OP with tie-breaking is also a Nash equilibrium, i.e., given that all principals use OP with tie-breaking, no individual principal can do better by switching to a different algorithm.

Theorem 83
.

Let
\sigma^{\chi}
be an OP with tie-breaking learning algorithm for
{\mathcal{D}}
, as defined in Definition
82
. Then all principals using
\sigma^{\chi}
is an optimal symmetric strategy profile in the LFC game for
D
. In particular,
\sigma^{\chi}
is optimal in the LFC problem for
D
, and it is

U(\sigma^{\chi})=\max_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{D}}}U(\sigma)=\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)
.

Proof.

We begin by showing that
U(\sigma^{\chi},\dotsc,\sigma^{\chi})=\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)
. Afterwards, we show that one cannot get a better payoff in the LFC game than that using a symmetric profile of learning algorithms, i.e., that
\sigma^{\chi},\dotsc,\sigma^{\chi}
is an optimal symmetric profile. It then follows immediately that also
U(\sigma^{\chi})=U(\sigma^{\chi},\dotsc,\sigma^{\chi})\geq U(\sigma,\dotsc,\sigma)=U(\sigma)
for any
\sigma\in\Sigma^{D}
, i.e., that
\sigma^{\chi}
is optimal in the LFC problem for
D
.

Recall the definition
\Pi_{\mathrm{OP}}^{E}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\argmax_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{E}}J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{E}(\pi^{\prime})
for
E\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Let
E,F\in{\mathcal{D}}
arbitrary and
f,\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
such that
E=f^{*}D
and
F=\tilde{f}^{*}D
. We want to show that
\sigma^{\chi}
learns compatible OP-optimal policies in both Dec-POMDPs. To that end, let
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\Pi_{\mathrm{OP}}^{E}
measurable such that
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi^{E}}\chi(E,\pi)
and such that
\sigma(E)(\mathcal{Z})=1
. Then since
\chi
is a tie-breaking function, there must exist a policy
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{OP}}^{E}
such that
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}\subseteq[\pi^{\prime}]
and thus
\sigma(E)([\pi^{\prime}])=1
and
\pi^{\prime}\in\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{OP}}^{E}}\chi(E,\pi)
(i). Letting
\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(f^{-1})^{*}\pi^{\prime}
, it follows from Corollary
71
that
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}
(ii).

Now we want to show that
\sigma^{\chi}
learns policies in
[\tilde{f}^{*}\pi]
in
F
.
Let
\tilde{\pi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\tilde{f}\circ f^{-1})^{*}\pi^{\prime}
and note that
\tilde{f}\circ f^{-1}\in\mathrm{Iso}(E,F)
, so
\tilde{\pi}\in\Pi^{F}
. Since
\chi
is invariant to isomorphism, it is

\chi(F,\hat{\pi})=\chi(E,(f\circ\tilde{f}^{-1})^{*}\hat{\pi})\overset{\text{(i)}}{\leq}\chi(E,\pi^{\prime}),

for any
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{F}
. Here, equality holds for
\hat{\pi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\tilde{\pi}
, as it is

(f\circ\tilde{f}^{-1})^{*}\tilde{\pi}=(f\circ\tilde{f}^{-1})^{*}(\tilde{f}\circ f^{-1})^{*}\pi^{\prime}=\pi^{\prime}

by Lemma
21
. Moreover, due to Corollary
71
it is again
\tilde{\pi}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{OP}}^{F}
. This shows that
\tilde{\pi}\in\argmax_{\hat{\pi}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{OP}}^{F}}\chi(F,\hat{\pi})
, and since
\chi
is a tie-breaking function, it is
\argmax_{\hat{\pi}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{OP}}^{F}}\chi(F,\hat{\pi})\subseteq[\tilde{\pi}]
. Hence, by the definition of
\sigma^{\chi}
, it follows that
\sigma^{\chi}(F)([\tilde{\pi}])=1
(iii).

Now choose any arbitrary isomorphism
f_{F,D}\in\mathrm{Iso}(F,D)
. By Lemma
21
, it is
(f_{F,D}^{*})^{-1}=(f_{F,D}^{-1})^{*}
. Hence, by the second part of Lemma
69
, it is
(f_{F,D}^{*})^{-1}[\pi]=\tilde{f}^{*}[\pi]
(iv). Moreover, using again Lemma
21
, it is

(f\circ\tilde{f}^{-1})^{*}\tilde{\pi}=(f\circ\tilde{f}^{-1})^{*}(\tilde{f}\circ f^{-1})^{*}\pi^{\prime}=\pi^{\prime}.

(222)

It follows that

(f_{F,D}^{*})^{-1}([\pi])\overset{\text{(iv)}}{=}\tilde{f}^{*}[\pi]=\tilde{f}^{*}[(f^{-1})^{*}\pi^{\prime}]\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:701})}}{=}\tilde{f}^{*}[(f^{-1})^{*}(f\circ\tilde{f}^{-1})^{*}\tilde{\pi}]\\
\overset{\text{Definition~\ref{defn-pushforward-of-equivalence-classes}}}{=}\tilde{f}^{*}(f^{-1})^{*}(f\circ\tilde{f}^{-1})^{*}[\tilde{\pi}]\overset{\text{Corollary~\ref{cor-pull-back-well-defined}~(iii)}}{=}[\tilde{\pi}]

(223)

and thus

f_{F,D}^{*}\sigma^{\chi}(F)([\pi])=\sigma^{\chi}(F)((f_{F,D}^{*})^{-1}([\pi]))\overset{(\ref{eq:702})}{=}\sigma^{\chi}(F)([\tilde{\pi}])\overset{\text{(iii)}}{=}1.

(224)

Since
F
was arbitrary, we can conclude that the above equation holds for any
F\in{\mathcal{D}}
and isomorphism
f_{F,D}\in\mathrm{Iso}(F,D)
.

Using this fact together with the definition of equivalence of policies in Definition
66
, as well as the expression for the payoff in the LFC game from Theorem
72
, it follows that

\displaystyle U(\sigma^{\chi},\dotsc,\sigma^{\chi})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Theorem~\ref{thm-zero-shot-invariant}}}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim U({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim f_{D_{j},D}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}(D_{j}),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}\left(\left(\Psi_{k}(\pi^{(k)})\right)_{k\in\mathcal{N}}\right)\right]\right]

(225)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:700})}}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{D_{i}\sim U({\mathcal{D}}),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(j)}\sim f_{D_{j},D}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}(D_{j}),\,j\in\mathcal{N}}\left[J^{D}\left(\left(\Psi_{k}(\pi)\right)_{k\in\mathcal{N}}\right)\right]\right]

(226)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J^{D}(\Psi(\pi))

(227)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Theorem~\ref{thm-op-mixture}}}{=}

\displaystyle J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)

(228)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(ii)}}{=}

\displaystyle\max_{\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{D}}J_{D}^{\mathrm{OP}}(\hat{\pi}),

(229)

Next, we show that we cannot do better than that with a symmetry-invariant profile of learning algorithms. To that end, let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N}\in\Sigma^{{\mathcal{D}}}
arbitrary such that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{g^{-1}i}
for any
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
,
i\in\mathcal{N}
. For
i\in\mathcal{N}
, define the distribution

\nu^{(i)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=|\mathrm{Sym}(D)|^{-1}\sum_{f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)}(f^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(f^{*}D)

and let
\mu\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\otimes_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\nu^{(i)}_{i}
.
Then it is

\displaystyle U(\boldsymbol{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{f}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Sym}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i)}\sim(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}D),\,i\in\mathcal{N}}[J^{D}((\pi^{(i)}_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{N}})]]

(230)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}|\mathrm{Sym}(D)|^{-N}\int_{(\pi^{(i)})_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\in(\Pi^{D})^{\mathcal{N}}}J^{D}((\pi^{(i)}_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{N}})\mathrm{d}\otimes_{i\in\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}D)

(231)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{f}\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)^{\mathcal{N}}}|\mathrm{Sym}(D)|^{-N}\int_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}(\pi)\mathrm{d}\otimes_{i\in\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}D)\circ\mathrm{proj}_{i}^{-1}

(232)

\displaystyle=\int_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}(\pi)\mathrm{d}\otimes_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(|\mathrm{Sym}(D)|^{-1}\sum_{\mathbf{f}_{i}\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{f}_{i}^{*}D)\circ\mathrm{proj}_{i}^{-1}\right)

(233)

\displaystyle=\int_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}(\pi)\mathrm{d}\mu

(234)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{expected-return-mixture})}{=}J^{D}(\mu).

(235)

Now we want to show that it is
J^{D}(\mu)\leq\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}(\pi)
, by proving that
\mu
and thus also the corresponding policy
\pi^{\mu}
is invariant to pushforward by automorphism.
Let
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
and let
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\subseteq\Pi_{i}^{D}
measurable for
i\in\mathcal{N}
. Recall that by Lemmas
13
and
21
,
g
is a bijective self-map on action-observation histories and
g^{*}
a bijective self-map on
\Pi^{D}
, and that isomorphisms can be inverted and composed by Lemma
18
. In the following, we use the notations
\pi_{i}\circ g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\pi_{i}(g\cdot\mid g\cdot)
and
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\circ g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{\pi_{i}(g\cdot\mid g\cdot)\mid\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\}
for
i\in\mathcal{N}
.

By definition,
\mu
has independent local policies. Hence, we can apply Equation
159
from the proof of Lemma
61
, which says that

(g^{*}\mu)(\mathcal{Z})=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}\mu_{g^{-1}i}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}\circ g).

(236)

Moreover, it is
(g^{*})^{-1}(\Pi^{D})=\Pi^{D}
and by definition,
(g^{*}\pi)_{i}=\pi_{g^{-1}i}\circ g^{-1}
, which implies that

\displaystyle\mathrm{proj}^{-1}_{g^{-1}i}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}\circ g)

\displaystyle=\{\pi\mid\pi\in\Pi^{D},\pi_{g^{-1}i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\circ g\}

(237)

\displaystyle=\{\pi\mid\pi\in(g^{*})^{-1}(\Pi^{D}),\pi_{g^{-1}i}\circ g^{-1}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\}

(238)

\displaystyle=\{\pi\mid g^{*}\pi\in\Pi^{D},(g^{*}\pi)_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\}

(239)

\displaystyle=\{(g^{*})^{-1}\pi\mid\pi\in\Pi^{D},\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\}

(240)

\displaystyle=(g^{*})^{-1}(\mathrm{proj}^{-1}_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})).

(241)

Hence, it follows for
i\in\mathcal{N}
that

\displaystyle\nu^{(i)}(\mathrm{proj}^{-1}_{g^{-1}i}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}\circ g))

\displaystyle=\nu^{(i)}((g^{*})^{-1}(\mathrm{proj}^{-1}_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})))

(242)

\displaystyle=|\mathrm{Sym}(D)|^{-1}\sum_{f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)}(f^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(f^{*}D)((g^{*})^{-1}(\mathrm{proj}_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})))

(243)

\displaystyle=|\mathrm{Sym}(D)|^{-1}\sum_{f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)}g^{*}(f^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(f^{*}D)(\mathrm{proj}_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}))

(244)

\displaystyle=|\mathrm{Sym}(D)|^{-1}\sum_{f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)}((f\circ g^{-1})^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(f^{*}D)(\mathrm{proj}_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}))

(245)

\displaystyle=|\mathrm{Sym}(D)|^{-1}\sum_{f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)}((f\circ g^{-1})^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}((f\circ g^{-1})^{*}D)(\mathrm{proj}_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}))

(246)

\displaystyle=|\mathrm{Sym}(D)|^{-1}\sum_{f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)}(f^{-1})^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}(f^{*}D)(\mathrm{proj}_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}))

(247)

\displaystyle=\nu^{(i)}(\mathrm{proj}_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})).

(248)

Here, we use in (
246
) and (
247
) that
g^{-1}\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,D)
and thus by Lemma
34
, it is
(f\circ g^{-1})^{*}D=f^{*}D
and
\mathrm{Sym}(D)=\mathrm{Sym}(D)\circ g^{-1}
, respectively.

Next, note that by assumption, it is
\nu^{(i)}=\nu^{(g^{-1}i)}
for
i\in\mathcal{N}
(v). It follows that

(g^{*}\mu)(\mathcal{Z})\overset{(\ref{eq:60})}{=}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{E}}\mu_{g^{-1}i}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}\circ g)=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}\nu^{(g^{-1}i)}(\mathrm{proj}_{g^{-1}i}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}\circ g))\\
\overset{\text{(v)}}{=}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}\nu^{(i)}(\mathrm{proj}_{g^{-1}i}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}\circ g))\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:72})--(\ref{eq:72c})}}{=}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}\nu^{(i)}(\mathrm{proj}_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}))\\
=\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}^{D}}\mu_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})=\mu(\mathcal{Z}).

(249)

Since the sets
\prod_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{Z}_{i}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{i}
are a
\pi
-system and generate
\mathcal{F}
, this shows that
\mu=g^{*}\mu
. By Lemma
61
, it is thus

g^{*}\pi^{\mu}=\pi^{g^{*}\mu}=\pi^{\mu}

(250)

for any
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
, where
\pi^{\mu}
is the policy corresponding to
\mu
as defined in Section
D.2
.

Using Proposition
60
, it follows that

U(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dotsc,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{N})\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:705a})--(\ref{eq:705b})}}{=}J^{D}(\mu)\overset{\text{Proposition~\ref{mixture-lemma}}}{=}J(\pi^{\mu})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}[J^{D}(\pi^{\mu})]\\
=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[J^{D}\left((\mathrm{proj}_{i}(\pi^{\mu}))_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\right)\right]\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:73})}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[J^{D}\left((\mathrm{proj}_{i}(\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}\pi^{\mu}))_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\right)\right]\\
=J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi^{\mu})\leq\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}(\pi)\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:55a})--(\ref{eq:55c})}}{=}U(\sigma^{\chi},\dotsc,\sigma^{\chi}).

(251)

This concludes the proof.
∎

Finally, it follows as a corollary of Theorem
48
that the profile
\sigma^{\chi},\dotsc,\sigma^{\chi}
is a Nash equilibrium of the LFC game.

Corollary 84
.

All principals using OP with tie-breaking is a Nash equilibrium of the LFC game for
D
.

Proof.

By Theorem
83
, a profile in which all principals use OP with tie-breaking is an optimal symmetric strategy profile in the LFC game for
D
. Hence, by Theorem
48
, it is a Nash equilibrium of the game.
∎

F.3
Random tie-breaking functions

In this last section, we show that a certain tie-breaking function, based on random hashes of normal forms of histories, is a tie-breaking function that is invariant to isomorphisms, in the sense of Definition
81
above. As we prove that a certain random function is a tie-breaking function with probability
1
, one may call this approach a “probabilistic method”
(
Alon & Spencer 2016
, cf.)
.

In the following, let
{\mathcal{D}}
be any set of Dec-POMDPs and
D\in{\mathcal{D}}
. Recall that in Section
5
, we defined a function
\iota
that maps histories to normal forms, where the normal form is a history in which the first occurrence of each state, action, or observation is set to a
0
, the second occurrence is set to a
1
, and so on, and where, if an element in
\tau\in\mathcal{H}^{D}
repeats itself, the number is repeated. For
\iota(\tau)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(s_{0},(a_{i,0})_{i\in\mathcal{N}},r_{0},\dotsc,s_{T},(o_{i,T})_{i\in\mathcal{N}},(a_{i,T})_{i\in\mathcal{N}},r_{T})
, we then define

f_{N}(\iota(\tau))\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(s_{0},(a_{f_{N}^{-1}i,0})_{i\in\mathcal{N}},r_{0},\dotsc,s_{T},(o_{f_{N}^{-1}i,T})_{i\in\mathcal{N}},(a_{f_{N}^{-1}i,T})_{i\in\mathcal{N}},r_{T}).

for a permutation
f_{N}\in\mathrm{Bij}(\mathcal{N})
of
\mathcal{N}
. The tie-breaking function in Section
5
was defined as

\tilde{\chi}^{\#}(D,\pi)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{N!}\sum_{f_{N}\in\mathrm{Bij}(\mathcal{N})}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi}\left[\#(f_{N}(\iota(H)))\right]\right],

(252)

for some neural random neural network
\#
.

To be able to prove a theoretical result, we have to modify this tie-breaking function to make it dependent on the Dec-POMDP that belongs to a normal form
\iota(\tau)
. The above function is simpler to implement, as we do not have to implement a representation of a Dec-POMDP, and it was sufficient for our experimental results. We leave it to future work to determine to what degree the above version works in general.

In order to define the modified function formally, let
(\Omega_{\#},\mathcal{E},\mathbb{P}_{\#})
be some probability space, such that for any
D\in{\mathcal{D}}
,
f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
and
\tau\in\mathcal{H}^{f^{*}D}
, there is an independent, identically distributed real-valued random variable
\#(f^{*}D,\tau)
on this space.
Assume that

\mathbb{P}_{\#}(\#(f^{*}D,\tau)=\lambda)=0\quad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R},D\in{\mathcal{D}},f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D),\tau\in\mathcal{H}^{f^{*}D}.

(253)

For instance, this would be satisfied by uniformly distributed hash values
\#(f^{*}D,\tau)\sim\mathcal{U}([0,1])
.

Now for a given Dec-POMDP
D
and history
\tau\in\mathcal{H}^{D}
, denote
{\mathcal{K}}(\tau)\subseteq\mathrm{Sym}(D)
for the set of labelings
f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
such that
f\tau=\iota(\tau)
. It is easy to see that for any history
\tau\in\mathcal{H}^{D}
, there must exist some labelings
f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
such that
f\tau=\iota(\tau)
, so
{\mathcal{K}}(\tau)
is non-empty. Then our tie-breaking function here is defined as

\chi^{\#}(D,\pi)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{E}_{\Psi^{D}(\pi)}\left[\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(H^{D}))}\left[\#(f^{*}D,fH^{D})\right]\right]

(254)

for
D\in{\mathcal{D}},\pi\in\Pi^{D}
.
We have to sample from the symmetrized policy
\Psi(\pi)
instead of just
\pi
, since we need a distribution over histories under which equivalent histories have equal probabilities, and the tie-breaking function needs to be equal for equivalent policies in order to always have a maximizer. The expectation over labelings in
{\mathcal{K}}(\tau)
makes the function invariant to isomorphisms. We need to make the hash function depend on the Dec-POMDP, since otherwise, we are unable to prove that it can distinguish all histories that it needs to be able to distinguish.

Remark 85
.

By Proposition
60
, the distribution over histories under
\mathbb{P}_{\Psi(\pi)}
is the same as the one under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
where
\mu
is the OP distribution of
\pi\in\Pi^{D}
. Hence, using the definition of
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
from Equation
119
and the definition of the OP distribution from Equation
173
, it follows that

\displaystyle\chi^{\#}(D,\pi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Psi^{D}(\pi)}\left[\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(H^{D}))}\left[\#(f^{*}D,fH^{D})\right]\right]

(255)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Proposition~\ref{mixture-lemma}}}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(H^{D}))}\left[\#(f^{*}D,fH^{D})\right]\right]

(256)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{defn-semidirect-product})}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\pi}\sim\mu}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\pi}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(H^{D}))}\left[\#(f^{*}D,fH^{D})\right]\right]\right]

(257)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{equation-other-play-mixture})}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Aut}(D)^{\mathcal{N}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}^{*}\pi}\left[\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(H^{D}))}\left[\#(f^{*}D,fH^{D})\right]\right]\right]

(258)

for any
D\in{\mathcal{D}},\pi\in\Pi^{D}
.
Given a realization of the hash function
\#
, one can hence compute an estimate of
\chi^{\#}(D,\pi)
by sampling a Monte Carlo estimate of the expectation in (
258
).
One can easily see that without the dependence on Dec-POMDPs, this formulation is equal to the one in Equation
252
.

Now we show that
\chi^{\#}
is invariant to isomorphism. Note that this statement holds for
any
sample
\omega\in\Omega_{\#}
and thus for any sample
\chi^{\#}(\cdot)(\omega)
of the tie-breaking function. Afterwards, we will show that
\chi^{\#}
is almost surely a tie-breaking function. We first need a small lemma.

Lemma 86
.

Let
D
,
E
isomorphic Dec-POMDPs with
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
and let
\tau\in\mathcal{H}^{D}
. Then it is

\{(\tilde{f}^{*}E,\tilde{f}(f\tau))\mid\tilde{f}\in{\mathcal{K}}(f\tau)\}=\{(\hat{f}^{*}D,\hat{f}\tau)\mid\hat{f}\in{\mathcal{K}}(\tau)\}.

Proof.

Note that for any labeling
\tilde{f}\in{\mathcal{K}}(f\tau)
,
\tilde{f}f\tau=(\tilde{f}\circ f)\tau
is in a normal form, and
\tilde{f}\circ f\in\mathrm{Sym}(D)
by Lemma
34
. Hence, also
\tilde{f}\circ f\in{\mathcal{K}}(\tau)
. An analogous argument from considering
f^{-1}
shows that for any
\hat{f}\in{\mathcal{K}}(\tau)
, it is
(\hat{f}\circ f^{-1})(f\tau)=\hat{f}\tau
in a normal form an thus
\hat{f}\circ f^{-1}\in{\mathcal{K}}(f\tau)
. It follows that
{\mathcal{K}}(f\tau)\circ f={\mathcal{K}}(\tau)
(i). Moreover, by Lemma
34
, it is
\tilde{f}^{*}E=(\tilde{f}\circ f)^{*}D
(ii), and thus

\displaystyle\{(\tilde{f}^{*}E,\tilde{f}(f\tau))\mid\tilde{f}\in{\mathcal{K}}(f\tau)\}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(ii)}}{=}\{((\tilde{f}\circ f)^{*}D,(\tilde{f}\circ f)\tau)\mid\tilde{f}\in{\mathcal{K}}(f\tau)\}

(259)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(i)}}{=}\{(\hat{f}^{*}D,\hat{f}\tau)\mid\hat{f}\in{\mathcal{K}}(\tau)\}.

(260)

∎

Using this Lemma, we show the first result.

Proposition 87
.

\chi^{\#}
as defined in Equation
254
is invariant to isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
D,E\in{\mathcal{D}}
,
f\in\mathrm{Iso}(D,E)
and let
\pi\in\Pi^{D},\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{E}
such that
f^{*}[\pi]=[\pi^{\prime}]
. We need to show that
\chi^{\#}(D,\pi)=\chi^{\#}(E,\pi^{\prime})
.

Note that
f^{*}[\pi]=[f^{*}\pi]
by definition, and thus it follows from the assumption that
f^{*}\pi\equiv\pi^{\prime}
, which means that
\Psi^{E}(f^{*}\pi)=\Psi^{E}(\pi^{\prime})
(i) by the definition of
\equiv
.

It follows that

\displaystyle\chi^{\#}(E,\pi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Psi^{E}(\pi^{\prime})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{f}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(H^{E}))}\left[\#(\tilde{f}^{*}E,\tilde{f}H^{E})\right]\right]

(261)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(i)}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\Psi^{E}(f^{*}\pi)}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{f}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(H^{E}))}\left[\#(\tilde{f}^{*}E,\tilde{f}H^{E})\right]\right]

(262)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{f^{*}\Psi^{D}(\pi)}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{f}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(H^{E}))}\left[\#(\tilde{f}^{*}E,\tilde{f}H^{E})\right]\right]

(263)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Psi^{D}(\pi)}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{f}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(fH^{D}))}\left[\#(\tilde{f}^{*}E,\tilde{f}(fH^{D}))\right]\right]

(264)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Psi^{D}(\pi)}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\hat{f}\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(H^{D}))}\left[\#(\hat{f}^{*}D,\hat{f}H^{D})\right]\right]

(265)

where in (
263
), we use that isomorphisms and symmetrizer
\Psi
commute by Corollary
67
, in (
264
), we use Theorem
22
, and in (
265
), we use Lemma
86
.

This shows that
\chi^{\#}
is invariant to isomorphism.
∎

To show that
\chi^{\#}
is almost surely a tie-breaking function, we need two technical assumptions. The first, substantial one, is that the set of OP-optimal equivalence classes of policies is finite, to make sure that there always exists a unique maximizer of
\chi^{\#}
. If this was not the case, our method for finding a tie-breaking function could fail. We leave it to future work to investigate whether and under what conditions this may be the case.

The second, less substantial assumption is that, if two policies
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
are not equivalent, then
\Psi(\pi)
and
\Psi(\pi^{\prime})
also do not lead to the same distribution over histories. Without this assumption,
\Psi(\pi)
and
\Psi(\pi^{\prime})
might differ on action-observation histories that are never reached, such that the policies induce the same distribution over histories but are not equivalent. Choosing policies which differ in that way should not matter for the objective of the LFC problem, but we wanted to avoid dealing with this complication in our proof that OP with tie-breaking is optimal. For this reason, we have stronger requirements for tie-breaking functions here, thus necessitating this technical assumption.
6
6

6

Note that in Definition
58
, each local policy is defined separately, so that
\Psi(\pi)_{i}(\cdot\mid\tau_{i,t})
can in principle be defined as something other than the uniform distribution, even if the action-observation history
\tau_{i,t}
is never reached under the joint policy
\Psi(\pi)
. Nevertheless, if all agents choose local policies from joint policies that lead to the same distribution over histories, the resulting cross-play policy should also induce that distribution, even if there exist opponent distributions where the local policies might differ.

Proposition 88
.

Let
\chi^{\#}
be defined as in Equation
254
. Assume that

(i)

for any
D\in{\mathcal{D}}
, the set
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\displaystyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{4.20613pt}{$\textstyle{\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\textstyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{3.21724pt}{$\scriptstyle{\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.72855pt}{$\scriptstyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{2.43056pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.287pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\equiv}$}}}
is finite, where
\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi^{D}}J_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}(\pi)
.

(ii)

for any
D\in{\mathcal{D}}
and policies
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{D}
, if
\mathbb{P}_{\Psi^{D}(\pi)}(H=\tau)=\mathbb{P}_{\Psi^{D}(\pi^{\prime})}(H=\tau)
for all
\tau\in\mathcal{H}^{D}
, it follows that
\Psi^{D}(\pi)=\Psi^{D}(\pi^{\prime})
.

Then
\chi^{\#}
is
\mathbb{P}_{\#}
-almost surely a tie-breaking function for
{\mathcal{D}}
.

For the proof, we first need a standard lemma in probability theory.

Lemma 89
.

Assume
\mathcal{J}
is a finite set and
X_{j},j\in\mathcal{J}
is a collection of independent real-valued random variables such that for any
j\in\mathcal{J}
, it is
\mathbb{P}(X_{j}=\lambda)=0
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
be the euclidean scalar product on
\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}}
. Then it is

\mathbb{P}(\langle X,v\rangle=\lambda)=0

for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
v\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}}\setminus\{0\}
.
Since
v\neq 0
, there exists an index
j\in\mathcal{J}
such that
v_{j}\neq 0
. It follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\langle X,v\rangle=\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\#}\left(X_{j}=\lambda-\frac{1}{v_{j}}\sum_{j^{\prime}\neq j}v_{j^{\prime}}X_{j^{\prime}}\right)

(266)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}}}\mathds{1}_{x_{j}=\lambda-\frac{1}{v_{j}}\sum_{{j^{\prime}}\neq j}x_{j^{\prime}}v_{j^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}(x)

(267)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}\setminus\{j\}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathds{1}_{x_{j}=\lambda-\frac{1}{v_{j}}\sum_{{j^{\prime}}\neq j}x_{j^{\prime}}v_{j^{\prime}})}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\circ X_{j}^{-1}(x_{j})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\circ X_{-j}^{-1}(x_{-j})

(268)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}\setminus\{j\}}}\mathbb{P}\left(X_{j}=\lambda-\frac{1}{v_{j}}\sum_{{j^{\prime}}\neq j}x_{j^{\prime}}v_{j^{\prime}}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\circ X_{-j}^{-1}(x_{-j})

(269)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}\setminus\{j\}}}0\;\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\circ X_{-j}^{-1}(x_{-j})

(270)

\displaystyle=0,

(271)

where we use the assumption
\mathbb{P}(X_{j}=\lambda)=0
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
in (
270
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
88
.

To check that
\chi^{\#}
always admits a maximum among the OP-optimal policies, which is part (a), (i) of Definition
81
, let
D\in{\mathcal{D}}
arbitrary and let
\pi\equiv\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{D}
be equivalent policies. By the definition of
\equiv
, it is
\Psi^{D}(\pi)=\Psi^{D}(\pi^{\prime})
(*).
Hence,
it is

\chi^{\#}(D,\pi)=\mathbb{E}_{\Psi^{D}(\pi)}\left[\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(H^{D}))}\left[\#(f^{*}D,fH^{D})\right]\right]\\
\overset{\text{(*)}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\Psi^{D}(\pi^{\prime})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(H^{D}))}\left[\#(f^{*}D,fH^{D})\right]\right]=\chi^{\#}(D,\pi^{\prime}).

(272)

This shows that
\chi^{\#}(D,\pi^{\prime})=\chi^{\#}(D,\pi)
for any
\pi\in\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}
and
\pi^{\prime}\in[\pi]
. Hence, we can define the function

\tilde{\chi}^{\#}(D,\cdot)\colon{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\displaystyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{4.20613pt}{$\textstyle{\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\textstyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{3.21724pt}{$\scriptstyle{\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.72855pt}{$\scriptstyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{2.43056pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.287pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\equiv}$}}}\rightarrow[0,1],[\pi]\mapsto\chi^{\#}(D,\pi).

Then, using assumption (i),
\tilde{\chi}^{\#}(D,\cdot)
is maximized by some equivalence class
[\pi]\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\displaystyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{4.20613pt}{$\textstyle{\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.31873pt}{$\textstyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{3.21724pt}{$\scriptstyle{\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.72855pt}{$\scriptstyle{\equiv}$}}{\raisebox{2.43056pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\Pi^{D}_{\mathrm{OP}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.287pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\equiv}$}}}
. By definition of
\tilde{\chi}^{\#}(D,\cdot)
, it follows that
for any
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}
, it is

\chi^{\#}(D,\pi^{\prime})=\tilde{\chi}^{\#}(D,[\pi^{\prime}])\leq\tilde{\chi}^{\#}(D,[\pi])=\chi^{\#}(D,\pi),

so
\pi
maximizes
\chi^{\#}(D,\cdot)
on the set
\Pi_{\mathrm{OP}}^{D}
.

To prove part (a), (ii) of the definition, consider two non-equivalent policies
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{D}
, i.e., assume that

\Psi(\pi)\neq\Psi(\pi^{\prime})
. We now want to show that
\mathbb{P}_{\#}\left(\chi^{\#}(D,\pi)=\chi^{\#}(D,\pi^{\prime})\right)=0
. If this is true, then
\chi^{\#}
is
\mathbb{P}_{\#}
-almost surely a tie-breaking function.

First, note that actions of automorphisms on histories are group actions (see Section
C.4
). Thus, we can consider a partition of the set of histories into orbits,
\mathcal{J}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{\mathrm{Aut}(D)\tau\mid\tau\in\mathcal{H}^{D}\}
. Moreover, note that since
\Psi^{D}(\pi),\Psi^{D}(\pi^{\prime})
are both invariant to automorphism by Corollary
67
, using Theorem
22
, we can conclude that for any
\tau\in\mathcal{H}^{D}
and
\tilde{\tau}\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)\tau
, it is
\mathbb{P}_{\Psi(\pi)}(H=\tau)=\mathbb{P}_{\Psi(\pi)}(H=\tilde{\tau})
. The same holds for
\Psi(\pi^{\prime})
. This step only works since we sample from
\Psi(\pi)
, instead of from the original policy
\pi
. As a result, it follows that we can define a vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}}
such that
v_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=|\mathrm{Aut}(D)\tau|\mathbb{P}_{\Psi(\pi)}(H=\tau)
for
j\in\mathcal{J}
, where
\tau\in\mathcal{H}^{D}
is arbitrary such that
\mathrm{Aut}(D)\tau=j
. Define
v^{\prime}
analogously for
\pi^{\prime}
. By assumption (ii),
\Psi(\pi)
and
\Psi(\pi^{\prime})
induce different distributions over histories, so it must also be
v\neq v^{\prime}
, since the map from orbit-invariant distributions to vectors
v
is injective.

Second, note that by Lemma
86
, it is

\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(\tau))}\left[\#(f^{*}D,f\tau)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(g\tau))}\left[\#(f^{*}D,fg\tau)\right]

for any
\tau\in\mathcal{H}^{D}
and
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(D)
, and thus for any
j\in\mathcal{J}
we can define the random variable
X_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(\tau))}\left[\#(f^{*}D,f\tau)\right]
, where
\tau
is an arbitrary history such that
j=\mathrm{Aut}(D)\tau
.

We now want to show that
\mathbb{P}_{\#}(\langle X,v-v^{\prime}\rangle=0)=0
, where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the euclidean scalar product on
\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}}
. This will then allow us to conclude that
\mathbb{P}_{\#}\left(\chi^{\#}(D,\pi)=\chi^{\#}(D,\pi^{\prime})\right)=0
. We already know that
v-v^{\prime}\neq 0
. To be able to apply Lemma
89
to show this, it remains to prove that for any two
j\neq j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}
, we also have two independent variables
X_{j},X_{j^{\prime}}
, and that
\mathbb{P}_{\#}(X_{j}=\lambda)=0
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

To prove independence, let
j=\mathrm{Aut}(D)\tau,j^{\prime}=\mathrm{Aut}(D)\tau^{\prime}
, and assume towards a contradiction that there exist
f\in{\mathcal{K}}(\tau),\tilde{f}\in{\mathcal{K}}(\tau^{\prime})
such that
f^{*}D=\tilde{f}^{*}D
and
f\tau=\tilde{f}\tau^{\prime}
. Then it follows that
\tau=f^{-1}\tilde{f}\tau^{\prime}
and thus
j=j^{\prime}
, which is a contradiction. It follows that the sets of variables
\{\#(f^{*}D,f\tau)\mid f\in{\mathcal{K}}(\tau)\}
and
\{\#(\tilde{f}^{*}D,\tilde{f}\tau^{\prime})\mid\tilde{f}\in{\mathcal{K}}(\tau^{\prime})\}
are disjoint. Since all the contained random variables are independent, also

\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(\tau))}\left[\#(f^{*}D,f\tau)\right]
and
\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(\tau^{\prime}))}\left[\#(f^{*}D,f\tau^{\prime})\right]
are independent random variables.

Next, let
j\in\mathcal{J}
. To prove that
\mathbb{P}_{\#}(X_{j}=\lambda)=0
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\tau\in\mathcal{H}^{D}
such that
j=\mathrm{Aut}(D)\tau
arbitrary and let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
M\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\left|\bigcup_{f\in{\mathcal{K}}(\tau)}\{\#(f^{*}D,f\tau)\}\right|
and define
Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{M}
as random variables such that
\{Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{M}\}=\bigcup_{f\in{\mathcal{K}}(\tau)}\{\#(f^{*}D,f\tau)\}
. Define
w\in[0,1]^{M}
via
w_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(\tau))}[\delta_{Y_{m},\#(f^{*}D,f\tau)}]
for
m=1,\dotsc,M
. By assumption (
253
), we have
\mathbb{P}_{\#}(Y_{m}=\lambda^{\prime})=0
for any
\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and
Y_{m},Y_{m^{\prime}}
are by definition independent variables for
m\neq m^{\prime}\in\{1,\dotsc,M\}
. We can hence apply Lemma
89
to conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\#}(X_{j}=\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\#}\left(\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(\tau))}[\#(f^{*}D,f\tau)]=\lambda\right)

(273)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\#}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{M}Y_{m}\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(\tau))}[\delta_{Y_{m},\#(f^{*}D,f\tau)}]=\lambda\right)

(274)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\#}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{M}Y_{m}w_{m}=\lambda\right)

(275)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\#}\left(\langle Y,w\rangle=\lambda\right)

(276)

\displaystyle=0.

(277)

Since we have shown above that
v\neq v^{\prime}
, that
X_{j},X_{j^{\prime}}
are independent for
j\neq j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}
, and that
{\mathbb{P}_{\#}(X_{j}=\lambda)=0}
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
j\in\mathcal{J}
, we can apply Lemma
89
again to get

\mathbb{P}_{\#}(\langle X,v-v^{\prime}\rangle=0)=0.

(278)

Finally, for
j\in\mathcal{J}
let
\tau^{(j)}\in\mathcal{H}^{D}
arbitrary such that
j=\mathrm{Aut}(D)\tau^{(j)}
.
Then

\langle X,v\rangle=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}|\mathrm{Aut}(D)\tau^{(j)}|\mathbb{P}_{\Psi^{D}(\pi)}(H^{D}=\tau^{(j)})X_{j}\\
=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}|\mathrm{Aut}(D)\tau^{(j)}|\mathbb{P}_{\Psi^{D}(\pi)}(H^{D}=\tau^{(j)})\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(\tau^{(j)}))}\left[\#(f^{*}D,f\tau^{(j)})\right]\\
=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{H}^{D}}\mathbb{P}_{\Psi^{D}(\pi)}(H^{D}=\tau)\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{K}}(\tau))}\left[\#(f^{*}D,f\tau)\right]=\chi^{\#}(D,\pi)

(279)

and analogously

\langle X,v^{\prime}\rangle=\chi^{\#}(D,\pi^{\prime}).

(280)

Hence, it is

\{\chi^{\#}(D,\pi)=\chi^{\#}(D,\pi^{\prime})\}=\{\langle X,v\rangle=\langle X,v^{\prime}\rangle\}.

(281)

It follows that

\mathbb{P}_{\#}(\chi^{\#}(D,\pi)=\chi^{\#}(D,\pi^{\prime}))\overset{(\ref{eq:504})}{=}\mathbb{P}_{\#}(\langle X,v\rangle=\langle X,v^{\prime}\rangle)=\mathbb{P}_{\#}(\langle X,v-v^{\prime}\rangle=0)\overset{(\ref{eq:505})}{=}0.

This concludes the proof.
∎
